# Bubbles in constant-proportion wealth and the unidentified volatility tail

Draft of 2026-10-06

## Abstract

Let a discounted price follow a driftless local-volatility diffusion \(dS = \sigma(S)\,dW\) under the pricing measure, absorbed at zero, and let \(V^\pi\) be the wealth of an investor who keeps a constant fraction \(\pi > 0\) of wealth in the asset. A natural conjecture is that \(V^\pi\) has a bubble (is a strict local martingale) exactly when \(S\) has one and \(\pi > 1/2\). The conjecture is false. With \(V^\pi\) taken to be the stochastic exponential of \(\pi\int dS/S\), stopped when \(S\) reaches zero, we show that on every finite horizon \(V^\pi\) is a strict local martingale if and only if either \(\pi > 1/2\) and \(\int^\infty x/\sigma^2(x)\,dx < \infty\), or \(0 < \pi < 1/2\) and \(\int_0 x/\sigma^2(x)\,dx < \infty\). Equivalently, \(V^\pi\) has a bubble exactly when \(\pi > 1/2\) and \(S\) has a bubble, or \(0 < \pi < 1/2\) and \(S\) can reach zero. The exposure \(\pi = 1/2\) never produces a bubble, and the conjectured threshold holds exactly for prices that cannot reach zero. Brownian motion absorbed at zero is a counterexample at \(\pi = 1/4\). A second result concerns identification. If the volatility function is known exactly on a bounded range \((0, R]\), then \(\int_c^\infty x/\sigma^2(x)\,dx\) can take every value above its known part \(\int_c^R x/\sigma^2(x)\,dx\), and the value infinity, so whether the price has a bubble is not identified. This is an exact version of the extrapolation problem of Jarrow, Kchia and Protter; it is a statement about the coefficient, and it is proved in Lean; it persists when \(\sigma\) is known only on \([r, R]\), where neither endpoint integral is identified. For price data we show, by stopping the price above a high level, that any test of a finite number of price paths on a finite horizon has, at every alternative, power at most its size taken uniformly over a broad null class, and it persists when every null is a driftless local-volatility diffusion, positive and continuous if the alternative is, under any equivalent physical law, including the physical law equal to the pricing law; a test with power must exclude the linear-tail completions of the volatility function used to build those nulls. We characterize the identified set of the bubble \(s_0 - E_Q[S_T]\) as a law-dependent interval and, in Sin's stochastic-volatility model, exhibit one physical price law compatible with both a bubble and no bubble. For funds rebalanced at discrete dates, as exchange-traded leveraged funds are, the zero-endpoint effect disappears and, for exposures in \((0, 1]\), a fund has a bubble exactly when its underlying does, and an inverse fund's gap is signed and needs a liquidation rule to describe a nonnegative price. Toward a usable test from prices alone, the visited part of the JKP integral is identified exactly through local time, and we give identification under a declared power tail, pooling across names that share an exponent, calibrated bands and e-values in a finite catalogue of exact price likelihoods, and a list of bubbly names with false-discovery control; none of these yet has useful power on realistic samples. In simulation, the score with a continuous Gaussian null, combined with that list, is the only procedure compared that keeps false discoveries controlled on realistic daily panels, but it fails under repeated volatility regimes, and a declared intraday clock gives high power on a year of exact five-minute prices. On real prices it flags no calm control and almost none of the episodes commonly called bubbles, while four existing tests flag calm controls at the rates we measure. Results that use option prices, namely tests from fund options, the decomposition of a basket's bubble across names, and upper bounds from American calls, rest on conventions about how option quotes relate to the bubble that are not derived from how options are priced, and we state them only as conditional theory. The first result rests on the martingale criterion of Mijatović and Urusov, which we apply rather than re-prove; it is conditional on that criterion and on the stated identification of wealth. Its deterministic part, the classification of the criterion's endpoint conditions, is proved in Lean without conditions, and the conditional classification of wealth is proved there from the cited criteria stated as hypotheses. The results on discretely rebalanced funds and on price data rest on bounded stopping and the supermartingale property of nonnegative local martingales, and on Sin's model, and are proved in Lean from them stated as hypotheses (Appendix A).

## 1. Introduction

In the strict-local-martingale theory of asset price bubbles `jarrow2007complete`, a discounted price has a bubble when it is a local martingale but not a martingale under the pricing measure \(Q\). For a driftless one-dimensional diffusion \(dS = \sigma(S)\,dW\) on \((0,\infty)\) the question has a complete answer: \(S\) is a strict local martingale if and only if the JKP integral \(\int^\infty x/\sigma^2(x)\,dx\) is finite (`mijatovic2010martingale`, Corollary 4.3).

This paper asks whether a rebalancing strategy inherits the bubble. An investor holds the fraction \(\pi > 0\) of wealth in \(S\) and the rest in the numéraire. The wealth process \(V^\pi\) is then a stochastic exponential of \(\pi\int dS/S\), and the question is when it is a martingale. A tempting answer, suggested by the case \(\pi = 1\), where \(V^1\) is proportional to \(S\), is that \(V^\pi\) is strict exactly when \(S\) is strict and \(\pi > 1/2\). We show that the upper threshold \(1/2\) is right, but that the statement misses the lower endpoint: when \(S\) can reach zero, every exposure strictly between \(0\) and \(1/2\) gives strict wealth, whether or not \(S\) has a bubble (Theorem 1, Corollary 2).

The result is an application of a known general theorem, not a new theorem about diffusions. Mijatović and Urusov `mijatovic2010martingale` characterize when a stochastic exponential of a diffusion is a martingale: two endpoint conditions, each decided by Feller's test for explosions applied to an auxiliary diffusion. We apply their Theorem 2.1 with integrand \(b_\pi(x) = \pi\sigma(x)/x\), check its hypotheses, and compute the two endpoint tests. The Feller test function for this auxiliary drift family is already written down by Karatzas and Ruf (`karatzas2015distribution`, equation (7.4)); what we add is the classification it implies for every \(\pi > 0\), the role of the zero endpoint, and the reading in terms of default.

Theorem 1, like the JKP criterion, turns on integrals of \(x/\sigma^2\) near the endpoints of the state space, and these are what price data do not reveal. Jarrow, Kchia and Protter (`jarrow2011detect`, §1 and §5) observe that \(\sigma\) can be estimated only at levels the price has visited, so the tail of the volatility function, and with it the finiteness of the JKP integral, must be extrapolated; they call this the extrapolation problem. Proposition 7 is its exact, set-valued version. Knowing \(\sigma\) exactly on \((0, R]\) leaves every value of the JKP integral above its known part attainable, and infinity too. The observation there is the coefficient on a bounded range, not price data, and Proposition 7 is not a theorem about tests.

Section 3.4 turns to price data. Theorem 18 says that, without structural assumptions, no test based on finitely many price paths on a finite horizon has power above its size at any alternative. The mechanism is elementary: stopping a strict price when it first exceeds a high level gives a true martingale whose paths agree with the original ones except on an event of small probability. The theorem is an application of bounded stopping `hulley2019weak`, not a new general impossibility theorem. Proposition 19 characterizes what the law of prices identifies about the size of the bubble: an interval that depends on that law, with no universal answer. Proposition 20 applies Sin's stochastic-volatility model `sin1995complications` to exhibit a single physical price law with one pricing measure under which the price is a martingale and another under which it is strict. The null class of Theorem 18 contains prices that are frozen above a level. Theorem 22 removes them: the bound holds with nulls that are driftless local-volatility diffusions, under any equivalent physical law including the pricing law itself, so local volatility, positivity and continuity alone do not make a bubble testable.

Section 2 states the setting and the cited inputs. Section 3 gives the theorem, its corollary, a counterexample to the conjecture, the identification result for the coefficient, the discretely rebalanced and inverse funds of exchange-traded practice, what price data cannot identify, and the ingredients of a usable test from prices alone (Section 3.5). Section 4 collects results that rest on conventions about option prices, as conditional theory. Section 5 reports simulations with known answers, including a comparison with four existing tests, and Section 6 applies the tests to real prices. Section 7 discusses related work and Section 8 lists what is not done.

## 2. Setting and standing assumptions

**Standing assumptions.** Throughout:

1. \(\sigma : (0,\infty) \to \mathbb{R}\) is Borel, \(\sigma(x) \neq 0\) for every \(x > 0\), and \(1/\sigma^2\) is integrable on every compact subinterval of \((0,\infty)\).
2. On a filtered probability space under \(Q\) satisfying the usual conditions, \(W\) is a Brownian motion and \(S\) is a nonnegative weak solution of \(dS = \sigma(S)\,dW\) started at a deterministic \(s_0 > 0\), stopped and absorbed at its first hitting time of zero. The equation is interpreted before that time, so \(\sigma\) need not be defined at zero.
3. \(\pi > 0\) and \(v_0 > 0\) are deterministic constants. Set \(b_\pi(x) = \pi\sigma(x)/x\), and let \(Z^\pi\) be the stochastic exponential of \(\int b_\pi(S)\,dW\) in the sense of `mijatovic2010martingale` (its equation 7, p. 4), including its convention at the exit time \(\zeta\) of \(S\) from \((0,\infty)\): \(Z^\pi\) keeps its value at \(\zeta\) if \(\int_0^\zeta b_\pi^2(S_t)\,dt < \infty\) and is set to zero otherwise.
4. **Wealth representation (W).** The wealth process under study is \(V^\pi = v_0 Z^\pi\) at all times.

Assumption (W) is the identification of the constant-proportion strategy with this stopped exponential. Before \(\zeta\) it is the usual self-financing equation \(dV^\pi = \pi V^\pi\,dS/S\). We do not prove the identification as a statement about admissible trading strategies, and we do not define holdings after \(S\) has reached zero; every statement about wealth below is conditional on (W). The exit convention is not a free modelling choice in practice: on \(\{\zeta < \infty\}\) the integral \(\int_0^\zeta b_\pi^2(S_t)\,dt = \pi^2\int_0^\zeta \sigma^2(S_t)/S_t^2\,dt\) is almost surely infinite, since otherwise \(\log S = \int \sigma(S)/S\,dW - \tfrac12\int \sigma^2(S)/S^2\,dt\) would have a finite limit at \(\zeta\), contradicting \(S_\zeta = 0\). So \(V^\pi\) is absorbed at zero when \(S\) is.

For a fixed \(c > 0\) define the extended integrals

$$I_0 = \int_0^c \frac{x}{\sigma^2(x)}\,dx, \qquad I_\infty = \int_c^\infty \frac{x}{\sigma^2(x)}\,dx. \tag{1}$$

Their finiteness does not depend on \(c\), because \(x/\sigma^2\) is integrable on compact subintervals of \((0,\infty)\) by assumption 1. \(I_\infty\) is the JKP integral.

**Cited inputs.** We use three published results, stated here in our notation; we apply them and do not re-prove them. For a diffusion with drift \(\mu\) and coefficient \(\sigma\) on \((0,\infty)\) and the reference point \(c\), write \(\rho(x) = \exp(-\int_c^x 2\mu/\sigma^2)\) for its scale density and \(s(x) = \int_c^x \rho\) for its scale function.

- *Martingale criterion* (`mijatovic2010martingale`, Theorem 2.1 and the remark following it). Under the Engelbert–Schmidt conditions on \(\mu\) and \(\sigma\) and local integrability of \(b^2/\sigma^2\), the exponential \(Z\) of `mijatovic2010martingale` (its equation 7, p. 4), is a martingale on \([0,T]\), for any fixed \(T \in (0,\infty)\), if and only if, at each endpoint, either the auxiliary diffusion with drift \(\mu + b\sigma\) and coefficient \(\sigma\) does not exit there, or the endpoint is good. Infinity is good if \(s(\infty) < \infty\) and \((s(\infty) - s)\,b^2/(\rho\sigma^2)\) is integrable near infinity; zero is good if \(s(0) > -\infty\) and \((s - s(0))\,b^2/(\rho\sigma^2)\) is integrable near zero. Goodness is computed with the original scale.
- *Feller's test for explosions*, in the endpoint form of `mijatovic2010martingale`, equations (20)–(23), and the global form of `karatzas2015distribution`, Appendix A. A diffusion as above exits at infinity with positive probability if and only if \(s(\infty) < \infty\) and \((s(\infty) - s)/(\rho\sigma^2)\) is integrable near infinity, and exits at zero if and only if \(s(0) > -\infty\) and \((s - s(0))/(\rho\sigma^2)\) is integrable near zero.
- *Existence and uniqueness in law* (`mijatovic2010martingale`, Section 2, p. 4; `karatzas2015distribution`, Proposition 9.1). Under standing assumption 1 the driftless equation has a weak solution absorbed at zero, and two such equations whose coefficients agree on an interval \((0, n)\) containing \(s_0\) have the same law when stopped at the first exit from \((0, n)\).
- *Share probability* (`kardaras2015strict`, Definition 2.5 and Theorem 2.12). For a strictly positive continuous local martingale \(X\) on a right-continuous, uncompleted filtration of a standard system, there is a probability \(\tilde Q\) with \(\tilde Q(A \cap \{t < \tau\}) = E_Q[(X_t/X_0)1_A]\) for \(A \in \mathcal{F}_t\), where \(\tau\) is the explosion time of \(X\) under \(\tilde Q\).
- *Bounded stopping* (`hulley2019weak`, Section 2 and Lemma 2.1). If \(S\) is a continuous local martingale on a right-continuous filtration with \(S_0 = s_0\), and \(\tau_n = \inf\{t \ge 0 : |S_t| > n\}\) for \(n > s_0\), then the stopped process \(S^n = S_{\cdot \wedge \tau_n}\) is a uniformly integrable martingale bounded by \(n\).
- *Supermartingale property* (`hulley2019weak`, Section 2, after the proof of Lemma 2.1; `mijatovic2010martingale`, p. 5). A nonnegative local martingale with integrable initial value is a supermartingale; in particular \(E_Q[S_t]\) is nonincreasing in \(t\).
- *Sin's model* (`sin1995complications`, Theorems 3.2 and 3.6). Under a probability \(Q^s\) let \(dS_t = S_t v_t^\alpha \gamma \cdot dW_t\) and \(dv_t = v_t\, a \cdot dW_t + \kappa(L - v_t)\,dt\), with \(S_0 = s_0 > 0\), \(v_0 = 1\), constants \(\alpha, \kappa, L > 0\), nonzero \(\gamma, a \in \mathbb{R}^2\) and a two-dimensional Brownian motion \(W\). Then \(S\) is a martingale on all finite horizons if and only if \(a \cdot \gamma \le 0\). If \(a \cdot \gamma > 0\) and the vectors are not parallel, then for each \(T > 0\) there is \(Q^m_T\), equivalent to \(Q^s\) on \(\mathcal{F}_T\), under which \(S\) is a martingale on \([0, T]\); under it the drift of \(v\) becomes \(\kappa L - (a \cdot \gamma)v^{1+\alpha}\). We use the 1995 report; the journal version `sin1998complications` has not been read.
- *Finite trading* (`etheridge2018continuous`, Theorem 7.11 and Proposition 7.14). If \(X\) is a continuous local martingale, \(0 \le t_0 < \dots < t_N\) a deterministic grid and \(h_i\) finite \(\mathcal{F}_{t_i}\)-measurable random variables, then \(\sum_i h_i (X_{\cdot \wedge t_{i+1}} - X_{\cdot \wedge t_i})\) is a continuous local martingale.
- *JKP criterion* (`mijatovic2010martingale`, Corollary 4.3 and the remark following it). Under assumptions 1–2, \(S\) is a strict local martingale on every finite horizon if and only if \(I_\infty < \infty\).

## 3. Main results

### 3.1 Constant-proportion wealth

**Theorem 1 (constant-proportion wealth).** Assume the standing assumptions and (W). For every finite \(T > 0\), \(V^\pi\) is a nonnegative continuous local martingale, and

$$V^\pi \text{ is a strict local martingale on } [0,T] \iff \Big(0 < \pi < \tfrac12 \text{ and } I_0 < \infty\Big) \text{ or } \Big(\pi > \tfrac12 \text{ and } I_\infty < \infty\Big). \tag{2}$$

Equivalently, \(V^\pi\) is a martingale on every finite horizon exactly in the following cases.

| Exposure | \(V^\pi\) is a martingale exactly when |
|---|---|
| \(0 < \pi < 1/2\) | \(I_0 = \infty\) |
| \(\pi = 1/2\) | always |
| \(\pi > 1/2\) | \(I_\infty = \infty\) |

*Status.* Conditional on the martingale criterion and on (W); the proof below also uses Feller's test, which the formal proof avoids. Machine-checked: the equivalence between the criterion's endpoint conditions and the negation of the right-hand side of (2) is proved in Lean without conditions, and (2) and the table are proved there from the martingale criterion stated as a hypothesis (Appendix A).

*Proof.* The integrand \(b_\pi\) is Borel and \(b_\pi^2/\sigma^2 = \pi^2/x^2\) is integrable on compact subintervals of \((0,\infty)\); the original drift is \(\mu = 0\). So the hypotheses of the martingale criterion hold, and it gives the local-martingale property of \(Z^\pi\), hence of \(V^\pi\). The auxiliary diffusion has drift \(\mu_\pi(x) = b_\pi(x)\sigma(x) = \pi\sigma^2(x)/x\), coefficient \(\sigma\) and start \(s_0\); its ratio \(\mu_\pi/\sigma^2 = \pi/x\) is locally integrable, so Feller's test applies to it.

*Both endpoints are bad.* With \(\mu = 0\) the original scale is \(\rho = 1\), \(s(x) = x - c\). At infinity \(s(\infty) = \infty\). At zero \(s(0) = -c\) is finite, but \((s(x) - s(0))\,b_\pi^2(x)/(\rho(x)\sigma^2(x)) = \pi^2/x\) is not integrable near zero. Neither fact depends on \(\sigma\).

*Auxiliary scale.* The auxiliary scale density is \(\tilde\rho_\pi(x) = (x/c)^{-2\pi}\). With \(q = 1 - 2\pi\), the scale function normalized by \(\tilde s_\pi(c) = 0\) is

$$\tilde s_\pi(x) = \frac{c^{2\pi}(x^q - c^q)}{q} \quad (\pi \neq \tfrac12), \qquad \tilde s_\pi(x) = c\log(x/c) \quad (\pi = \tfrac12). \tag{3}$$

*Right endpoint.* If \(\pi \le 1/2\), then \(\tilde s_\pi(\infty) = \infty\) and the auxiliary diffusion does not exit at infinity. If \(\pi > 1/2\), then \(\tilde s_\pi(\infty) = c/(2\pi - 1)\) and

$$\frac{\tilde s_\pi(\infty) - \tilde s_\pi(x)}{\tilde\rho_\pi(x)\sigma^2(x)} = \frac{1}{2\pi - 1}\,\frac{x}{\sigma^2(x)}, \tag{4}$$

so by Feller's test the auxiliary diffusion exits at infinity if and only if \(\pi > 1/2\) and \(I_\infty < \infty\).

*Left endpoint.* If \(\pi \ge 1/2\), then \(\tilde s_\pi(0) = -\infty\) and there is no exit at zero. If \(0 < \pi < 1/2\), then \(\tilde s_\pi(0) = -c/(1 - 2\pi)\) and

$$\frac{\tilde s_\pi(x) - \tilde s_\pi(0)}{\tilde\rho_\pi(x)\sigma^2(x)} = \frac{1}{1 - 2\pi}\,\frac{x}{\sigma^2(x)}, \tag{5}$$

so the auxiliary diffusion exits at zero if and only if \(0 < \pi < 1/2\) and \(I_0 < \infty\).

Since both endpoints are bad, the martingale criterion says that \(Z^\pi\) is a martingale on \([0,T]\) exactly when the auxiliary diffusion exits at neither endpoint. Substituting the two exit conditions gives (2) and the table, and multiplication by \(v_0 > 0\) changes nothing. \(\square\)

The finite-cutoff Feller test function of the auxiliary diffusion is already in `karatzas2015distribution`, equation (7.4). In that source the diffusion coefficient is \(s\) and the drift is \(s\,b\); with \(s = \sigma\) and \(b(x) = a\,s(x)/x\), \(a = \pi\), its drift is our \(\mu_\pi\). For \(\pi \neq 1/2\) the kernel \((\tilde s_\pi(x) - \tilde s_\pi(z))/(\tilde\rho_\pi(z)\sigma^2(z))\) obtained from (3) is exactly the integrand of (7.4), and (4) and (5) are its limits, up to orientation, as \(x \to \infty\) and \(x \to 0\). At \(\pi = 1\) the computation reduces to the proof of the JKP criterion in `mijatovic2010martingale`, Corollary 4.3, as it must, since \(V^1 = v_0 S/s_0\).

**Corollary 2 (bubbles and default).** Under the assumptions of Theorem 1, \(I_0 < \infty\) if and only if \(S\) reaches zero with positive probability. Hence, on every finite horizon,

$$V^\pi \text{ is strict} \iff \Big(\pi > \tfrac12 \text{ and } S \text{ is strict}\Big) \text{ or } \Big(0 < \pi < \tfrac12 \text{ and } S \text{ can reach zero}\Big). \tag{6}$$

In particular \(V^{1/2}\) is never strict, and the statement "\(V^\pi\) is strict if and only if \(S\) is strict and \(\pi > 1/2\)" holds for every \(\pi > 0\) exactly when \(S\) cannot reach zero. If \(S\) can reach zero, it fails for every \(0 < \pi < 1/2\), whether or not \(S\) is strict.

*Status.* Conditional on Theorem 1, Feller's test and the JKP criterion. The equivalence of \(I_0 < \infty\) with reaching zero is not formalized; the scope statement in its last sentence, read with \(I_0\) in place of reaching zero, is proved in Lean.

*Proof.* Apply Feller's test to \(S\) itself: \(\mu = 0\), \(s(x) = x - c\), \(s(0) = -c\) is finite, and \((s(x) - s(0))/(\rho(x)\sigma^2(x)) = x/\sigma^2(x)\). So \(S\) exits at zero with positive probability if and only if \(I_0 < \infty\). (The same equivalence is `karatzas2015distribution`, Proposition 7.1(ii).) Combine with (2) and the JKP criterion. \(\square\)

Read through Corollary 2, a constant-proportion investor's wealth has a bubble from one of two sources. Above one-half, wealth inherits the bubble of the asset. Below one-half, it inherits the asset's default: a positive probability that \(S\) is absorbed at zero, which a fixed-proportion investor rides down to zero wealth, is enough to make \(V^\pi\) lose expected value, even when \(S\) itself is a true martingale. This is an effect of rebalancing continuously into default: the continuously rebalanced investor keeps selling cash for the asset as its price falls to zero. Propositions 11 and 12 show that it does not carry over to funds rebalanced at discrete dates, where the threshold \(1/2\) does not either. In particular, an exposure at or below one-half need not give bubble-free wealth, and the threshold is not universal across volatility functions: if \(\sigma(x) = 1 + x^2\), both \(I_0\) and \(I_\infty\) are finite, and \(V^\pi\) is strict for every \(\pi \neq 1/2\).

**Example 3 (counterexamples to the conjecture).**

(a) *Brownian motion absorbed at zero.* Take \(\sigma \equiv 1\), \(s_0 = v_0 = c = 1\) and \(\pi = 1/4\). Then \(I_0 = \int_0^1 x\,dx = 1/2\) and \(I_\infty = \infty\). By the JKP criterion \(S\) is a martingale; by Theorem 1, \(V^{1/4}\) is a strict local martingale on every finite horizon. Both the integrals and this conclusion, conditional on the two cited criteria, are proved in Lean.

(b) *The driftless square-root diffusion.* Take \(\sigma(x) = \sqrt{x}\), the constant-elasticity case \(dS = S^{1/2}\,dW\). Then \(x/\sigma^2(x) = 1\), so \(I_0 < \infty\) and \(I_\infty = \infty\), and by Corollary 2 \(S\) is a martingale that reaches zero with positive probability, and \(V^\pi\) is strict for every \(0 < \pi < 1/2\).

As numerical evidence about the cases computed, not as proof: for \(dS = S^{1/2}\,dW\) with \(S_0 = 1\), two independent methods (a finite-difference solution of the Feynman–Kac equation for \(E_Q[V^\pi_T]\) on truncated domains, and a closed form in incomplete gamma functions from the hitting law of a Bessel-type transform of the auxiliary diffusion) give the martingale defect \(1 - E_Q[V^\pi_T]/V^\pi_0\) as \(0.0455\) at \(\pi = 0.25\) and \(0.0130\) at \(\pi = 0.4\) for \(T = 1\), and \(0.371\) and \(0.148\) for \(T = 5\), with agreement to better than \(10^{-6}\). On a grid of elasticities \(\{0.5, 1, 1.5, 2\}\) and exposures from \(0.25\) to \(2\), the defect is positive exactly in the cells predicted by (2). At \(\pi = 1/2\) the finite-difference method gives only an upper bound of order \(10^{-4}\) and the closed form gives zero.

Over an infinite horizon the classification collapses. Allow now \(\pi \ge 0\), including \(\pi = 0\), and take \(V^\pi = v_0Z^\pi\) at all times, with the exit convention of Theorem 1.

**Proposition 4 (uniform integrability only at zero exposure).** Under the standing assumptions and (W), \(V^\pi\) is a martingale and \(\{V_t^\pi : t \ge 0\}\) is uniformly integrable if and only if \(\pi = 0\). With Theorem 1, the classification reads:

| Exposure | Martingale on every finite horizon exactly when | Uniformly integrable martingale on \([0, \infty)\) |
|---|---|---|
| \(\pi = 0\) | always | yes |
| \(0 < \pi < 1/2\) | \(I_0 = \infty\) | no |
| \(\pi = 1/2\) | always | no |
| \(\pi > 1/2\) | \(I_\infty = \infty\) | no |

*Status.* Conditional on the uniform-integrability criterion of Mijatović and Urusov (`mijatovic2010martingale`, Theorem 2.3), stated as a hypothesis with a vacuous instance, and on the inputs of Theorem 1 for the finite-horizon column; the endpoint computation is proved unconditionally. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* The criterion gives a uniformly integrable martingale exactly when the integrand vanishes almost everywhere, or a suitable endpoint is good. For \(\pi > 0\) the integrand \(\pi\sigma(x)/x\) vanishes nowhere, and both endpoints are bad, as in the proof of Theorem 1: infinity because the original scale is unbounded, and zero because the goodness integrand \(\pi^2/x\) is not integrable there, whether or not \(S\) reaches zero. For \(\pi = 0\) the integrand vanishes. The constant \(v_0 > 0\) preserves both properties. \(\square\)

So every positive exposure fails over an infinite horizon, whatever the two endpoint integrals. The statement concerns the conjunction of the martingale property and uniform integrability over all \(t \ge 0\); it does not say that a strict local martingale's values cannot be uniformly integrable, since some are, and it does not bear on uniform integrability over a fixed finite interval. On geometric Brownian motion, a martingale for every \(\pi\), \(E[V_t^\pi 1\{V_t^\pi > 10\}]\) with \(v_0 = 1\) tends to one as \(t\) grows for every \(\pi > 0\), for example \(0.006\), \(0.629\) and \(1.000\) at \(t = 10\), \(100\) and \(1{,}000\) for \(\pi = 1/4\), so its values are not uniformly integrable although every finite-horizon mean is one. On Brownian motion absorbed at zero, \(E[V_t^\pi] < 1\) for \(0 < \pi < 1/2\), for example \(0.8465\), \(0.5166\) and \(0.2931\) at \(t = 1\), \(10\) and \(100\) for \(\pi = 1/4\), and \(E[V_t^\pi] = 1\) for \(\pi \ge 1/2\), as Theorem 1 says.

Letting the exposure decay over time restores uniform integrability in the geometric model. Let the price be a weak solution of \(dS = \sigma(S)\,dW\) on the canonical path space with cemetery time \(\zeta\), with \(\sigma\) as in the standing assumptions, let \(\pi_t\) be a predictable exposure with \(|\pi_t| \le M\) on every path, and let wealth be \(V = v_0Z\), with \(Z\) the stochastic exponential of \(\int\pi_t\,dS_t/S_t\), extended at the end of its lifetime as in Ruf's criterion (`ruf2015martingale`, Proposition 3.1 and Theorem 3.3). That criterion says that \(V\) is a martingale on every finite horizon, or a uniformly integrable martingale on \([0, \infty)\), exactly when the energy \(\int\pi_s^2\sigma(S_s)^2/S_s^2\,ds\) is finite almost surely under an auxiliary law \(Q_{\mathrm{aux}}\), up to every finite time or over the whole lifetime respectively.

**Proposition 5 (time-varying exposure).** (a) If \(\pi_s^2\sigma(x_s)^2/x_s^2 \le h(s)\) on every path and for almost every \(s\) before the end of its lifetime, for a deterministic locally integrable \(h \ge 0\), then \(V\) is a martingale on every finite horizon, and a uniformly integrable martingale on \([0, \infty)\) if \(\int_0^\infty h < \infty\). (b) For geometric Brownian motion, \(\sigma(x) = \kappa x\), the lifetime is infinite under the pricing law and under every auxiliary law, \(V\) is a martingale on every finite horizon, and it is a uniformly integrable martingale exactly when \(Q_{\mathrm{aux}}(\int_0^\infty\pi_s^2\,ds < \infty) = 1\). For a deterministic bounded schedule \(\pi(t)\) this is \(\int_0^\infty\pi(t)^2\,dt < \infty\): \(e^{-t}\) gives a uniformly integrable martingale, \(\pi \equiv 1\) a martingale that is not uniformly integrable, and \((1 + t)^{-\gamma}\) a uniformly integrable one exactly when \(\gamma > 1/2\). (c) Bounded exposure alone is not enough: with \(\sigma(x) = x^2\) and \(\pi \equiv 1\), \(V\) is strict on every finite horizon.

*Status.* Conditional on Ruf's criterion, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance, and, for (c), on Theorem 1 and its inputs. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) The pathwise bound gives a deterministic bound on the energy, finite at every finite time, and finite over the whole lifetime when \(h\) is integrable, under any auxiliary law. (b) Each path's range before \(\zeta\) is compact, so the lifetime of the criterion is \(\zeta\). Since the energy is at most \(\kappa^2M^2t\), the localizing times exceed every finite \(T\) once their index is large, so the event \(\{\zeta \le T\}\) is in the stopped \(\sigma\)-field, and the criterion's density identity carries its \(Q\)-nullity to every auxiliary law. Then the energy is \(\kappa^2\int_0^\infty\pi_s^2\,ds\). (c) \(\int_c^\infty x/\sigma(x)^2\,dx = 1/(2c^2) < \infty\) and \(\pi = 1 > 1/2\), so Theorem 1 applies. \(\square\)

On geometric Brownian motion with \(\kappa = 1\) and \(v_0 = 1\), \(E[V_T1\{V_T > 10\}]\) at \(T = 10^{12}\) is \(0.0018\) for \(e^{-t}\) and \(0.1785\) for \((1 + t)^{-3/4}\), but \(0.9857\) and still rising for \((1 + t)^{-1/2}\), whose energy grows logarithmically, and \(1.0000\) for \((1 + t)^{-0.4}\) and for \(\pi \equiv 1\). The auxiliary law matters: the bounded adaptive exposure \(\pi_t = 1\{S_t > s_0\}\) on geometric Brownian motion has finite energy almost surely under the pricing law, because the price eventually stays below \(s_0\), but under the auxiliary law the path escapes upwards with probability one half, and wealth is not uniformly integrable, with \(E[V_\infty] = 1/2\); in a simulation the mean of \(V_T\) truncated at 100 fell from \(0.888\) at \(T = 5\) to \(0.503 \pm 0.002\) at \(T = 100\) and \(200\). Finite energy under the pricing law does not suffice, so the classification by \(\int\pi(t)^2\,dt\) holds for deterministic schedules only.

Two independent assets each keep their own endpoint test. Let \(S_1\) and \(S_2\) satisfy the standing assumptions with their own \(\sigma_i\), driven by Brownian motions in a common filtration, and let \(Z_i\) be the exponential of Theorem 1 for exposure \(\pi_i \ge 0\), with its own exit convention, and \(Z_i \equiv 1\) when \(\pi_i = 0\). Assume the full systems \((S_1, Z_1)\) and \((S_2, Z_2)\) are independent under \(Q\), and take wealth \(V = v_0Z_1Z_2\) at all times, supplied as a continuous local martingale. Write \(m_i(T) = E_Q[Z_{i,T}]\) and \(\delta_i(T) = 1 - m_i(T)\).

**Proposition 6 (two independent assets).** (a) For every \(T \ge 0\), \(m_i(T) \in [0, 1]\) and \(1 - E_Q[V_T]/v_0 = \delta_1(T) + \delta_2(T) - \delta_1(T)\delta_2(T)\). For \(T > 0\), \(V\) is a martingale on \([0, T]\) if and only if each coordinate passes the test of Theorem 1 with its own endpoint integrals; otherwise \(V\) is strict on every finite horizon. (b) \(V\) is a martingale and \(\{V_t : t \ge 0\}\) is uniformly integrable if and only if \(\pi_1 = \pi_2 = 0\). (c) With \(\sigma_1 \equiv 1\), \(\sigma_2(x) = x\) and \(\pi_1 = \pi_2 = 1/4\), both prices are martingales, but \(V\) is strict on every finite horizon, with \(m_2 \equiv 1\) and defect \(\delta_1(T) > 0\).

*Status.* Conditional on the supermartingale property of nonnegative local martingales, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance, and on the inputs of Theorem 1 and Proposition 4 for each factor, supplied as premises. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) Independence factorizes the mean. Each factor and the product are supermartingales from a constant, so each is a martingale on \([0, T]\) exactly when it keeps its mean at \(T\), and \(m_1m_2 = 1\) with \(m_i \le 1\) forces both means to one. (b) For independent nonnegative \(X\), \(Y\) with \(E[Y] = 1\), \(E[X1\{X \ge 2C\}] \le 2E[XY1\{XY \ge C\}]\), so uniform integrability of the product, with both means one at every time, passes to each factor, and Proposition 4 forces both exposures to zero. (c) For \(\sigma_1 \equiv 1\), \(I_0 < \infty\), so \(\pi_1 = 1/4\) fails its test; for \(\sigma_2(x) = x\) both endpoint integrals diverge, so the second factor is a martingale. \(\square\)

An independent true-martingale factor does not cure the zero-endpoint obstruction. With \(\sigma_1 \equiv 1\), \(\sigma_2(x) = x\) and \(\pi_1 = \pi_2 = 1/4\), both prices are true martingales, but the product's defect equals that of the absorbed Brownian factor: \(0.0005\), \(0.1535\), \(0.4834\) and \(0.7069\) at \(T = 0.1\), \(1\), \(10\) and \(100\), in agreement with a Monte Carlo check.

Theorem 1 therefore extends in three directions, and stops at a fourth. Over an infinite horizon, constant exposure gives a uniformly integrable martingale only at zero exposure (Proposition 4). On geometric Brownian motion, bounded time-varying exposure gives a martingale on every finite horizon, and a deterministic schedule gives uniform integrability exactly when its square is integrable, under the auxiliary law rather than the pricing law (Proposition 5); bounded exposure alone is not enough in general. For independent assets, wealth is a martingale exactly when each factor passes its own test, and uniformly integrable exactly when both exposures are zero (Proposition 6); an absorbed Brownian factor at exposure \(1/4\) keeps its defect even when paired with a true-martingale geometric Brownian motion. Correlated assets and liquidation through default are not covered.

### 3.2 What the volatility on a bounded range identifies

Fix \(0 < c < R < \infty\) and \(\sigma\) satisfying standing assumption 1. Let \(\mathcal{C}_R(\sigma)\) be the class of all coefficients \(g\) satisfying standing assumption 1 with \(g(x) = \sigma(x)\) for every \(0 < x \le R\). For \(g \in \mathcal{C}_R(\sigma)\) write \(F_c(g) = \int_c^\infty x/g^2(x)\,dx \in [0, \infty]\), let \(\theta(g) = 1\) if \(F_c(g) < \infty\) and \(\theta(g) = 0\) otherwise, and let \(J_{cR} = \int_c^R x/\sigma^2(x)\,dx\). The identified set of a quantity is the set of its values over all coefficients compatible with the observation, here \(\sigma\) on \((0, R]\).

**Proposition 7 (the volatility tail is not identified).** Under these assumptions,

$$\{F_c(g) : g \in \mathcal{C}_R(\sigma)\} = (J_{cR}, \infty], \qquad \{\theta(g) : g \in \mathcal{C}_R(\sigma)\} = \{0, 1\}. \tag{7}$$

The lower endpoint \(J_{cR}\) is not attained. If \(\sigma\) is positive and continuous, the completions exhibited in the proof are positive and continuous as well.

*Status.* The identities (7) are unconditional given standing assumption 1 and are machine-checked, together with the completions (9) and the tail value (10). Their reading as a statement about bubbles is conditional on the JKP criterion: for each \(g\), and for an absorbed driftless diffusion with coefficient \(g\) as in standing assumption 2, \(\theta(g) = 1\) exactly when that diffusion is a strict local martingale. We do not construct those diffusions (Appendix A).

*Proof.* \(J_{cR}\) is finite, because \(x \le R\) on \([c, R]\) and \(1/\sigma^2\) is integrable there. For \(g \in \mathcal{C}_R(\sigma)\),

$$F_c(g) = J_{cR} + \int_R^\infty \frac{x}{g^2(x)}\,dx. \tag{8}$$

The last integral is strictly positive, possibly infinite, because its integrand is measurable and strictly positive on \((R, R + 1)\). Hence \(F_c(g) \in (J_{cR}, \infty]\). Conversely, put \(k = \sigma(R) \neq 0\) and, for \(p \ge 1\),

$$g_p(x) = \sigma(x) \quad (0 < x \le R), \qquad g_p(x) = k\,(x/R)^p \quad (x > R). \tag{9}$$

Each \(g_p\) is Borel and nowhere zero, and \(1/g_p^2 \le 1/k^2\) above \(R\), so \(g_p \in \mathcal{C}_R(\sigma)\); it is positive and continuous when \(\sigma\) is. Above \(R\) the integrand is \(x/g_p^2(x) = (R^{2p}/k^2)\,x^{1 - 2p}\). For \(p = 1\) its integral over \([R, H]\) is \((R^2/k^2)\log(H/R)\), which tends to infinity, so \(F_c(g_1) = \infty\). For \(p > 1\),

$$\int_R^\infty \frac{x}{g_p^2(x)}\,dx = \frac{R^2}{2(p - 1)k^2}, \tag{10}$$

and the choice \(p = 1 + R^2/(2k^2 a)\) gives the value \(a\) for any \(0 < a < \infty\). By (8) every value in \((J_{cR}, \infty]\) is attained, and \(\theta\) takes both values. \(\square\)

Proposition 7 is the exact, set-valued form of the extrapolation problem of `jarrow2011detect` (§1 and §5). There it is stated informally: the volatility function can be estimated only at levels the price has visited, so its tails cannot be known, nor whether the JKP integral is finite. The two remedies proposed there, a parametric fit checked against a nonparametric estimate and an extension in a reproducing kernel Hilbert space, each choose one completion \(g\). Proposition 7 says that without such a structural choice the observation leaves every value in \((J_{cR}, \infty]\) open.

What Proposition 7 does not say matters as much. Its observation is the coefficient \(\sigma\) known exactly on \((0, R]\). That is not a finite sample of prices, nor even a continuously observed price path. It does not compare the laws of price observations under two completions, and it does not control the probability that the price reaches \(R\). So it is not a theorem that tests of "no bubble" against "bubble" have no power, and \(\{0, 1\}\) in (7) is not a confidence statement; Theorem 18 below is a statement of that kind for price data, over a broader null class. Two features of the observation should be noted. The result is monotone in information: an observation that reveals no more than \(\sigma\) on \((0, R]\), for example \(\sigma\) on a subrange, has a compatible class containing \(\mathcal{C}_R(\sigma)\), so it does not identify \(\theta\) either. On the other hand, the observation is generous at the lower end, because \(\sigma\) on \((0, c]\) determines \(I_0\). Under this observation, then, the condition of Theorem 1 for \(0 < \pi < 1/2\) is identified and the condition for \(\pi > 1/2\) is not. Proposition 17 shows that with \(\sigma\) known only on \([r, R]\), \(r > 0\), neither endpoint integral is identified.

### 3.3 Discretely rebalanced and inverse funds

Exchange-traded leveraged and inverse funds rebalance at discrete dates, pay fees and financing costs, and track their target imperfectly. Between two rebalancing dates such a fund holds a fixed number of shares and a cash position. Fix a horizon \(T > 0\), a pricing probability \(Q\) with a filtration satisfying the usual conditions, and a continuous nonnegative \(Q\)-local martingale \(X\) with \(X_0 = 1\): the underlying price discounted by a positive reference cash account and normalized by its initial value. For a real exposure \(\pi\), the fixed-share-plus-cash contract over one holding interval, before costs, is, in the same normalized units,

$$G^\pi_t = (1 - \pi) + \pi X_t, \qquad 0 \le t \le T. \tag{11}$$

The net asset value is modelled as

$$Y_t = G^\pi_t - c_t + e_t, \tag{12}$$

where the costs \(c\) and tracking deviations \(e\) are specified adapted processes, integrable at each time, with \(c_0 = e_0 = 0\) and \(E_Q|e_T| \le \varepsilon\). Write \(\delta_T = 1 - E_Q[X_T]\) and \(d_T = E_Q[c_T]\). A concrete daily convention takes \(T = \Delta\) one day, the reference gross return \(B = 1 + r\Delta\), an expense rate \(f\), a stock-borrowing spread \(\lambda\) and a spread \(\ell\) on borrowed cash, and sets

$$C = \big[f + (-\pi)^+\lambda + (\pi - 1)^+\ell\big]\Delta, \qquad c_T = C/B. \tag{13}$$

With \(\ell = 0\) this is the daily return relation of `avellaneda2009path` (its equation 1 for \(\pi > 1\) and equation 2 for \(\pi \le -1\)); for other exposures, and for \(\ell > 0\), it is our stated convention.

**Proposition 8 (one holding interval: a signed gap).** \(G^\pi\) is a local martingale. For \(\pi \neq 0\) it is a martingale on \([0, T]\) exactly when \(X\) is, and

$$1 - E_Q[G^\pi_T] = \pi\,\delta_T, \quad 0 \le \delta_T \le 1, \qquad \big|(1 - E_Q[Y_T]) - \pi\,\delta_T - d_T\big| \le \varepsilon, \tag{14}$$

with \(\delta_T > 0\) exactly when \(X\) is strict on \([0, T]\). If the underlying has a bubble, the gap is positive for \(\pi > 0\) and negative for \(\pi < 0\).

*Status.* Conditional on bounded stopping and the supermartingale property `hulley2019weak`. Machine-checked in full, from those two inputs stated as hypotheses, including the cost convention (13), its two specializations to `avellaneda2009path`, and the deterministic-expense example below (Appendix A).

*Proof.* The stopped processes \(X_{\cdot \wedge \tau_n}\), \(\tau_n = \inf\{t : X_t > n\}\), are bounded martingales, so their affine images localize \(G^\pi\). For \(\pi \neq 0\), \(X = (G^\pi - 1 + \pi)/\pi\), so either is a martingale when the other is. The supermartingale property gives \(0 \le E_Q[X_T] \le 1\); if \(E_Q[X_T] = 1\), the nonnegative gaps \(X_u - E_Q[X_t \mid \mathcal{F}_u]\) have mean zero and vanish, so \(X\) is a martingale. Taking expectations in (11) and (12) gives (14). \(\square\)

So the defect of a leveraged or inverse fund over one interval is \(\pi\delta_T\) plus its costs, up to tracking error. Two cautions follow. A fall in the mean value of a fund is not by itself a bubble: with \(X \equiv 1\), \(e = 0\) and \(c_t = t/(2T)\), the value \(Y_t = 1 - t/(2T)\) is positive and deterministic and not a local martingale, and its shortfall is an expense. And for \(\pi < 0\) the gap \(\pi\delta_T\) is negative: \(G^\pi\) is a signed strict local martingale, not a positive price with a bubble.

**Proposition 9 (inverse funds need a liquidation rule).** The value \(1 - \pi + \pi x\) is nonnegative for every \(x \ge 0\) exactly when \(0 \le \pi \le 1\). If \(X\) is strict on \([0, T]\) and \(\pi < 0\), then

$$Q\big(G^\pi_t < 0 \text{ for some } t \le T\big) > 0. \tag{15}$$

For \(\pi < 0\) and \(1 < K < 1 - 1/\pi\), let \(\tau_K = \inf\{t \ge 0 : X_t > K\}\) and liquidate into cash at \(\tau_K\): \(H_t = 1 - \pi + \pi X_{t \wedge \tau_K}\). Then \(H\) is a uniformly integrable martingale with

$$0 < 1 + \pi(K - 1) \le H_t \le 1 - \pi. \tag{16}$$

This holds even when \(X\) is strict on every finite horizon, for example \(dX = X^2\,dW\), \(X_0 = 1\), with \(\pi = -1\) and \(K = 3/2\), where \(1/2 \le H \le 2\).

*Status.* Conditional on bounded stopping and the supermartingale property; the example also on the JKP criterion. Machine-checked, with the example as a conditional corollary (Appendix A).

*Proof.* The positivity range is read off the two coefficients. If (15) failed, \(G^\pi\) would be a nonnegative local martingale started at one, so \(E_Q[G^\pi_T] \le 1\), contradicting \(E_Q[G^\pi_T] = 1 - \pi\delta_T > 1\). The stopped \(X_{\cdot \wedge \tau_K}\) is a martingale with values in \([0, K]\), and \(H\) is its affine image with \(\pi < 0\), which gives (16). In the example \(\int_1^\infty x/x^4\,dx = 1/2 < \infty\), so \(X\) is strict by the JKP criterion. \(\square\)

A nonnegative inverse fund is therefore a different contract from (11): it needs a stated rule for what happens when the underlying rises far enough, and under the rule above it has no bubble even though its underlying does. Whether an inverse fund "inherits" its underlying's bubble depends on that rule, not on \(\pi\) alone.

The same rule works on a finite grid.

**Proposition 10 (finite-grid inverse funds with a liquidation floor).** Let \(\pi < 0\), let \(W\) be the signed fixed-share recursion that rebalances to exposure \(\pi\) at the dates \(0 = t_0 < \dots < t_N = T\) of a finite grid (the recursion (17) below, now with \(\pi < 0\)), and for a floor \(0 \le a < 1\) let \(\tau_a\) be the first time \(W\) enters \((-\infty, a)\), capped at \(T\), monitored continuously between rebalancing dates. The liquidated wealth \(H^a = W_{\cdot \wedge \tau_a}\) is a continuous local martingale with \(H^a_0 = 1\), equal to \(a\) from \(\tau_a\) on when \(\tau_a < T\), and

$$a \le H^a_t \le (1 - \pi)^N \quad \text{for every } t.$$

Hence \(H^a\) is a uniformly integrable martingale with \(E_Q[H^a_T] = 1\), strictly positive if \(a > 0\), even when the underlying is strict. With one interval, \(\tau_a\) is the first time \(X\) exceeds \(1 + (1 - a)/(-\pi)\), the rule of Proposition 9, and if \(X\) is strict on \([0, T]\) then \(Q(\tau_a < T) > 0\).

*Status.* An application of bounded stopping `hulley2019weak`, with the finite-trading fact, the stopping-time property of exit times and the stopping property of stochastic integrals; not a new closure theorem. Machine-checked from these inputs stated as hypotheses (Appendix A).

*Proof.* \(W\) is a continuous local martingale by the finite-trading fact, and \(\tau_a\), an entry time of an open set, is a stopping time, so \(H^a\) is a continuous local martingale. Before \(\tau_a\) the wealth is at least \(a\), and at a finite entry it equals \(a\). Each interval factor \(1 - \pi + \pi X_t/X_{t_i}\) is at most \(1 - \pi\), so induction over the intervals gives \(H^a \le (1 - \pi)^N\). A bounded local martingale is a uniformly integrable martingale. With one interval the floor is reached exactly when \(X\) exceeds the stated level; if \(\tau_a\) never occurred before \(T\), \(H^a\) would agree with the signed contract, which would make \(X\) a martingale by Proposition 8. \(\square\)

Three limits should be stated plainly. The floor holds only under continuous monitoring with continuous prices and exact execution: a fund that checks its floor at discrete times, or whose price gaps, can overshoot the floor and go negative. The upper bound is for each fixed grid and grows with \(N\), so nothing uniform in the number of rebalances is claimed. And the rule is a model of liquidation, not a description of any issuer's actual practice.

For \(0 \le \pi \le 1\) and a finite grid \(0 = t_0 < \dots < t_N = T\), rebalancing at each \(t_i\) to exposure \(\pi\) gives, with \(W_0 = 1\) and for \(t_i \le t \le t_{i+1}\),

$$W_t = W_{t_i}\big[(1 - \pi) + \pi X_t/X_{t_i}\big] \text{ if } X_{t_i} > 0, \qquad W_t = W_{t_i} \text{ if } X_{t_i} = 0. \tag{17}$$

The second case keeps the fund in cash after the underlying has been absorbed at zero.

**Proposition 11 (finite grids remove the zero-endpoint effect).** If \(X\) is a martingale on \([0, T]\), then for every finite grid \(W\) is a nonnegative martingale on \([0, T]\). In the setting of Example 3(a), with \(\pi = 1/4\), every finite-grid \(W\) is a martingale while the continuous wealth \(V^{1/4}\) of Theorem 1 is strict, and

$$E_Q|W_T - V^{1/4}_T| \ge 1 - E_Q[V^{1/4}_T] > 0 \quad \text{for every grid}. \tag{18}$$

*Status.* Conditional on bounded stopping and the supermartingale property, and for the example on Theorem 1 and the JKP criterion. Machine-checked, with the example as a conditional corollary (Appendix A).

*Proof.* On \(\{X_{t_i} > 0\}\) the holding \(\pi W_{t_i}/X_{t_i}\) is nonnegative and \(\mathcal{F}_{t_i}\)-measurable; pulling it out of the conditional expectation (through bounded truncations and monotone convergence) gives \(E_Q[W_t \mid \mathcal{F}_{t_i}] = W_{t_i}\), and on \(\{X_{t_i} = 0\}\) the wealth is constant. The tower property carries this across rebalancing dates. In Example 3(a) the underlying is a martingale by the JKP criterion and \(V^{1/4}\) is strict by Theorem 1, so \(E_Q[W_T] = 1 > E_Q[V^{1/4}_T]\), and (18) is the triangle inequality. \(\square\)

At a default the discretely rebalanced fund keeps its cash fraction, while the continuously rebalanced wealth is absorbed at zero with the price (Section 2). The lower-endpoint case of Theorem 1 is thus a property of rebalancing continuously into default, absent on every finite grid. By (18), finite-grid wealth cannot converge to \(V^{1/4}\) in \(L^1\) at \(T\); we do not assert or exclude convergence in a weaker sense.

Over a strict underlying, the bubble survives every finite grid.

**Proposition 12 (finite-grid funds inherit the underlying's bubble).** Let \(X\) be any continuous nonnegative \(Q\)-local martingale with \(X_0 = 1\), let \(0 < \pi \le 1\), and let \(W\) be defined by (17) on a finite grid. Then \(W\) is a nonnegative continuous local martingale, and it is a martingale on \([0, T]\) exactly when \(X\) is, and strict there exactly when \(X\) is. In the driftless setting of Section 2, every such \(W\) is strict exactly when \(I_\infty < \infty\), with no condition at the lower endpoint. For \(\sigma(x) = x^2\) and \(\pi = 1/4\), every finite-grid \(W\) is strict, whereas \(V^{1/4}\) of Theorem 1 is a martingale.

*Status.* Conditional on the supermartingale property `hulley2019weak` and on the fact that a finite sum of predictable multiples of increments of a continuous local martingale is again one (`etheridge2018continuous`, Theorem 7.11 and Proposition 7.14); the diffusion statements also on the JKP criterion and, for the comparison with \(V^{1/4}\), on Theorem 1. Machine-checked from these inputs stated as hypotheses, with the diffusion statements as conditional corollaries (Appendix A).

*Proof.* On each interval \(W_t = W_{t_i} + h_i(X_t - X_{t_i})\) with the \(\mathcal{F}_{t_i}\)-measurable holding \(h_i = \pi W_{t_i}/X_{t_i}\), zero when \(X_{t_i} = 0\), so \(W\) is a continuous local martingale by the cited fact, and nonnegative because \(0 < \pi \le 1\). With the conditional losses \(A_i = X_{t_i} - E_Q[X_{t_{i+1}} \mid \mathcal{F}_{t_i}] \ge 0\), pulling out \(h_i\) gives the exact decomposition \(1 - E_Q[W_T] = \sum_{i=0}^{N-1} E_Q[h_i A_i]\). If \(X\) is a martingale, every \(A_i\) vanishes and \(W\) is a martingale. Conversely, \(W_{t_{i+1}} \ge \pi (W_{t_i}/X_{t_i}) X_{t_{i+1}}\) gives by induction \(W_{t_i} \ge \pi^i X_{t_i}\), hence \(h_i \ge \pi^{i+1}\), and the decomposition is at least \(\pi^N (1 - E_Q[X_T])\), which is positive when \(X\) is strict. The diffusion statements follow from the JKP criterion; for \(\sigma(x) = x^2\), \(\int_1^\infty x/x^4\,dx = 1/2\) while \(\int_0^1 x/x^4\,dx = \infty\), so by Theorem 1 \(V^{1/4}\) is a martingale. \(\square\)

The result is the classification, not the bound \(\pi^N(1 - E_Q[X_T])\) used in its proof, which is far too small to be informative at realistic \(N\) (daily rebalancing over a year has \(N\) about 250). Together with Proposition 11, it shows that the continuous-rebalancing criterion of Theorem 1 differs from every finite grid in both directions: \(\sigma \equiv 1\), \(\pi = 1/4\) gives martingale funds and strict continuous wealth, and \(\sigma(x) = x^2\), \(\pi = 1/4\) the reverse. Costs and tracking enter as in Proposition 8: if the terminal value is \(Y_T = W_T - c_T + e_T\), with \(c_T, e_T\) the terminal difference from \(W\) and \(E_Q|e_T| \le \varepsilon\), then \(|(1 - E_Q[Y_T]) - (1 - E_Q[W_T]) - E_Q[c_T]| \le \varepsilon\).

With Proposition 11, Proposition 12 means that the threshold \(1/2\) and the zero-endpoint case of Theorem 1 can hold at most as limits as the rebalancing becomes continuous.

Such a limit holds away from zero.

**Proposition 13 (grid refinement before zero).** Let \(X\) be a continuous nonnegative \(Q\)-local martingale with \(X_0 = 1\), absorbed at zero, let \(\pi\) be any real number, and let \(W^{(n)}\) be the fixed-share recursion (17) on finite grids of \([0, T]\) whose mesh tends to zero, not necessarily nested. For \(m > 1\) let \(\rho_m\) be the first time \(X\) leaves \((1/m, m)\), capped at \(T\), let \(L^m = \int dX^{\rho_m}/X^{\rho_m}\) and \(V^{\pi, m} = \exp(\pi L^m - \pi^2\langle L^m\rangle/2)\). Then \(W^{(n)}_{\cdot \wedge \rho_m} \to V^{\pi, m}\) uniformly on \([0, T]\) in probability. If \(Q(X_t > 0 \text{ for all } t \le T) = 1\), then \(W^{(n)} \to V^\pi = \exp(\pi L - \pi^2\langle L\rangle/2)\), \(L = \int dX/X\), uniformly on \([0, T]\) in probability, and \(Q(\inf_{t \le T} W^{(n)}_t \le 0) \to 0\). In either case, expectations of bounded functionals of the path that are Lipschitz in the uniform norm converge, and if the terminal values are uniformly integrable, \(E_Q|W^{(n)}_T - V_T| \to 0\) and the bubble sizes \(1 - E_Q[W^{(n)}_T]\) converge to \(1 - E_Q[V_T]\). In the driftless setting of Theorem 1 with \(\sigma\) locally bounded and \(\pi > 0\), the exponent of \(V^\pi\) is that of the stochastic exponential of \(\int b_\pi(X)\,dW\), the wealth of Theorem 1.

*Status.* An application of Émery's stability theorem for stochastic differential equations (`emery1979lipschitz`, Theorem 2), with the stochastic integral and exponential as in `etheridge2018continuous` and the stopping-time property of exit times; it is not a new approximation theorem. Machine-checked from these inputs and the supermartingale property stated as hypotheses (Appendix A).

*Proof.* Stopped at \(\rho_m\), the recursion is the solution of \(U = 1 + \int F_n(U)_{s-}\,dX^{\rho_m}_s\) with \(F_n(U)_t = \pi U_{\kappa_n(t)}/X^{\rho_m}_{\kappa_n(t)}\), \(\kappa_n(t)\) the last grid point at or before \(t\); these operators are non-anticipating and Lipschitz with the common constant \(1 + |\pi| m\), because \(X^{\rho_m} \ge 1/m\), and converge at the limiting solution to \(F(U)_t = \pi U_t/X^{\rho_m}_t\), whose equation is solved by \(V^{\pi, m}\). Émery's theorem gives the first convergence. Under strict positivity \(Q(\rho_m < T) \to 0\) and \(V^{\pi, m} = V^\pi_{\cdot \wedge \rho_m}\), which removes the stop; the breach limit uses the positive minimum of \(V^\pi\). The expectation statements are the bounded-Lipschitz comparison and Vitali's theorem. \(\square\)

Three limits of its scope should be stated plainly. First, this convergence does not preserve strictness: for \(0 < \pi \le 1\) every finite-grid fund over a strict price is strict (Proposition 12), while for a strictly positive price the limit \(V^\pi\) is strict only when \(\pi > 1/2\). Second, bubble sizes converge only if the terminal values are uniformly integrable, which Proposition 13 assumes rather than proves (Proposition 14 proves it when the limit is a martingale); expectations of bounded Lipschitz functionals of the path, such as a put payoff for \(0 \le \pi \le 1\), converge in any case. Third, prices that can reach zero are not covered, consistent with (18).

When the continuous limit is a martingale, uniform integrability is not an extra hypothesis.

**Proposition 14 (bubble sizes converge when the limit is a martingale).** In the setting of Proposition 13 with \(Q(X_t > 0 \text{ for all } t \le T) = 1\) and \(0 \le \pi \le 1\), suppose \(E_Q[V^\pi_T] = 1\). Then \(E_Q|W^{(n)}_T - V^\pi_T| \to 0\), \(E_Q[W^{(n)}_T] \to 1\), the family \(\{W^{(n)}_T\}\) is uniformly integrable, and \(E_Q[g(W^{(n)}_T)] \to E_Q[g(V^\pi_T)]\) for every Lipschitz \(g\), calls and puts included. At \(\pi = 0\) and \(\pi = 1\) the grid wealth equals the limit (one, and the price itself), so these conclusions hold even over a strict price, with common bubble size \(1 - E_Q[X_T]\) at \(\pi = 1\). Without the hypothesis \(E_Q[V^\pi_T] = 1\), only \(\limsup_n (1 - E_Q[W^{(n)}_T]) \le 1 - E_Q[V^\pi_T]\) holds. In the driftless setting of Theorem 1 with zero inaccessible (\(I_0 = \infty\)), the hypothesis holds for \(0 < \pi \le 1/2\) for every \(\sigma\), and for \(0 < \pi \le 1\) when \(I_\infty = \infty\).

*Status.* An application of Fatou's lemma, dominated convergence and the converse of Vitali's theorem (`etheridge2018continuous`, Appendix B, for the probabilistic forms), conditional on the inputs of Propositions 12 and 13; the diffusion regimes are conditional in addition on the martingale criterion and (W). Machine-checked; the formal proof takes the convergence theorems from the Lean library rather than as cited inputs (Appendix A).

*Proof.* The grid wealths are nonnegative with mean at most one (Proposition 12 and the supermartingale property) and converge in probability to \(V^\pi_T\) (Proposition 13). If \(E_Q[V^\pi_T] = 1\), Fatou's lemma forces their means to one, dominated convergence applied to the shortfall \((V^\pi_T - W^{(n)}_T)^+ \le V^\pi_T\) gives \(L^1\) convergence, and the converse of Vitali's theorem gives uniform integrability; the Lipschitz bound \(|E g(W) - E g(V)| \le C\,E|W - V|\) gives the payoff prices. At \(\pi = 1\) the grid factors telescope to \(X\). The diffusion regimes are those where Theorem 1 makes \(V^\pi\) a martingale. \(\square\)

So the loss of strictness in the limit is compatible with uniform integrability: for \(\sigma(x) = x^2\) and \(\pi = 1/4\), every grid fund is strict (Proposition 12), yet its bubble size tends to zero. When the limit is strict, here \(1/2 < \pi < 1\) with \(I_\infty < \infty\), only the one-sided bound holds, and whether the bubble sizes converge to that of the limit is open. As evidence only, a registered numerical experiment for \(\sigma(x) = x^2\), \(\pi = 3/4\), \(T = 1\) (Appendix A) gives \(E_Q[W_1] \approx 0.786\), \(0.819\) and \(0.833\) for \(N = 4\), \(64\) and \(1008\) grid intervals, approaching \(E_Q[V^{3/4}_1] \approx 0.8465\) from below, with small tail expectations; it cannot exclude mass escaping in rare paths.

Leveraged funds can be brought in with the liquidation rule of Proposition 10. For \(\pi > 1\), let \(W\) be the fixed-share recursion on a finite grid with \(N\) intervals, \(H^a\) its liquidation the first time it enters \((-\infty, a)\), \(0 \le a < 1\), monitored continuously, \(M = \pi^N\), and \(d_H = 1 - E_Q[H^a_T]\).

**Proposition 15 (liquidated leveraged funds).** \(H^a\) is a continuous local martingale with \(H^a_0 = 1\), \(a \le H^a_t \le a + M X_t\) at every time, \(0 \le d_H \le 1 - a\), and \(d_H \le M\delta_T\); so \(d_H > 0\) implies \(\delta_T > 0\). If \(X\) is a martingale on \([0, T]\), so is \(H^a\). With one interval, \(\tau_a\) is the first time \(X\) falls below \(1 - (1 - a)/\pi\) and \(d_H = \pi(1 - E_Q[X_{\tau_a}])\).

*Status.* Conditional on the inputs of Propositions 10 and 12. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* On each interval before liquidation the wealth is at most \(\pi\) times its value at the last rebalancing date times the price ratio, so by induction \(W \le M X\) up to \(\tau_a\), and \(H^a = a\) afterwards. Then \(R = MX - H^a + a\) is a nonnegative continuous local martingale started at \(M - 1 + a\), and the supermartingale property for \(X\), \(H^a\) and \(R\) gives the bounds on \(d_H\). \(\square\)

A positive bubble in a liquidated leveraged fund thus implies one in the underlying; no converse is asserted. The bound \(M = \pi^N\) is enormous at realistic \(N\), so \(d_H/M\) is a negligible lower bound on \(\delta_T\). The floor holds only under continuous monitoring with continuous prices and exact execution; with discrete monitoring the fund can overshoot it. And the rule is a model of liquidation, not a description of any issuer's practice.

Continuous monitoring cannot be simulated exactly, but it can be bounded from one side. For a liquidated fund of Proposition 10 or Proposition 15, and a finite set \(F \subseteq [0, T]\) of monitoring times containing every rebalancing date, let \(Z_F = a\) if \(W_r < a\) at some \(r \in F\), and \(Z_F = W_T\) otherwise: follow the signed recursion until the first sampled breach and replace the sampled value by \(a\). This is a numerical device, not the value of a fund, since the replacement is an artificial cash adjustment.

**Proposition 16 (clipped monitoring bounds the liquidated defect).** (a) On every continuous nonnegative price path, \(a \le H^a_T \le Z_F\), with \(Z_F - H^a_T = (W_T - a)1_{\{\tau_a < T,\ W_r \ge a \text{ for all } r \in F\}}\); a sampled breach gives \(Z_F = H^a_T = a\), and \(F \subseteq F'\) gives \(Z_{F'} \le Z_F\). (b) \(d_F = 1 - E_Q[Z_F]\) satisfies \(d_F \le d_H \le 1 - a\); for \(\pi < 0\), \(E_Q[Z_F] \ge 1\); for \(\pi > 1\), \(d_F > 0\) implies \(\delta_T > 0\), and \(E_Q[Z_F] \ge 1\) when \(X\) is a martingale. (c) If the monitoring sets \(F_m\) become dense in \([0, T]\), for a fixed rebalancing grid, then \(Z_{F_m} = H^a_T\) eventually on every path, \(E_Q|Z_{F_m} - H^a_T| \to 0\) and \(d_{F_m} \to d_H\). (d) No bound that tends to zero with the mesh holds uniformly over continuous paths: for every finite \(F\), with \(\pi = 2\), \(a = 1/2\), one interval and \(T = 1\), a path equal to one on \(F\) that dips to \(1/2\) in a gap of \(F\) has \(Z_F = 1\) and \(H^a_T = 1/2\).

*Status.* Parts (a) and (d) are deterministic and unconditional; parts (b) and (c) are conditional on the inputs of Propositions 10 and 15. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* Before the open entry the wealth is at least \(a\), so a sampled \(W_r < a\) lies strictly after it and forces \(H^a_T = a\); if no breach is sampled, \(T \in F\) gives \(W_T \ge a\), which is the identity in (a). Taking expectations gives (b), with Propositions 10 and 15. Under dense monitoring an open excursion below the floor is eventually sampled, so \(Z_{F_m} = H^a_T\) eventually, and dominated convergence gives (c). The tent path gives (d). \(\square\)

A positive \(d_F\) therefore shows a positive bubble in the continuously liquidated fund, and for \(\pi > 1\), by Proposition 15, in the underlying; \(d_F\) itself can be negative. The convergence in (c) has no rate, and (d) shows that none is possible from the samples alone. Freezing the sampled wealth at a breach, without clipping it to \(a\), gives no such one-sided bound, so simulated defects computed that way are not bounds on \(d_H\). A positive simulated estimate of \(d_F\) is, moreover, evidence, not a proof of a positive expectation.

### 3.4 What price data cannot identify

We begin with coefficients and then turn to price data. Proposition 7 assumed \(\sigma\) known down to zero; a realistic observation covers neither endpoint.

**Proposition 17 (neither endpoint integral is identified).** Let \(0 < r < c < R < \infty\), and let \(\sigma\) be Borel and nowhere zero on \([r, R]\) with \(1/\sigma^2\) integrable there. Among Borel nowhere-zero extensions \(g\) of \(\sigma\) to \((0, \infty)\) with \(1/g^2\) locally integrable, the lower tail \(\int_0^r x/g^2(x)\,dx\) takes every value in \((0, \infty]\), and, independently, the upper tail \(\int_R^\infty x/g^2(x)\,dx\) takes every value in \((0, \infty]\). Hence \(I_0\) ranges over \(\int_r^c x/\sigma^2 + (0, \infty]\), \(I_\infty\) over \(\int_c^R x/\sigma^2 + (0, \infty]\), and both conditions of Theorem 1 are unidentified.

*Status.* Unconditional, deterministic, and machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* Below \(r\) put \(g_p(x) = \sigma(r)(x/r)^p\) for real \(p\). Then

$$\int_0^r \frac{x}{g_p^2(x)}\,dx = \frac{r^{2p}}{\sigma^2(r)} \int_0^r x^{1 - 2p}\,dx = \begin{cases} \dfrac{r^2}{2(1 - p)\sigma^2(r)}, & p < 1, \\ \infty, & p \ge 1, \end{cases} \tag{19}$$

and \(p = 1 - r^2/(2a\sigma^2(r))\) gives any \(a \in (0, \infty)\). The value \(0\) is excluded because the integrand is positive. With the lower part fixed, the coefficient is known on \((0, R]\), and the completions (9) of Proposition 7 supply any upper tail in \((0, \infty]\) without changing it. \(\square\)

This is a statement about coefficients. It does not say that a realized price path reveals \(\sigma\) exactly on its visited range, and it compares no laws of observations; the rest of this section does that.

Fix \(s_0 > 0\), a finite horizon \(T > 0\) and a number \(k\) of observed paths. Prices are discounted and pay no dividends. Let \(E_T\) be the space of continuous nonnegative paths on \([0, T]\) starting at \(s_0\), with the uniform topology. An admissible model consists of a filtered probability space satisfying the usual conditions, a physical probability \(P\), a pricing probability \(Q\) equivalent to \(P\) on \(\mathcal{F}_T\), and an adapted nonnegative continuous \(Q\)-local martingale \(S\) with \(S_0 = s_0\). No coefficient form, tail bound or uniqueness of \(Q\) is imposed, and stopped or frozen prices are allowed. The data are \(k\) independent copies of \(S\) on \([0, T]\) under \(P\). A test is a Borel map \(\phi : E_T^k \to [0, 1]\), the probability of rejecting; a finite sampling grid, or any other measurable function of the paths, is included by composition. The null hypothesis \(H_0\) is the set of admissible models in which \(S\) is a \(Q\)-martingale on \([0, T]\), and the alternative \(H_1\) the set in which it is strict there. The size of \(\phi\) is \(\mathrm{size}(\phi) = \sup_{H_0} E_{P^{\otimes k}}[\phi]\).

**Theorem 18 (power is at most uniform size).** For every test \(\phi\) and every model in \(H_1\),

$$E_{P^{\otimes k}}[\phi(S^1, \ldots, S^k)] \le \mathrm{size}(\phi). \tag{20}$$

The bound is attained by \(\phi \equiv \varepsilon\). Consequently, for fixed \(T\), if the uniform size of a sequence of tests has limit superior at most \(\varepsilon\) as the number of paths grows, then so does its power at each fixed alternative. Nothing is claimed for tests whose size is controlled only pointwise over the null.

*Status.* Conditional on bounded stopping `hulley2019weak`. Machine-checked, from that input stated as a hypothesis, including sharpness and the asymptotic statement (Appendix A). The null class contains frozen prices (below), and Theorem 22 gives the bound with local-volatility nulls.

*Proof.* Fix a model in \(H_1\) and \(n > s_0\). Stop \(S\) at \(\tau_n = \inf\{t : S_t > n\}\). By bounded stopping \(S^n\) is a bounded \(Q\)-martingale, so the same \(P\), \(Q\) and the price \(S^n\) form a model in \(H_0\). Couple each observed path with its stopped version on the same sample point, and let \(A_n\) be the event that some original path reaches \(n\) on \([0, T]\). Off \(A_n\) the original and stopped paths coincide, so \(|\phi(S^1, \ldots, S^k) - \phi((S^1)^n, \ldots, (S^k)^n)| \le 1_{A_n}\), and

$$E_{P^{\otimes k}}[\phi(S^1, \ldots, S^k)] \le \mathrm{size}(\phi) + P^{\otimes k}(A_n). \tag{21}$$

Continuous paths are bounded on \([0, T]\), so \(P^{\otimes k}(A_n) \to 0\) for finite \(k\), which gives (20). The asymptotic statement follows by taking limits superior of (20). \(\square\)

The mechanism is the unobserved tail of Section 3.2 in statistical form: the null model differs from the alternative only above a level that the data reach with small probability. The abstract form, that a null approximation agreeing with an alternative with probability close to one caps the power of every bounded test, is standard, and `bahadur1956nonexistence` may anticipate it; we have not read that paper against this problem. Theorem 18 is the application of bounded stopping to this observation scheme. The stopped null model is frozen above \(n\). It is not a diffusion with nowhere-zero volatility, so Theorem 18 does not say that tests confined to the local-volatility family of Section 2, whose null models are true-martingale diffusions, have no power. Theorem 22 supplies that restricted statement.

For the size of the bubble, take the unconditional defect \(b_T = s_0 - E_Q[S_T]\), the bubble at time zero for maturity \(T\) when initial information is trivial. Fix a law \(P\) of the price path on \(E_T\), with the canonical price and its natural filtration and no other traded assets, and set

$$\mathcal{M}(P) = \{Q \sim P : S \text{ is a } Q\text{-local martingale on } [0, T]\}, \qquad B_T(P) = \{s_0 - E_Q[S_T] : Q \in \mathcal{M}(P)\}. \tag{22}$$

**Proposition 19 (the identified set of the bubble depends on the law of prices).** Let \(\mathcal{M}(P) \neq \emptyset\). Then \(B_T(P)\) is the exact identified set of \(b_T\) given the law \(P\): every admissible model with physical price law \(P\), including models with hidden factors, has its defect in \(B_T(P)\), and every point of \(B_T(P)\) is attained. It is an interval in \([0, s_0]\) with infimum \(s_0 - \sup_{\mathcal{M}(P)} E_Q[S_T]\) and supremum \(s_0 - \inf_{\mathcal{M}(P)} E_Q[S_T]\), containing each endpoint exactly when the corresponding extremum is attained, and it excludes \(s_0\) if \(S_T > 0\) \(P\)-almost surely. If only the law \(R\) of a Borel observation \(O\) is known, for example prices on a finite grid, the identified set is the union of \(B_T(P)\) over all \(P\) with \(O_\# P = R\) and \(\mathcal{M}(P) \neq \emptyset\). For all maturities jointly, the set of defect curves \((s_0 - E_Q[S_t])_{0 \le t \le T}\), \(Q \in \mathcal{M}(P)\), is convex, and each curve starts at zero and is nondecreasing; one common \(Q\) is required for all maturities, so this set is not the product of its marginals. There is no universal answer: a constant price path gives \(B_T(P) = \{0\}\), and a known pricing law gives a singleton.

*Status.* Conditional on bounded stopping, the supermartingale property `hulley2019weak` and the fact that exit times of open sets by continuous adapted processes are stopping times (`etheridge2018continuous`, its equation 14). Machine-checked, from those inputs stated as hypotheses, including the exactness of (22) for every path law (Appendix A).

*Proof.* A model with physical law \(P\) pushes its pricing measure forward to a probability equivalent to \(P\) on the path space. The bounded stopped prices \(S^n\) are martingales in the model's filtration, hence in the natural filtration of the price, and they localize the canonical price; so the pushforward lies in \(\mathcal{M}(P)\) with the same terminal mean. Conversely each \(Q \in \mathcal{M}(P)\) is a model. For \(Q_1, Q_2 \in \mathcal{M}(P)\) and \(\lambda \in [0, 1]\), the same pathwise stopping times localize \(S\) under both, and the martingale identities for \(S^n\) are linear in the measure, so \(\lambda Q_1 + (1 - \lambda)Q_2 \in \mathcal{M}(P)\). Terminal means are affine in \(\lambda\), so \(B_T(P)\) is an interval, and the same argument makes the set of curves convex. The supermartingale property gives \(0 \le E_Q[S_t] \le s_0\), nonincreasing in \(t\), and \(E_Q[S_T] > 0\) when \(S_T > 0\) almost surely. Equivalence preserves the constant-path event, which forces \(\{0\}\); a known pricing law fixes the mean. \(\square\)

A known pricing measure identifies the bubble, and Theorem 18 is not contradicted: it concerns physical observations with \(Q\) unspecified. Under stochastic volatility even the full physical law can leave the bubble undetermined.

**Proposition 20 (one physical law, a bubble and no bubble).** There are a finite horizon \(T_0\) and a single physical law \(P\) of a continuous, strictly positive price on \([0, T_0]\) such that \(\mathcal{M}(P)\) contains a measure under which \(S\) is a martingale on \([0, T_0]\) and a measure under which it is strict there. Every test has the same rejection probability under both models, for every \(k\). The identified set of the binary label is \(\{0, 1\}\), and \([0, b] \subseteq B_{T_0}(P)\) for some \(b > 0\); \(s_0 \notin B_{T_0}(P)\).

*Status.* An application of Sin's model `sin1995complications`, conditional on its Theorems 3.2 and 3.6 as stated in Section 2, and on bounded stopping and the supermartingale property. Machine-checked from a configuration with one physical law and a strict and an equivalent martingale pricing measure; that configuration is derived from a hypothesis structure for Sin's model whose instance is vacuous, so this result is exactly as conditional as the prose (Appendix A). We do not determine \(\sup B_{T_0}(P)\).

*Proof.* Take \(\alpha = \kappa = L = 1\), \(\gamma = (1, 0)\) and \(a = (1, 1)\). Then \(a \cdot \gamma = 1 > 0\) and \(1 < \sqrt2 = |a||\gamma|\), so the vectors are not parallel. Sin's theorems give a finite \(T_0\) on which \(S\) is strict under \(Q^s\), and \(Q^m_{T_0} \sim Q^s\) under which it is a martingale on \([0, T_0]\). Let the physical measure be \(P = Q^s\) in both models; the null prices with \(Q^m_{T_0}\), the alternative with \(Q^s\). The price process and its physical law are the same, so rejection probabilities agree exactly; the volatility drifts under the two pricing measures are \(1 - v^2\) and \(1 - v\). Let \(b = s_0 - E_{Q^s}[S_{T_0}]\). If \(b = 0\), the supermartingale property would give \(E_{Q^s}[S_t] = s_0\) for all \(t \le T_0\), and a supermartingale with constant mean is a martingale, a contradiction; so \(b > 0\). The two measures give \(0\) and \(b\) in \(B_{T_0}(P)\), and Proposition 19 gives \([0, b]\). Strict positivity of \(S_{T_0}\) excludes \(s_0\). \(\square\)

That bubbles depend on the choice among pricing measures in incomplete markets is not new in substance: Jarrow, Protter and Shimbo note that bubbles can be born in incomplete markets (`jarrow2007complete`, Section 1) and treat them in a companion paper `jarrow2007asset`, which we have not read. Proposition 20 is an exact instance in which the physical law of prices is held fixed. It also bears on the open stochastic-volatility question for Theorem 1, as far as identification goes: under stochastic volatility, the physical price law need not determine whether the asset itself has a bubble.

**Corollary 21 (confidence sets).** If \(C\) is a measurable random set with \(P^{\otimes k}(b_T \in C) \ge 1 - \varepsilon\) for every admissible model, then the test \(1_{\{0 \notin C\}}\) has power at most \(\varepsilon\) at every alternative.

*Status.* As Theorem 18; machine-checked. This is a coverage implication, not a confidence procedure.

*Proof.* Under the null \(b_T = 0\), so the test has size at most \(\varepsilon\); apply (20). \(\square\)

The frozen nulls of Theorem 18 can be replaced by genuine diffusions. Fix the setting of Section 2 and let the pricing law of a coefficient \(\sigma\) be the law \(\nu_\sigma\) on \(E_T\) of the absorbed solution of \(dS = \sigma(S)\,dW\), \(S_0 = s_0\). A local-volatility model is a pair \((\sigma, P)\) with \(\sigma\) satisfying standing assumption 1 and a physical law \(P\) on \(E_T\) equivalent to \(\nu_\sigma\); nothing is assumed about the physical drift or the likelihood ratio \(dP/d\nu_\sigma\), and no tail bound or parametric form is imposed on \(\sigma\). The null hypothesis is \(I_\infty(\sigma) = \infty\), the alternative \(I_\infty(\sigma) < \infty\), and size is the supremum over the null local-volatility models.

**Theorem 22 (local volatility alone gives no power above size).** For every test \(\phi\) of \(k\) paths and every local-volatility model in the alternative, \(E_{P^{\otimes k}}[\phi] \le \mathrm{size}(\phi)\). The same holds when the coefficients are restricted throughout to positive continuous functions, when the physical law is restricted throughout to \(P = \nu_\sigma\), or both, with size taken over the corresponding nulls. Explicitly, for \(n > s_0\) let \(\sigma_n = \sigma\) on \((0, n]\) and \(\sigma_n(x) = \sigma(n)x/n\) above \(n\), the completion \(g_1\) of Proposition 7 with \(R = n\), and let \(p_n = P(\max_{[0,T]} S \ge n)\). Then there is a null model \((\sigma_n, P_n)\) with \(|E_{P^{\otimes k}}[\phi] - E_{P_n^{\otimes k}}[\phi]| \le 1 - (1 - p_n)^k \le k p_n\), and \(p_n \to 0\).

*Status.* Conditional on uniqueness in law of the stopped equation (`karatzas2015distribution`, Proposition 9.1) and on the existence of the absorbed pricing laws (`mijatovic2010martingale`, Section 2, with Feller's test excluding explosion), as stated in Section 2. Machine-checked from these inputs stated as premises (Appendix A).

*Proof.* Each \(\sigma_n\) satisfies standing assumption 1, is positive and continuous when \(\sigma\) is, and has \(\int_n^H x/\sigma_n^2(x)\,dx = (n^2/\sigma(n)^2)\log(H/n) \to \infty\), so its absorbed diffusion exists, cannot explode because the scale function \(s(x) = x - c\) is unbounded above, and is a martingale by the JKP criterion. Let \(A_n\) be the event that the path stays below \(n\) on \([0, T]\). Stopping at the first hit of \(n\) is the identity on \(A_n\) and maps paths that reach \(n\) to paths that still reach \(n\), and the two coefficients agree on \((0, n)\), so uniqueness in law of the stopped equations gives \(\nu_\sigma(B \cap A_n) = \nu_{\sigma_n}(B \cap A_n)\) for every Borel \(B\). Put \(r_n = \nu_{\sigma_n}(A_n^c)\), which is positive exactly when \(p_n\) is, and \(P_n(B) = P(B \cap A_n) + (p_n/r_n)\,\nu_{\sigma_n}(B \cap A_n^c)\), or \(P_n = P\) if \(r_n = 0\). Then \(P_n\) is a probability equivalent to \(\nu_{\sigma_n}\) that agrees with \(P\) on \(A_n\), and it equals \(\nu_{\sigma_n}\) when \(P = \nu_\sigma\). The \(k\)-fold products agree on \(A_n^k\), whose complement has mass \(1 - (1 - p_n)^k\) under both, which gives the bound; \(p_n \to 0\) because continuous paths are bounded on \([0, T]\). Letting \(n \to \infty\) proves the theorem. \(\square\)

Theorem 22 is the local-volatility specialization of Theorem 18 and closes its scope gap: every null is a genuine driftless local-volatility diffusion, positive and continuous whenever the alternative is, under \(P = Q\) or any equivalent physical law. So local volatility, positivity and continuity alone do not make the presence of a bubble testable. It is the statistical form of the extrapolation problem of `jarrow2011detect` (§1 and §5), with Proposition 7 supplying the completions; we claim no novelty for the general mechanism, which `bahadur1956nonexistence`, unread, may anticipate. Two limits of scope should be stated plainly. First, the physical null \(P_n\) replaces the physical law above \(n\) by a rescaled pricing law, so it need not preserve the physical dynamics of the alternative. That does not mean a restriction on the physical dynamics escapes Theorem 22: the theorem already holds within \(P = Q\), where the physical drift is zero and the likelihood ratio is one. A test escapes only if its model class excludes the linear-tail completions \(\sigma_n\) of the proof; a restriction such as unspecified eventual regularity of the tail need not do so. Second, Theorem 22 concerns tests from finitely many paths with size controlled uniformly over the null; it is not a statement about population identification. Under \(P = Q\), with the full path law known, the bubble is identified (Proposition 19).

### 3.5 A usable price-based test

Sections 3.2 and 3.4 say what price data cannot identify. This section collects what a test from prices alone can use: the visited part of the JKP integral, an estimator of it, identification under a declared tail, pooling across names, calibrated bands, and a false-discovery list. None of it yet gives a test with useful power on realistic samples (Sections 5, 6 and 8).

What a single price path does reveal is the visited part of the JKP integral, through its local time. Let \(L_t^x\) be the semimartingale local time of \(S\) at level \(x\) up to a fixed time \(t\), normalized so that the occupation formula reads \(\int_0^t f(S_u)\,\sigma^2(S_u)\,du = \int f(x) L_t^x\,dx\) before absorption, and let \(m_t\) and \(M_t\) be the minimum and maximum of the path on \([0, t]\).

**Proposition 23 (the visited part of the JKP integral from local time).** Under standing assumptions 1 and 2, with \(S\) a supplied weak solution absorbed at zero, fix \(t \ge 0\) and set the integrand below to zero when \(S_u = 0\) or \(L_t^{S_u} = 0\). Then, outside one \(Q\)-null set and simultaneously for every Borel \(B \subseteq (0, \infty)\),

$$\int_0^t 1_B(S_u)\,\frac{S_u}{L_t^{S_u}}\,du = \int_{B \cap (m_t, M_t)} \frac{x}{\sigma^2(x)}\,dx < \infty.$$

The same holds almost surely under any probability \(P\) absolutely continuous with respect to \(Q\) on \(\mathcal{F}_t\).

*Status.* An application of the occupation formula (`bandi2003fully`, Lemmas 1–3), the quadratic variation of a stochastic integral (`etheridge2018continuous`, Theorem 7.11), the positivity of diffusion local time after a level is hit (`mijatovic2010martingale`, Propositions A.1–A.2), and Feller's test for finiteness when the price can reach zero; not a new stochastic result.
