Machine-checked from these inputs stated as hypotheses (Appendix A).

*Proof.* On one event of probability one, the occupation formula on rational intervals and uniqueness of finite measures on the line give equality of the occupation measure of the path with \(L_t^x\,dx\), before any path-dependent weight is chosen. Local time is positive at almost every level strictly between \(m_t\) and \(M_t\): each such level is hit before \(t\) and before absorption, and positivity at a rational time after the hit carries to \(t\) by monotonicity, then Tonelli's theorem passes to almost every level. Local time vanishes outside \([m_t, M_t]\). Dividing the density \(L_t^x\) out on the set where it is positive gives the identity. \(\square\)

With \(B = (0, \infty)\) the right side is the integral of \(x/\sigma^2\) over the whole visited range; with \(B = (a, b)\) the indicator on the left cannot in general be dropped. The identity holds through absorption at zero, where time spent at zero contributes nothing, and needs no recurrence or martingale assumption. The local time in the denominator is the semimartingale local time; the chronological local time \(L_t^x/\sigma^2(x)\) of `bandi2003fully` is a different object. Two limits should be stated plainly. The identity determines only the visited part of the JKP integral, \(\int_{m_t}^{M_t} x/\sigma^2(x)\,dx\), and says nothing about the tail beyond \(M_t\), which by Propositions 7 and 17 and Theorem 18 is what decides whether there is a bubble. And it is an identity for the continuous path and its exact local time: it provides no estimator from sampled prices and no rate.

Over a fixed compact interval, part of the JKP integral can be estimated from sampled prices. Suppose \(\log S\) has a representation \(d\log S_t = b_t\,dt + a(\log S_t)\,dW_t\) with diffusion coefficient \(a(y) = \sigma(e^y)/e^y\), continuously differentiable and positive, whose law on each finite horizon is equivalent to that of the driftless equation \(dY = a(Y)\,dW\), and observe \(S\) at \(n + 1\) equally spaced times in \([0, 1]\). Fix \(u < v\) and a lower bound \(c > 0\) on \(a^2 = \sigma(x)^2/x^2\) over \([e^u, e^v]\), supplied in advance. Let \(\Gamma_n\) be the kernel estimator of \(a^2\) of `jacod2000parametric`, built from the sampled log prices on a spatial grid of mesh \(2/\sqrt{n}\) with its occupation threshold, and let \(D\) be its event that the path spends enough time near every level of \([u, v]\), and \(D^n\) the sample version of that event. Set \(B = \int_{e^u}^{e^v} x/\sigma^2(x)\,dx\) and \(\hat B_n = \int_u^v 1/\max(\Gamma_n(y), c)\,dy\).

**Proposition 24 (a compact part of the JKP integral from sampled prices).** In this setting: (a) \(0 < B \le (v - u)/c\) and \(0 < \hat B_n \le (v - u)/c\), and \(\hat B_n\) is a finite sum over the cells of the grid that meet \([u, v]\), of each cell's length in \([u, v]\) divided by \(\max(\Gamma_n, c)\) at the cell, so it is a measurable function of the sampled prices. (b) For every sample, \(|\hat B_n - B| \le c^{-2}\int_u^v |\Gamma_n(y) - a^2(y)|\,dy\). (c) With \(D_n = D\) for every \(n\), or \(D_n = D^n\), \(n^{1/3}(\hat B_n - B)1_{D_n}\) is bounded in probability, and \(P(D_n \cap \{|\hat B_n - B| > \varepsilon\}) \to 0\) for every \(\varepsilon > 0\). Also \(P(D^n) \to P(D)\), and if \(P(D) > 0\) the same rate and consistency hold conditionally on \(D^n\).

*Status.* Conditional on Jacod's kernel estimation theorem (`jacod2000parametric`, Theorem 2), stated as a hypothesis with a vacuous instance; the change of variables, the bounds, the finite sum and the clipping bound (b) are proved without probability. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) The substitution \(x = e^y\) gives \(B = \int_u^v a(y)^{-2}\,dy\), and \(a^2 \ge c\) gives the bounds; \(\Gamma_n\) is constant on each grid cell, which gives the finite sum. (b) The map \(x \mapsto 1/\max(x, c)\) is \(c^{-2}\)-Lipschitz, and \(\max(a^2, c) = a^2\) on \([u, v]\). (c) The cited theorem gives the uniform rate \(n^{-1/3}\) for \(\Gamma_n\) on the occupation event and the convergence of the sample events, and (b) transfers it to \(\hat B_n\). \(\square\)

This is an application of Jacod's theorem together with the bound (b), not new estimation theory. Its premises matter. The floor \(c\) must be known in advance and cannot be fitted from the same data: if it exceeds the true minimum of \(a^2\), the estimator is biased for every \(n\) (with \(a^2 \equiv 1\) and \(c = 2\), \(\hat B_n = 1/2\) while \(B = 1\)). The interval is fixed, not the random visited range of the identity above; estimators over data-chosen intervals, such as the reciprocal-local-time estimator of a registered numerical experiment (Appendix A), are different and inherit nothing from this rate. Off the occupation event no consistency is asserted. And the estimator gives no confidence interval, says nothing about the tail beyond \(e^v\), and is not a test: Theorem 22 still applies.

A declared tail is the kind of restriction Theorem 22 calls for, and with it identification becomes exact. Fix, before seeing any data, an onset \(R > 0\) and a ratio \(r > 1\), and write \(B_1 = \int_R^{Rr} x/\sigma^2\,dx\), \(B_2 = \int_{Rr}^{Rr^2} x/\sigma^2\,dx\) and \(I_R = \int_R^\infty x/\sigma^2\,dx\).

**Proposition 25 (identification under a declared power tail).** Let \(\sigma\) be positive, continuous and admissible, with \(\sigma(x) = \kappa x^p\) for every \(x \ge R\), for some \(\kappa > 0\) and \(p\).

(a) \(B_1, B_2 > 0\), \(q = B_2/B_1 = r^{2 - 2p}\), \(p = 1 - \log q/(2\log r)\), \(I_R = B_1/(1 - q)\) if \(q < 1\) and \(I_R = \infty\) otherwise, and \(I_R < \infty\) exactly when \(B_2 < B_1\). Every pair of positive values of \((B_1, B_2)\) is attained, so these identifications are sharp.

(b) If only \(B_i \in [L_i, U_i]\) with \(0 < L_i \le U_i\) is known, the identified set of the label \(1_{\{I_R < \infty\}}\) contains \(0\) exactly when \(L_1 \le U_2\) and contains \(1\) exactly when \(L_2 < U_1\). If the bands are measurable and cover both integrals jointly with probability at least \(1 - \alpha\) at every null model of a specified family, the rule that rejects when \(U_2 < L_1\) has size at most \(\alpha\) over that family.

(c) With sampled prices, if the estimators of Proposition 24 for the two blocks satisfy their \(n^{-1/3}\) rate on the occupation events, the test rejecting when the two estimates differ by more than \(h_n\), with \(h_n \to 0\) and \(n^{1/3}h_n \to \infty\), has rejection probability tending to zero at each fixed null, and to the probability of the occupation event of \([R, Rr^2]\) at each fixed alternative.

(d) If the onset is not declared, the identified set of the label is \(\{0, 1\}\) over positive continuous coefficients that are eventually power laws, even with \(\sigma\) known on \((0, R_0]\) for any finite \(R_0\), and the bound of Theorem 22 holds within that class.

*Status.* Parts (a), (b) and the coefficient statement of (d) are unconditional and machine-checked, except that the size statement in (b) assumes the supplied joint coverage. Part (c) is machine-checked with the rate of the estimator of Proposition 24 and the convergence of its occupation events as premises; Proposition 24 proves them in that form, conditionally on Jacod's kernel estimation theorem. The test statement of (d) is conditional on the inputs of Theorem 22. The reading of the label as the presence of a bubble is conditional on the JKP criterion (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) The substitution \(x = ry\) gives \(B_2 = r^{2 - 2p} B_1\) on the power tail, and the tail integral is a power integral that converges exactly when \(p > 1\), that is, when \(q < 1\); the attaining coefficients are the completions of Proposition 7 of a line below \(R\). (b) Given positive values in the bands, (a) realizes every ratio in \([L_2/U_1, U_2/L_1]\), and the label is zero exactly when the ratio can be at least one; under joint coverage a rejection at a null forces an uncovered integral. (c) On the occupation event both estimates are within \(o(h_n)\) of their targets, so the threshold separates \(B_1 - B_2 > 0\) from \(B_1 - B_2 \le 0\). (d) The completions with exponents \(1\) and \(2\) above any level are eventually power laws with opposite labels, and the null completions in the proof of Theorem 22 are exact power laws beyond \(n\). \(\square\)

This is the parametric extrapolation of `jarrow2011detect` (§5.1) made exact; no gain over that method is claimed. The band rule's size rests on joint coverage that must be supplied, and the estimator of Proposition 24 does not supply it. The threshold test is pointwise, not uniform, and its power is capped by how often the path visits the whole interval. The identification comes from the declared onset and the exact form, and the data cannot test them: the tail above \(Rr^2\) is never observed.

A first cross-sectional step declares a common exponent: for names \(i = 1, \dots, m\) with declared onsets \(R_i\) and a common \(r\), \(\sigma_i(x) = \kappa_i x^p\) above \(R_i\), with one unknown \(p\) and separate scales \(\kappa_i\). Write \(B_{i,1}, B_{i,2}\) for name \(i\)'s blocks, and for rectangles \(B_{i,j} \in [L_{i,j}, U_{i,j}]\) put \(\ell_i = L_{i,2}/U_{i,1}\), \(u_i = U_{i,2}/L_{i,1}\), \(\ell_* = \max_i \ell_i\) and \(u_* = \min_i u_i\).

**Proposition 26 (a common power tail across names).** (a) Every name has \(B_{i,2}/B_{i,1} = q = r^{2 - 2p}\) and the same label \(1_{\{q < 1\}}\). If only the first blocks are observed, every \(p\) is compatible and the label set is \(\{0, 1\}\) for every panel; any common \(q > 0\) is attained with any positive first blocks. (b) With rectangles, the sharp set for \(q\) is \([\ell_*, u_*]\), empty if \(\ell_* > u_*\). When it is nonempty, the exponent set is \([1 - \log u_*/(2\log r), 1 - \log \ell_*/(2\log r)]\), the label set contains \(0\) exactly when \(u_* \ge 1\) and \(1\) exactly when \(\ell_* < 1\), and \(u_* < 1\) exactly when some single name has \(U_{i,2} < L_{i,1}\). (c) If measurable rectangles cover all \(2m\) integrals simultaneously with probability at least \(1 - \alpha\) at every null of the common-exponent class, the rule rejecting when \(\ell_* \le u_* < 1\) has size at most \(\alpha\); per-name coverage failures \(\alpha_i\) with \(\sum_i \alpha_i \le \alpha\) give that coverage without independence, and there is a two-name example in which each name's coverage is exactly \(1 - \alpha\) and the pooled rejection probability is exactly \(2\alpha\). (d) With sampled prices and the premises of Proposition 25(c) for every name under one joint law, the rule rejecting when any name's threshold test rejects has rejection probability tending to zero at each fixed common null and to \(P(\bigcup_i D_i)\) at each fixed common alternative, where \(D_i\) is name \(i\)'s occupation event; \(P(\bigcup_i D_i) \ge \max_i P(D_i)\).

*Status.* Parts (a)–(c) are unconditional, except that (c) assumes the supplied coverage; part (d) is conditional on the premises of Proposition 25(c) for each name. Machine-checked (Appendix A). The reading of the label as the presence of a bubble is conditional on the JKP criterion.

*Proof.* (a) and (b) follow from Proposition 25 name by name, with the separate scales realizing any first blocks; the intersection of intervals is \([\ell_*, u_*]\), and \(\min_i u_i < 1\) exactly when some \(u_i < 1\). (c) A rejection at a null forces an uncovered integral; the union bound gives the per-name version, and an explicit three-point space gives the example. (d) Apply Proposition 25(c) to each name and the union bound, using that the symmetric difference of the unions lies in the union of the symmetric differences. \(\square\)

So the intersection can narrow the exponent range and signal incompatibility when it is empty, but it cannot produce a positive verdict that no single name produces, and calibrating each name at \(\alpha\) does not control the pooled size. The only gain of the pooled threshold test is occupation: its power is the probability that some name's interval is fully visited, at least, and possibly equal to, the best single name's, with no improvement in rate and no uniformity. The common exponent is itself a strong assumption that the data cannot test and that membership of an index does not imply; a nonempty intersection does not confirm it. Methods that average weak evidence across names are a different route and are not analysed.

Propositions 25 and 26 take jointly covering bands as given. They can be constructed by split likelihood (`wasserman2019universal`) when the observations have a known likelihood. Suppose each name \(i\) has a nonempty finite catalogue \(\Theta_i\) of parameters, each specifying a coefficient of the declared power-tail form, with exponent \(p_i(\vartheta)\) and block integrals \(b_{i,1}(\vartheta), b_{i,2}(\vartheta)\), and an exact, strictly positive conditional likelihood \(g_{i,\vartheta}(v \mid u)\) of an evaluation block \(V_i\) given a training block \(U_i\). Fit \(\hat\vartheta_i\) on \(U_i\) alone, let \(T_i(\vartheta) = g_{i,\hat\vartheta_i}(V_i \mid U_i)/g_{i,\vartheta}(V_i \mid U_i)\), keep \(C_i = \{\vartheta : T_i(\vartheta) \le 1/\alpha_i\}\) with \(\sum_i \alpha_i \le \alpha\), and take the extreme block integrals and the exponents \(A_i = p_i(C_i)\) over \(C_i\).

**Proposition 27 (calibrated bands in a finite catalogue).** Suppose each name's observations have the likelihood of its true parameter, with arbitrary dependence across names. (a) Every \(C_i\) is nonempty, and the bands \([\min_{C_i} b_{i,j}, \max_{C_i} b_{i,j}]\) have positive endpoints and cover all \(2m\) block integrals simultaneously with probability at least \(1 - \alpha\); in particular each name's band rule of Proposition 25(b) has size at most \(\alpha_i\) whatever the other names are. (b) If all true exponents are equal, \(P(\bigcap_i A_i = \emptyset) \le \alpha\). (c) If the catalogue has null parameters \(p_i \le 1\), then \(E_i = \min_{p_i(\vartheta) \le 1} T_i(\vartheta)\) is a positive e-value for name \(i\)'s null, \(E_P[E_i] \le 1\), and \(\min(1, 1/E_i)\) is a valid p-value; \(C_i\) excludes the null parameters exactly when \(E_i > 1/\alpha_i\).

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound `wasserman2019universal`, stated as a hypothesis; there is not yet a hypothesis structure with an instance for it. Machine-checked from that input (Appendix A). The link between each likelihood and the pricing coefficient it lists is a modelling premise and appears in no formal statement.

*Proof.* At the true parameter, \(E_P[T_i(\vartheta_i^*)] \le 1\) by the split-likelihood bound, so Markov's inequality gives \(P(\vartheta_i^* \notin C_i) \le \alpha_i\), and the union bound gives simultaneous coverage without independence. The bands, the exponent intersection and the e-value are images of \(C_i\), and \(E_i \le T_i(\vartheta_i^*)\) at a null. \(\square\)

So the size statements of Propositions 25(b) and 26(c) hold with derived rather than supplied coverage, rejecting a common exponent when the retained exponent sets do not intersect tests that restriction within the catalogue without verifying the power form outside the observed range, and not rejecting does not establish it. Its limit is severe and should be stated plainly: everything rests on a correctly specified finite catalogue of exact likelihoods for the observations, each tied to the pricing coefficient it lists, and for price data such a catalogue is available only under strong assumptions (Proposition 28), since an equivalent physical law alone does not specify a transition likelihood. Checks on finite examples confirm the arithmetic, not validity for price data, and the per-name e-values are inputs for a later multiple-testing procedure, not such a procedure.

A first instance with actual price laws exists under strong assumptions. Suppose the physical law equals the pricing law (\(P = Q\)), and each name's price follows exactly one of a finite, declared catalogue of global power laws \(\sigma(x) = \kappa x^p\) with \(p \in \{1, 2\}\) and \(\kappa > 0\), containing at least one candidate with \(p = 1\): geometric Brownian motion, a martingale, or the reciprocal of a three-dimensional Bessel process, a strict local martingale. Observe each name at the dates of a declared grid, train on its first prices by maximum likelihood, and evaluate on the rest, starting the evaluation chain at the last training price.

**Proposition 28 (a price-law instance of the catalogue calibration).** In this setting, with arbitrary dependence across names: (a) the products of the explicit lognormal and Bessel transition densities over the evaluation dates are exact, positive, normalized conditional densities of the evaluation prices given the training prices, the maximum-likelihood selector is measurable, and every candidate coefficient has the declared power tail with explicit block integrals; so all premises of Proposition 27 hold, and \(p = 1\) is a martingale while \(p = 2\) is strict. (b) All conclusions of Proposition 27 hold: calibrated bands, the common-exponent test, and per-name e-values and p-values for "no bubble", unconditionally, with no occupation event. (c) On a fixed grid any two candidates have mutually equivalent laws, so no test with size below one has power one at any alternative, and a test of size zero has no power.

*Status.* An application of Proposition 27 with the cited lognormal and Bessel transition laws, which enter as hypothesis structures whose instances are vacuous; the laws of the prices on the grid are supplied as premises (Appendix A). Machine-checked.

*Proof.* The finite products of positive normalized one-step kernels are positive and normalized by iterated integration, and the true candidate's product is the conditional density of the observed evaluation prices. The block integrals are those of Proposition 25 for exact power laws. Then Proposition 27 applies. All candidate grid densities are positive, so the grid laws are mutually equivalent, which gives (c). \(\square\)

Its limits are severe. It assumes no physical drift, exact global power laws rather than a tail above a level, and a correct finite catalogue; physical drift, other powers or scales, stochastic volatility or jumps void the calibration. By (c), power at realistic sampling, such as two years of monthly prices, may be low, and it is not quantified here.

The same construction works for any finite catalogue of constant-elasticity powers, with absorption at zero retained. Let each candidate be \(dS = \kappa S^p\,dW\) for some \(p > 0\), \(\kappa > 0\), absorbed at zero, with the null catalogue the candidates with \(p \le 1\). Let the reference measure be Lebesgue measure on \((0, \infty)\) plus a unit atom at zero, and the one-step kernel the cited constant-elasticity density on \((0, \infty)\), with an atom at zero of the cited mass for \(p < 1\) and none for \(p \ge 1\), and trapped at zero from zero. Split, fit and evaluate as in Proposition 28, with \(\hat G_i\) the fitted evaluation likelihood and \(G_{i,0}\) the null maximum.

**Proposition 29 (a constant-elasticity catalogue with absorption).** In this setting, with \(P = Q\) and arbitrary dependence across names: (a) every \(C_i\) is nonempty and covers the true pair with probability at least \(1 - \alpha_i\), jointly at least \(1 - \alpha\); \(\hat G_i > 0\) implies \(G_{i,0} > 0\), so the score \(E_i = \hat G_i/G_{i,0}\), or \(0\) if \(\hat G_i = 0\), is finite on every nonnegative array, with \(E_P[E_i] \le 1\) at every true null and a valid p-value. (b) The bands from the closed-form block integrals cover jointly with probability at least \(1 - \alpha\), the band rule has size at most \(\alpha_i\), and the common-exponent test has size at most \(\alpha\). (c) The e-BH rule of Proposition 40 applied to these \(E_i\) has \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\), also after any zero-masked screen, and the labels are bubble labels: \(p \le 1\) is a martingale and \(p > 1\) a strict local martingale. (d) A name absorbed by the end of training has \(E_i = 1\) and \(C_i\) the whole catalogue; a name absorbed later has \(E_i \le 1\); so a name observed at zero is never flagged.

*Status.* An application of the cited constant-elasticity kernels, the support-aware split-likelihood bound (proved), the JKP criterion and the e-BH theorem, the stochastic inputs stated as hypothesis structures with vacuous instances and the grid laws supplied as premises. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* The chain densities are normalized from every starting value by iterated integration against the reference measure, and the support-aware bound gives coverage and the null moment. If the fitted likelihood is positive, either the fitted candidate is null, or a positive \(p > 1\) chain has an all-positive or all-zero pattern, which some null candidate also gives positive likelihood. The bands use the block integrals of Proposition 25; the labels are the JKP criterion, \(\int^\infty x/x^{2p}\,dx < \infty\) exactly when \(p > 1\). \(\square\)

A default thus counts as evidence for an absorbing null, by the structure of the procedure rather than by discarding defaulted names. The premises are those of Proposition 28: \(P = Q\) and a correct finite catalogue. The misspecification bounds of Proposition 43 show that an off-grid volatility makes the guarantee nearly vacuous at high sampling frequency, and power at monthly sampling may be low; it is not quantified here.

Off-grid volatility and physical drift, for the geometric-Brownian part of the null, can be absorbed into the null itself. Add to each name's null the continuous family of Gaussian conditional laws for the evaluation prices, with log returns independent and normal with mean \((b - \kappa^2/2)\Delta_j\) and variance \(\kappa^2\Delta_j\) over steps \(\Delta_j\), for any drift \(b\) and volatility \(\kappa > 0\). With evaluation prices \(y_1, \dots, y_n\) after the last training price \(y_0\), log returns \(R_j\), \(\tau = \sum_j \Delta_j\), \(a = \sum_j R_j/\tau\) and \(Q = \sum_j (R_j - a\Delta_j)^2/\Delta_j\), let \(D_G\) be the supremum of these densities over \(b \in \mathbb{R}\) and \(\kappa > 0\), and set \(E_i^{\mathrm{drift}} = \hat G_i/\max(G_{i,0}, D_G)\), or \(0\) if \(\hat G_i = 0\) or \(D_G = \infty\).

**Proposition 30 (a continuous Gaussian null).** In the setting of Proposition 29, with the actual law \(P\) arbitrary across names and in training, suppose each name without a bubble has, given its training prices, either a catalogue null law or one of these Gaussian laws for its evaluation prices. (a) On positive paths with \(Q > 0\), the supremum is attained at \(\kappa^2 = Q/n\), \(b = a + Q/(2n)\), and

$$\log D_G = -\frac{n}{2}\Big[\log\frac{2\pi Q}{n} + 1\Big] - \frac12\sum_j \log\Delta_j - \sum_j \log y_j;$$

if \(Q = 0\), \(D_G = \infty\). The score is finite, nonnegative and measurable on every nonnegative array. (b) At every name without a bubble, \(E_P[E_i^{\mathrm{drift}} \mid \text{training}] \le 1\), uniformly over all drifts and volatilities, so \(E_i^{\mathrm{drift}} > 1/\alpha_i\) has size at most \(\alpha_i\), \(\min(1, 1/E_i^{\mathrm{drift}})\) is a valid p-value, and the probability of some false rejection is at most \(\sum\alpha_i\), without independence. (c) The e-BH rule of Proposition 40 applied to these scores has \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\), also after any zero-masked screen with the original \(m\). (d) \(E_i^{\mathrm{drift}} \le E_i\) on every path; a name absorbed by the end of training has score one, one absorbed later has score at most one, a positive path with \(Q = 0\) has score zero, and with a single evaluation step the score is at most one everywhere, so none of these is ever flagged.

*Status.* An application of the composite-null split-likelihood bound (`wasserman2019universal`, Theorem 3), which is proved here, and, for (c), the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29, and the normalization of the Gaussian family is proved. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) For fixed \(\kappa^2 = w\), the log density is maximized at \(b = a + w/2\), leaving \(-(n/2)\log w - Q/(2w)\) up to constants, maximized at \(w = Q/n\); if \(Q = 0\) it is unbounded as \(w \to 0\). (b) The denominator dominates every null density, catalogue or Gaussian, so the score's conditional mean under any null is at most the selected density's mass, which is at most one; Markov's inequality gives the tests. (c) is the e-BH theorem. (d) A larger denominator gives a smaller score; the absorbed cases are Proposition 29(d), and with one step \(Q = 0\). \(\square\)

This answers the off-grid failure of Proposition 43 for the geometric-Brownian part of the null, with no grid and no total-variation budget. The cost is power: rejections under \(E_i^{\mathrm{drift}}\) are a subset of those under \(E_i\), and the continuous Gaussian null absorbs much of the evidence a mild constant-elasticity alternative leaves in returns; a simulation below illustrates how much. The link to bubbles is a premise: reading such a name as bubble-free needs a supplied pricing law under which its price is driftless geometric Brownian motion, whose existence is not proved here, and neither a good Gaussian fit nor the sign of the drift supplies it. Drift at other powers, stochastic volatility, jumps and rounding are not covered.

The drift need not be left free. Declare in advance, for each name, a closed interval \(B_i = [b_{\mathrm{lo}}, b_{\mathrm{hi}}]\) of physical drifts, let \(D_{G,B}\) be the supremum of the Gaussian densities of Proposition 30 over drifts in \(B_i\) and all \(\kappa > 0\), and set \(E_i^B = \hat G_i/\max(G_{i,0}, D_{G,B})\), or \(0\) if \(\hat G_i = 0\) or \(D_{G,B} = \infty\).

**Proposition 31 (a declared drift interval).** In the setting of Proposition 30, suppose each name without a bubble has, given its training prices, either a catalogue null law or a Gaussian law with drift in \(B_i\) and any volatility for its evaluation prices. (a) On positive paths \(D_{G,B}\) is infinite exactly when \(Q = 0\) and \(a \in B_i\); otherwise it is the largest of the Gaussian likelihoods at the two drift endpoints, each with its profiled variance \(w_c = 2A(c)/(n + \sqrt{n^2 + \tau A(c)})\), where \(A(c) = Q + \tau(a - c)^2\), and at the unconstrained maximizer of Proposition 30(a) when its drift lies in \(B_i\). The score is finite and measurable on every nonnegative array. (b) At every name without a bubble the score has conditional mean at most one, so the per-name tests, the p-value and the union bound hold as in Proposition 30, and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 has \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\), also after a zero-masked screen with the original \(m\). (c) On every path \(E_i^{\mathrm{drift}} \le E_i^B \le E_i\); a narrower interval gives a larger score, and as the intervals grow to the whole line the scores decrease to \(E_i^{\mathrm{drift}}\). Absorbed names are never flagged.

*Status.* An application of the composite-null split-likelihood bound (`wasserman2019universal`), which is proved here, and, for the false-discovery bound, the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) For a fixed variance the best drift in \(B_i\) is the clipped unconstrained one, so the supremum lies on an edge of the interval or on the ridge \(b = a + w/2\); along each edge the profile in the variance is unimodal with peak \(w_c\), and along the ridge it peaks at \(w = Q/n\). If \(Q = 0\) and \(a \in B_i\), the likelihood is unbounded as \(w \to 0\). (b) The denominator dominates every null density, as in Proposition 30(b). (c) Larger parameter sets give larger denominators. \(\square\)

This ordering is not a power guarantee, and the interval must be fixed before the data are seen; choosing it after inspecting the scores voids the guarantee. In one simulation, of daily prices over two years with the first year for training, the interval \([-0.10, 0.20]\), a catalogue of powers \(p \in \{1/2, 1, 3/2\}\) and scales \(\kappa \in \{0.2, 0.3, 0.45\}\), \(\alpha = 0.1\) and 10,000 paths per law, the score of Proposition 29 had null mean 28 and a \(6.9\%\) false-alarm rate under geometric Brownian motion with off-grid volatility \(0.35\), while the free-drift and bounded-drift scores had means about \(0.13\) and \(0.14\) and false-alarm rates below \(0.1\%\) under every Gaussian law tried, including one with drift outside the interval. Against \(p = 3/2\) the price is power: with \(\kappa = 0.3\) it fell from \(41\%\) for the score of Proposition 29 to \(5\%\) with free drift and \(7\%\) with bounded drift, and with \(\kappa = 0.45\) from \(60\%\) to \(21\%\) and \(24\%\). Bounding the drift recovers little; most of the loss comes from leaving the volatility scale free. This is one design and one interval, not a systematic study. Outside the interval the guarantee is lost. For a Gaussian law whose drift lies at distance \(\delta\) from the interval, the in-bound law with the nearest drift and the same volatility \(\sigma\) is, by Proposition 43(c), within total variation \(\sqrt{1 - \exp(-\delta^2\tau/(4\sigma^2))}\) of it, whatever the number of observations over the evaluation horizon \(\tau\): with the interval \([-0.2, 0.2]\), \(\sigma = 0.3\) and one year, a drift of \(0.21\) gives about \(0.017\) and a drift of \(0.25\) about \(0.083\). This is a sufficient budget only: comparing bounded functions as in Proposition 43(a) turns it into a per-name size of at most \(\alpha_i\) plus the budget, not \(\alpha_i\), it does not restore the false-discovery bound for the unclipped scores, and it tends to one as \(\sigma \to 0\), so the interval alone gives no small uniform budget. Stochastic volatility, jumps, time-varying drift and rounding are not covered.

Restricting the volatility scale recovers much of the lost power, at the price of fragility. Declare in advance, for each name, a closed drift interval \(B_i = [b_{\mathrm{lo}}, b_{\mathrm{hi}}]\) and a closed scale interval \([s_{\mathrm{lo}}, s_{\mathrm{hi}}]\) with \(s_{\mathrm{lo}} > 0\), let \(D_{G,B,C}\) be the maximum of the Gaussian densities of Proposition 30 over this rectangle, and set \(E_i^{B,C} = \hat G_i/\max(G_{i,0}, D_{G,B,C})\), or \(0\) if \(\hat G_i = 0\), where \(G_{i,0}\) keeps every catalogue null, including those with scales outside the interval.

**Proposition 32 (a declared drift and scale rectangle).** In the setting of Proposition 30, suppose each name without a bubble has, given its training prices, either a catalogue null law or a Gaussian law with drift in \(B_i\) and scale in \([s_{\mathrm{lo}}, s_{\mathrm{hi}}]\) for its evaluation prices. (a) On positive paths \(D_{G,B,C}\) is the largest of the Gaussian likelihoods at at most five explicit points of the rectangle: each drift endpoint with its profiled variance, the positive root of a quadratic, clipped to the scale interval; each variance endpoint with its profiled drift clipped to the drift interval; and the unconstrained maximizer of Proposition 30(a) when it lies in the rectangle. It is positive and finite there, and the score is finite and measurable on every nonnegative array. (b) At every name without a bubble the score has conditional mean at most one, so the per-name tests, the p-value and the union bound hold as in Proposition 30, and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 has \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\), also after a zero-masked screen with the original \(m\). (c) On every path \(E_i^{\mathrm{drift}} \le E_i^{B} \le E_i^{B,C} \le E_i\), where \(E_i^B\) is the bounded-drift score of Proposition 31, so the rejections are nested; enlarging the rectangle decreases the score, and as the scale interval grows to \((0, \infty)\) the score decreases to \(E_i^B\). Absorbed names are never flagged.

*Status.* An application of the composite-null split-likelihood bound (`wasserman2019universal`), which is proved here, and, for the false-discovery bound, the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) Along each drift edge the profile in the variance is unimodal, with its peak at the displayed root; along the ridge \(b = a + w/2\) it peaks at \(w = Q/n\); every point of the rectangle is dominated by one of the five candidates, and all five are feasible. (b) The denominator dominates every null density, so the argument of Proposition 30(b) applies. (c) Larger parameter sets give larger denominators. \(\square\)

In the simulation design used above for the bounded-drift score, with drift \(0.05\) for the Gaussian laws and scale intervals \([0.25, 0.35]\) and \([0.28, 0.32]\), power against \(p = 3/2\) rose from \(20\%\) with free drift to \(44\%\) and \(52\%\) at \(\kappa = 0.45\), against \(60\%\) for the score of Proposition 29, and from \(6\%\) to \(12\%\) and \(24\%\) at \(\kappa = 0.3\), against \(41\%\); Gaussian nulls inside the rectangle kept means below one and false-alarm rates below \(0.3\%\) at \(\alpha = 0.1\). A true volatility outside the declared interval loses the guarantee: at \(0.40\), the null mean was \(1.2\) and \(5.5\) for the two intervals, with false-alarm rates of \(2\%\) and \(4\%\), and at \(0.25\), outside the narrower interval, \(2.2\) and \(2\%\). The narrower the interval, the more power and the more fragility. The interval must be declared before the data are seen; choosing it from the training data voids the guarantee. One honest way to declare it is described next. A continuum of elasticity exponents or scales outside the finite catalogue, stochastic volatility, jumps and rounding are not covered.

An honest declaration costs most of that power. Declare the rectangle of Proposition 32 from a disjoint earlier record \(W\), by measurable rules fixed in advance, with a fallback to the free-drift score of Proposition 30, and call the resulting score \(E_i^{\mathrm{dec}}\). For a name without a bubble, the coverage event is that its true null law is retained by the declared rectangle or by the fallback.

**Proposition 33 (declarations from an earlier window).** Suppose that, given \(W\) and its training prices, each name without a bubble has a fixed catalogue null or Gaussian law for its evaluation prices. (a) On the coverage event, \(E_i^{\mathrm{dec}}\) has conditional mean at most one given \(W\) and the training prices, so \(E_i^{\mathrm{dec}} > 1/\alpha_i\) has conditional size at most \(\alpha_i\); if coverage holds almost surely, the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 has \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\), also conditionally on \(W\). (b) If coverage fails with probability at most \(\delta\), the rule \(E_i^{\mathrm{dec}} > 1/\alpha_i\) has size at most \(\alpha_i + (1 - \alpha_i)\delta\), the threshold \((1 - \delta)/(\alpha_i - \delta)\) has size at most \(\alpha_i\) when \(\delta < \alpha_i\), and the safe score \(\min(E_i^{\mathrm{dec}}, c_i)/(1 + (c_i - 1)\delta)\), for \(c_i \ge 1\), has mean at most one, so e-BH applied to it keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\) under any dependence; it can reject only if \(\delta < \alpha_i\) and \(c_i > (1 - \delta)/(\alpha_i - \delta)\). (c) If the earlier window has \(n \ge 2\) Gaussian log returns with the same parameters as the later law, the parameters whose likelihood is at least \(\delta\) times that of a reference Gaussian law fixed in advance lie in an explicit rectangle, with drift and scale endpoints computed from the two roots of a one-variable equation in the variance and with a positive lower scale; this rectangle fails to retain the true parameters with probability at most \(\delta\), and the fallback has probability zero. (d) Any measurable upper scale that covers every scale almost surely is infinite almost surely; a finite upper scale misses a later scale with probability tending to one as that scale grows, so a change of scale between the windows breaks (c); and for every \(\gamma \in (0, 1]\) there is a catalogue and a declaration failing with probability \(\gamma\) under which the raw score of a true driftless geometric Brownian motion has infinite null mean.

*Status.* An application of the composite-null split-likelihood bound and likelihood confidence inversion (`wasserman2019universal`), both proved here, the mixture bound of Proposition 46 and, for the false-discovery bounds, the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29, and the counterexample in (d) needs no cited input. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) On the coverage event the denominator dominates the true null density, so the argument of Proposition 30(b) applies conditionally on \(W\). (b) Integrate: the conditional mean is at most one on the coverage event and the capped score is at most \(c_i\) off it. (c) The likelihood set is a confidence set by the split-likelihood bound with a single-point null; for each variance, the drift section is an interval around \(a + w/2\), and the variance range is between the two roots. (d) A positive density gives every set of positive measure positive probability; the counterexample pairs a rare narrow declaration with the strict power-two catalogue law, whose density bound makes the raw mean diverge. \(\square\)

The first premise is substantive: given the earlier record as well as the training prices, the later evaluation law must be the same law with the same parameters. Disjoint timestamps do not ensure it, and a bound on parameter change, or an envelope as in Proposition 43, would have to be supplied. Because coverage is only probabilistic, the raw score must not be used. In one simulation, with a one-year daily earlier window, \(\delta = 0.05\), the reference law with drift \(0\) and volatility \(0.3\), the test design above on the following two years, \(\alpha = 0.1\), cap \(50\) and 2,000 paths per law, the rectangle covered the true parameters in at least \(99.6\%\) of earlier windows, so the budget \(\delta\) is conservative. Under Gaussian nulls the safe score never rejected. Against \(p = 3/2\) its power was \(8\%\) at \(\kappa = 0.3\) and \(15\%\) at \(\kappa = 0.45\), against \(24\%\) and \(52\%\) for the narrower interval after Proposition 32, chosen knowing the true scale; to reject, the uncapped score had to exceed \(34.5\) rather than \(10\). Much of the power recovered by a scale interval thus depends on knowing the scale, and the free-drift score of Proposition 30 remains the simplest valid choice.

Variance need not accrue in calendar time. Overnight and weekend gaps, and the open and close, carry different amounts of variance per unit of calendar time, while the physical drift accrues in calendar time. Declare in advance a finite catalogue \(\mathcal V_i\) of variance clocks \(v = (v_1, \dots, v_n)\), with \(v_j > 0\), that contains the calendar clock \(v = \Delta\), and consider the laws whose log returns are independent normal with mean \(b\Delta_j - wv_j/2\) and variance \(wv_j\), for any drift \(b\) and scale \(w > 0\). On a positive path put \(A_v = \sum_j \Delta_j^2/v_j\), \(B_v = \sum_j \Delta_j R_j/v_j\), \(Q_v = \sum_j R_j^2/v_j - B_v^2/A_v\) and \(H_v = \sum_j v_j - (\sum_j \Delta_j)^2/A_v\), let \(D_v\) be the supremum of these densities over \(b\) and \(w\), and set \(E_i^{\mathrm{clock}} = \hat G_i/\max(G_{i,0}, \max_{v \in \mathcal V_i} D_v)\), or \(0\) if \(\hat G_i = 0\) or the denominator is infinite.

**Proposition 34 (declared variance clocks).** In the setting of Proposition 30: (a) if \(Q_v > 0\), the supremum is attained uniquely at \(w^* = 2Q_v/(n + \sqrt{n^2 + H_vQ_v})\) and \(b^* = (B_v + w^*\sum_j\Delta_j/2)/A_v\), with a closed-form value; if \(Q_v = 0\), \(D_v = \infty\). \(Q_v = 0\) exactly when all return rates \(R_j/\Delta_j\) coincide, whatever the clock; this set has measure zero when \(n \ge 2\), and contains every positive path when \(n = 1\). (b) Suppose that, given its training prices, each name without a bubble has an evaluation law that is a mixture of catalogue null laws and such clock laws. Then the score is finite and measurable, has conditional mean at most one, \(E_i^{\mathrm{clock}} > 1/\alpha_i\) has size at most \(\alpha_i\), and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\). (c) \(D_{cv} = D_v\) for every \(c > 0\), and \(D_\Delta\) is the Gaussian supremum of Proposition 30; so \(E_i^{\mathrm{clock}} \le E_i^{\mathrm{drift}}\), adding clocks lowers the score, and if every clock is proportional to \(\Delta\) the score is the free-drift score.

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound, which is proved here, with an explicit Gaussian profile, and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) Completing the square in \(b\) leaves \(Q_v\) and \(H_v\); in \(w\), the gap from the root \(w^*\) of \(H_vw^2 + 4nw - 4Q_v = 0\) is \(-(n/2)(\log t + 1/t - 1) - (H_v/8)(w - w^*)^2/w\) with \(t = w/w^*\), which is negative for \(w \ne w^*\). \(Q_v\) is the weighted residual of regressing the returns on \(\Delta\), so it vanishes exactly when the returns are proportional to \(\Delta\). (b) The denominator dominates every null component; integrate over the latent law. (c) Scaling \(v\) by \(c\) and \(w\) by \(1/c\) leaves each density unchanged. \(\square\)

Covering a clock costs power, as other enlargements of the null do. In a simulation with two years of weekday closes, weekend gaps of three calendar days, training on the first year, \(\alpha = 0.1\) and 2,000 paths per law, the null was a geometric Brownian motion whose variance accrues only in trading time. The free-drift score, which does not cover it, had null mean \(0.23\) and a false-alarm rate of \(0.05\%\). With the trading clock added, the score covered the null, but its power against \(p = 3/2\), \(\kappa = 0.45\) fell from \(6.2\%\) to \(0.55\%\). The constant-elasticity forecast in that design ignores the clock, which weakens both scores; a forecast that follows a clock is given in Proposition 64. The guarantee is for exact prices and declared clocks: a clock fitted to the data has no coverage guarantee, and observation noise, rounding and jumps are not covered. At intraday frequency, with a forecast that follows the clock, the declared clock pays for itself. A simulation used sessions of 6.5 hours with overnight gaps and a declared U-shaped clock, 2,000 paths per null and 1,000 per alternative, and constant-elasticity alternatives moving in clock time. There, the score of Proposition 34 had no rejections at \(\alpha = 0.05\) on any of five Gaussian nulls, including two whose clocks lie outside the declared set, which carry no guarantee. Its power against the four alternatives with \(p \in \{3/2, 2\}\) and \(\kappa \in \{0.3, 0.45\}\) was \(85\) to \(100\%\) over 250 sessions of five-minute bars, \(3\) to \(65\%\) over 60 sessions, and \(11\) to \(77\%\) at hourly sampling over 250 sessions. The free-drift score with a calendar-time forecast and null also had essentially no false alarms on these paths, but found at most \(33\%\) and mostly under \(7\%\). A clock-aware forecast over a calendar-time Gaussian null, in contrast, rejected nulls with clocks outside the declared set in \(26\) to \(40\%\) of five-minute paths. The 250 sessions include the training half, and real intraday prices may not follow any declared clock.

Implied volatility can serve as the clock, known one step ahead. Let prices \(S_t\) and implied volatilities \(IV_t > 0\) be adapted to a filtration \((\mathcal F_t)\), with the training record in \(\mathcal F_0\), deterministic durations \(\Delta_t > 0\), returns \(R_t = \log(S_t/S_{t-1})\) and \(v_t = IV_{t-1}^2\Delta_t\). Declare, before the evaluation record, a band \(0 < w_L \le w_H\) for the ratio of realized to implied variance. The null is that, for one drift \(b\) and one ratio \(w \in [w_L, w_H]\) over the whole record,

$$R_t \mid \mathcal F_{t-1} \sim N\big(b\Delta_t - wv_t/2,\ wv_t\big), \qquad 1 \le t \le N;$$

how implied volatility itself evolves is unrestricted. Choose normalized forecasts \(q_t\) of \(R_t\) measurable in \(\mathcal F_{t-1}\), let \(D_t^{IV}\) be the supremum over \(b\) and \(w \in [w_L, w_H]\) of the product of these conditional normal densities over the returns up to \(t\), and set \(E_t^{IV} = \prod_{j \le t} q_j(R_j)/D_t^{IV}\), with \(E_0^{IV} = 1\).

**Proposition 35 (an implied-volatility-scaled null).** (a) For \(t \ge 1\), \(D_t^{IV}\) is positive, finite and measurable, and is attained at the scale of Proposition 34(a) computed on the realized \(v_j\) and clipped to \([w_L, w_H]\), or at \(w_L\) if the returns are proportional to \(\Delta\), with the corresponding drift; in particular it is finite with one step. (b) Under any law satisfying the null, \(E^{IV}\) is nonnegative and adapted, and for every stopping time \(\tau \le N\) and \(0 < \alpha \le 1\), \(E[E_\tau^{IV} \mid \mathcal F_0] \le 1\) and \(P(E_t^{IV} \ge 1/\alpha \text{ for some } t \le N \mid \mathcal F_0) \le \alpha\); the same holds for any score with a smaller numerator and a larger denominator. (c) A local-lognormal law whose returns are \(N(b\Delta_t - a_t/2, a_t)\) given the past, with \(a_t = \kappa^2(S_{t-1}/s)^{2p-2}\Delta_t\), and with \(IV_{t-1} = \kappa(S_{t-1}/s)^{p-1}/\sqrt{w_0}\) for some \(w_0 \in [w_L, w_H]\), satisfies the null for every \(p\), so (b) holds for it.

*Status.* An application of the product rule for conditional e-values, proved from Mathlib, with the profile of Proposition 34. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) Completing the square in \(b\) as for Proposition 34 leaves a function of \(w\) that increases up to the free root and decreases after it, so its maximum over the band is at the clipped root; the lower end keeps it finite when the weighted residual vanishes. (b) Under the null with the true \((b, w)\), each factor \(q_t(R_t)/p_{b,w,t}(R_t)\) has conditional mean \(\int q_t = 1\) given \(\mathcal F_{t-1}\), so their product is a test martingale; \(D_t^{IV}\) dominates the true product, so \(E^{IV}\) is at most that martingale, and optional stopping and Ville's inequality apply, conditionally on each event of \(\mathcal F_0\). (c) Substituting gives \(w_0 v_t = a_t\). \(\square\)

The conditioning must be on the past only. Treating the whole implied-volatility path as given fails: a later implied volatility can reveal the sign of an earlier return, and a forecast that bets on that sign then rejects with probability one at threshold 2. By (c), level dependence that implied volatility tracks belongs to the null, so a rejection shows a departure from the null, such as non-Gaussian returns, a changing variance ratio or a changing drift, and not necessarily a bubble; \(E^{IV}\) need not itself be a supermartingale. In a check with 250 daily returns, the band \([0.5, 1]\), a forecast with \(p = 2\) level dependence that ignores implied volatility, and 2,000 paths per law, the score never reached 20 under the local-lognormal law with \(p = 2\), \(\kappa = 0.45\) and implied volatility tracking the level, where it stayed at or below one at every time on every path, nor under a random predictable implied volatility. With the same returns but implied volatility frozen at its starting value, it reached 20 on \(77\%\) of paths: a departure from the null, not a bubble. A variance ratio drifting inside the band, which the null does not cover, was not flagged by this forecast. The band must cover the actual ratio of realized to implied variance, and its width decides how much level dependence is absorbed. For monthly S&P 500 data from 1990 to 2007, the mean realized variance was \(14.93\) against a mean squared implied volatility of \(33.23\) (`bollerslev2009expected`, Table 1), a ratio of about \(0.45\), below a band such as \([0.5, 1]\). Returns with jumps, regime switches, stochastic volatility beyond the implied volatility, or a time-varying variance premium are outside the null.

Other names can narrow the clock catalogue. For a tested name, take \(r\) reference names observed over the same evaluation steps, and let \(A\) be all the training information. Form a normalized forecast \(q^{\mathrm{ref}}\) of the references' joint evaluation prices from \(A\) alone, for example a mixture over clocks of products of Gaussian densities. For each declared clock \(v\), let \(D^{\mathrm{ref}}_v\) be the product of the references' own Gaussian suprema on that clock, as in Proposition 34, and keep the calendar clock and every clock with \(q^{\mathrm{ref}}/D^{\mathrm{ref}}_v \le 1/\delta\); call the kept set \(C\). Score the tested name with the denominator of Proposition 34 over the clocks in \(C\), giving \(E^{\mathrm{sel}}\), with any normalized forecast that does not use its own evaluation prices, and let \(E^{\mathrm{safe}} = \min(E^{\mathrm{sel}}, c)/(1 + (c - 1)\delta)\) for a fixed \(c \ge 1\).

**Proposition 36 (a clock confidence set from other names).** Suppose that, for a name without a bubble, given \(A\) there is a latent component that is either a catalogue null for the tested name, or a clock \(v\) together with drifts and scales for which the tested evaluation law is the Gaussian law of Proposition 34 on \(v\) and, given \(A\) and the component, the references are independent Gaussian laws on the same clock \(v\), independent of the tested name. (a) The calendar clock is always kept, \(C\) grows as \(\delta\) decreases, and on every Gaussian component the true clock is excluded with probability at most \(\delta\), with no union cost over the catalogue. (b) Given the references, the raw score has conditional mean at most one when the true clock is kept. In all cases \(E^{\mathrm{safe}}\) has mean at most one given \(A\), \(E^{\mathrm{sel}} > 1/\alpha\) has probability at most \(\alpha + (1 - \alpha)\delta\), and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 applied to the safe scores with the original \(m\) keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\). (c) \(E^{\mathrm{sel}}\) is at least the score over the full catalogue; the safe score is at most \(c/(1 + (c - 1)\delta)\), and can reject at level \(\alpha\) only if \(\delta < \alpha\) and \(c > (1 - \delta)/(\alpha - \delta)\).

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound, which is proved here, to the references and to the tested name, of the correction of Proposition 33 and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) Each reference density is at most its Gaussian supremum, so \(q^{\mathrm{ref}}/D^{\mathrm{ref}}_v\) has mean at most one under the true clock, and Markov's inequality bounds the exclusion probability; only the true clock matters. (b) Given the references, the tested law is still the component's law, since the tested name is independent of the references; on the coverage event the denominator dominates it, and off it the capped score is at most \(c\). Integrating over the references and the component gives the bounds, as in Proposition 33. (c) A smaller set of clocks gives a smaller denominator. \(\square\)

The premise is strong: the references must share the tested name's clock, with no common return shock after conditioning on it, which a market factor violates. Name-specific changes of the clock's shape, and clocks outside the catalogue, are not covered. In the weekend design after Proposition 34, with 20 reference names on the trading clock, \(\delta = 0.01\), cap \(100\) and 2,000 panels per law, the references always reduced the catalogue to the calendar and trading clocks, and no null panel raised a false alarm. But against \(p = 3/2\), \(\kappa = 0.45\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\), the safe score had power \(12.3\%\), against \(14.9\%\) for the uncorrected score over the full catalogue. The excluded clocks barely constrained the tested path, so the narrowing gained little and the safety correction cost more. A gain would need wrong clocks that would otherwise absorb the evidence of a bubble.

Drift and volatility can also be allowed to change from day to day. Split the \(N\) evaluation steps into consecutive days, day \(d\) with \(n_d\) steps and a declared finite set of clock shapes, each day starting from the previous day's last price. Let \(P_d\) be the largest of the Gaussian suprema of Proposition 34 over day \(d\)'s shapes, \(P_{\mathrm{day}} = \prod_d P_d\), and \(E_{\mathrm{day}} = q/P_{\mathrm{day}}\), or \(0\) if \(q = 0\) or \(P_{\mathrm{day}} = \infty\), for a normalized forecast \(q\) of the evaluation prices chosen on the training record.

**Proposition 37 (daily drift and scale profiles).** Suppose that, given the training record and the earlier days, each day's evaluation prices follow a finite mixture of the Gaussian clock laws of Proposition 34 for that day, with drift, scale and weights that may depend on the history but are fixed before the day's returns. (a) \(P_{\mathrm{day}}\) is the supremum of the product of daily Gaussian densities over independent daily choices of shape, drift and scale; it is infinite exactly when some day's returns are proportional to its time steps, which always happens for a one-step day, and this set has measure zero when every day has at least two steps; rescaling a day's shapes leaves it unchanged. (b) \(E_{\mathrm{day}}\) has conditional mean at most one, \(P(E_{\mathrm{day}} > 1/\alpha) \le \alpha\), and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\) at a fixed horizon. (c) Grouping days to share parameters, or a single profile over the whole record with clocks whose daily restrictions are declared, gives a denominator at most \(P_{\mathrm{day}}\), so the same forecast scores at least as high. (d) With the calendar clock, let \(Q_d\) be day \(d\)'s weighted residual and \(Q\) the residual of one profile over the whole record, which also counts the spread of the daily return rates. Then
$$\log P_{\mathrm{day}} - \log P_{\mathrm{common}} = \frac{N}{2}\log\frac{Q}{N} - \sum_d \frac{n_d}{2}\log\frac{Q_d}{n_d} \ge 0,$$
with equality exactly when all days have the same return rate and the same residual variance per step.

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound with a supremum that separates over days, using the profile of Proposition 34, and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter only through an optional forecast. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) The product of daily densities has independent parameters for each day, so its supremum is the product of the daily suprema; a singular day can be driven to infinity with the others fixed. (b) Each mixture component of each day's law is at most that day's supremum, so the null density is at most \(P_{\mathrm{day}}\) at every history. (c) Fewer free parameters give a smaller supremum. (d) The pooled residual decomposes as \(\sum_d Q_d + \sum_d T_d(\hat a_d - \hat a)^2\), with \(T_d\) day \(d\)'s duration and \(\hat a_d\), \(\hat a\) the daily and pooled return rates; insert both profiles of Proposition 34. Equality needs every day's factor to attain its own maximum at the common optimum. \(\square\)

So the daily score can only lose to the single profile with the same forecast, by exactly that amount, while switches inside a day are excluded by the premise. In a simulation with days of seven equal steps, 60 training and 60 evaluation days, a training-selected constant-elasticity forecast and 2,000 records per law, a null whose scale switched between \(0.15\) and \(0.45\) with probability \(1/10\) a day and whose drift was drawn afresh each day gave the single-profile score a mean of about \(5 \times 10^7\) and a \(3.2\%\) rate of scores above 20, while the daily score stayed calibrated. But the resets cost about \(1.1\) in log terms per day even under a constant geometric Brownian motion (a median \(J\) of \(68\) over 60 days), so against \(p = 2\), \(\kappa = 0.45\) the rate of scores above 20 fell from \(26.8\%\) for the single profile to zero. A forecast that adapts to each day would be needed for power, and none is supplied.

The scale need not reset every day. Declare one clock shape for each day and a budget \(K\) on the number of days at which the variance scale changes, keeping a free drift for every day; the budget and the shapes are declared before the data and may not be fitted. For a run \(I\) of consecutive days, let \(P_I\) be the supremum of the product of the daily Gaussian densities over one common scale and a free drift for each day, and let \(P_{\mathrm{sw}}(K)\) be the supremum over paths with at most \(K\) scale changes, with \(E_{\mathrm{sw}} = q/P_{\mathrm{sw}}(K)\), or \(0\), for a normalized forecast \(q\) chosen on the training record.

**Proposition 38 (a budget on daily scale switches).** (a) With the daily statistics of Proposition 34 summed over the run, \(P_I\) has a closed form, attained at a scale that is the positive root of a quadratic and at each day's own profiled drift; it is infinite exactly when every day of the run has returns proportional to its time steps. (b) \(P_{\mathrm{sw}}(K)\) is the maximum, over partitions of the days into at most \(K + 1\) consecutive runs, of the product of the \(P_I\), computed exactly by a recursion on the last run; it is infinite exactly when an allowed partition has a singular run, and otherwise attained. (c) If, given the training record, the evaluation prices follow a mixture of the daily Gaussian laws over admissible paths, then \(E_{\mathrm{sw}}\) has conditional mean at most one, \(P(E_{\mathrm{sw}} > 1/\alpha) \le \alpha\), and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\); the same holds for a finite catalogue of full-record shapes. (d) \(P_{\mathrm{common}} \le P_{\mathrm{sw}}(0) \le \dots \le P_{\mathrm{sw}}(D - 1) = P_{\mathrm{day}}\), so the scores decrease with the budget.

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound to a finite maximum of profiles, using the daily statistics of Proposition 34 and the daily profile of Proposition 37, and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) Completing the square in each day's drift leaves the summed residual, and the remaining scalar profile in the scale is that of Proposition 34. (b) A path with at most \(K\) scale changes is constant in scale on at most \(K + 1\) runs; the supremum exchanges with the product, and grouping by the last run gives the recursion. (c) Each admissible path's density is at most \(P_{\mathrm{sw}}(K)\). (d) Larger parameter sets give larger suprema. \(\square\)

The recursion takes of order \((K + 1)D^2\) operations for \(D\) days. In the simulation after Proposition 37, a null with free daily drifts and exactly three scale switches gave the single-profile score a mean of about \(1.6 \times 10^7\) and a \(2.8\%\) rate of scores above 20, while the score with \(K = 3\) stayed calibrated. But the free daily drifts alone, at \(K = 0\), raised the log denominator by about \(31.5\) over 60 days, and the rate of scores above 20 against \(p = 2\), \(\kappa = 0.45\) fell from \(27.2\%\) for the single profile to \(0.35\%\), before any scale switch was allowed. The cost is in the free drifts, not in the switch budget; a common or budgeted drift with a switching scale would be the natural variant. The mixture is over whole parameter paths, not a switching rule driven by the returns, and switches inside a day are not covered.

Budgeting changes of drift and scale together cuts that cost, but not enough. Count a day boundary when the pair of drift and scale changes, a change of either or both counting once, and allow at most \(K\) such boundaries, so that drift no longer resets freely each day. For a run \(I\) of consecutive days, let \(P_I^{\mathrm{joint}}\) be the Gaussian supremum of Proposition 34 on the concatenated run, with one drift and one scale; let \(P_{\mathrm{joint}}(K)\) be the supremum of the daily product of Gaussian densities over pair paths with at most \(K\) changes, and \(E_{\mathrm{joint}} = q/P_{\mathrm{joint}}(K)\), or \(0\), for a normalized forecast \(q\) chosen on the training record.

**Proposition 39 (a budget on joint drift and scale changes).** (a) The concatenated run's residual is the sum of the within-day residuals and the spread of the daily return rates about the run's pooled rate; \(P_I^{\mathrm{joint}}\) is infinite exactly when all return rates in the run agree. (b) \(P_{\mathrm{joint}}(K)\) is the maximum, over partitions of the days into at most \(K + 1\) consecutive runs, of the product of the run profiles, computed exactly by a recursion on the last run; it is infinite exactly when an allowed partition has a singular run, and otherwise attained. (c) If, given the training record, the evaluation prices follow a mixture over pair paths with at most \(K\) changes of the corresponding Gaussian laws, then \(E_{\mathrm{joint}}\) has conditional mean at most one, \(P(E_{\mathrm{joint}} > 1/\alpha) \le \alpha\), and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\). (d) With \(P_{\mathrm{sw}}(K)\) the free-daily-drift denominator of Proposition 38, \(P_{\mathrm{common}} = P_{\mathrm{joint}}(0) \le P_{\mathrm{joint}}(K) \le P_{\mathrm{sw}}(K) \le P_{\mathrm{day}}\), with \(P_{\mathrm{joint}}(D - 1) = P_{\mathrm{day}}\), and the scores reverse this order. (e) With the calendar clock, on a fixed run of \(N_I\) steps with concatenated residual \(Q_I\) and summed within-day residuals \(Q_{\mathrm{within},I} > 0\), the cost of free daily drifts against a common drift is \((N_I/2)\log(Q_I/Q_{\mathrm{within},I}) \ge 0\), and these costs add over a fixed partition.

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound to a finite maximum of profiles, using the profile of Proposition 34 on concatenated runs and the daily profile of Proposition 37, and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) Pool the run's weighted statistics and complete the square in the drift. (b) A pair path with at most \(K\) changes is constant on at most \(K + 1\) runs, so its density is a product of run densities, and the supremum exchanges with the product; grouping by the last run gives the recursion. (c) Each admissible path's density is at most \(P_{\mathrm{joint}}(K)\). (d) Larger parameter sets give larger suprema. (e) Insert (a) into both profiles of Proposition 34. \(\square\)

In the simulation after Proposition 37, a null with a common drift and three scale switches gave the single-profile score a mean of about \(1.5 \times 10^3\) and a \(2.6\%\) rate of scores above 20, while the score with \(K = 3\) stayed calibrated. On the alternative, the median cost at \(K = 3\) was \(12.4\) in log terms, against about 31 for free daily drifts, but the rate of scores above 20 against \(p = 2\), \(\kappa = 0.45\) was still \(27.8\%\), \(1.2\%\) and \(0\) at \(K = 0\), \(1\) and \(3\). Daily drift redraws are not covered at a budget below the number of days.

Allowing each day its own volatility, and drift, thus keeps the scores calibrated under day-to-day regime switches where a single profile breaks, under declared block laws and budgets. But each free daily nuisance absorbs the evidence of a bubble: on these simulated hourly alternatives, power fell from about \(28\%\) to between \(0\) and \(1\%\). With a free daily scale, only changes of level within a day remain informative: in a constant-elasticity model the variance ratio between two prices in a day is their price ratio to the power \(2(p - 1)\), so a \(5\%\) range gives a ratio of about \(1.05\) for \(p = 3/2\) and \(1.10\) for \(p = 2\). Switches within a day, jumps and noise remain uncovered, and five-minute sampling and a predictive forecast are untested.

The per-name e-values give a list of bubbly names with a false-discovery guarantee. For a level \(0 < q < 1\), let \(k^*\) be the largest \(k \le m\) such that at least \(k\) names have \(E_i \ge m/(qk)\), or \(0\) if there is none, and report \(\mathcal{R} = \{i : E_i \ge m/(qk^*)\}\) when \(k^* > 0\), and nothing otherwise; equivalently, sort the \(E_i\) in decreasing order and report the first \(k^*\). Let \(N_0\) be the set of names without a bubble, \(m_0 = |N_0|\), and \(\mathrm{FDR} = E_P[|\mathcal{R} \cap N_0|/\max(|\mathcal{R}|, 1)]\).

**Proposition 40 (a list of bubbly names with false-discovery control).** In the setting of Proposition 27, with arbitrary dependence across names and bubbly and non-bubbly names mixed: (a) each \(E_i\) is positive and measurable, and \(E_P[E_i] \le 1\) for \(i \in N_0\); (b) \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m \le q\), and if every name is non-bubbly, \(P(\mathcal{R} \ne \emptyset) \le q\); (c) for any measurable random screen \(S\), setting \(E_i = 0\) for \(i \notin S\) and rerunning the rule with the original \(m\) gives \(\mathcal{R} \subseteq S\), \(\mathcal{R} = \emptyset\) when \(S = \emptyset\), and still \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\).

*Status.* An application of the e-BH theorem of Wang and Ramdas (`wang2020false`), stated as a hypothesis whose structure has a degenerate instance, together with the split-likelihood bound of Proposition 27, which is proved; not a new multiple-testing result. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* At a null name the true parameter lies in the null catalogue, so \(E_i \le T_i(\vartheta_i^*)\), whose mean is at most one; this is (a). The e-BH theorem turns null e-values into (b), under any dependence. Masking multiplies each null e-value by an indicator, which keeps its mean at most one, and (b) applies again with the original \(m\). \(\square\)

The guarantee concerns the expected proportion, not each realized list, and it is not a guarantee conditional on the screen; replacing \(m\) by the number of screened names at the same \(q\) does not preserve it. Inclusion uses the weak inequality \(E_i \ge m/(qk^*)\), whereas Proposition 27's confidence set excludes the null only when \(E_i > 1/a\), so a name can be reported while its closed confidence set at the random level \(a = qk^*/m\) still contains a null. For price data, the list inherits all the premises of Proposition 28 and its low power. It is also conservative, because each name's e-value is a minimum over its null catalogue and so lies below the likelihood ratio at the true parameter; how conservative, and how many bubbly names it finds at realistic sampling, is not quantified here.

Pooling the names into one global test does not help, at least in its simplest valid form. Model the \(m\) names' evaluation log-return vectors over \(n\) steps as independent Gaussian vectors with means \(a\Delta_j\) and covariances \(\Sigma\Delta_j\), for any drift vector \(a\) and any positive-definite \(\Sigma\); this allows any correlation across names, including an unknown factor structure. Let \(\hat a\) be the pooled return rate and \(S = n^{-1}\sum_j (R_j - \hat a\Delta_j)(R_j - \hat a\Delta_j)^{\mathsf T}/\Delta_j\) the weighted residual covariance, with residual correlation matrix \(C\). For the numerator \(q\), take the forecast that fits, on the training prices, one common power \(p > 1\) for all names, with name-specific scales and independent names, by the first maximizer over a declared finite catalogue. Let \(D_{\mathrm{pool}}\) be the supremum of the Gaussian price densities over \(a\) and \(\Sigma\), and set \(E_{\mathrm{pool}} = q/D_{\mathrm{pool}}\), or \(0\) if \(D_{\mathrm{pool}} = \infty\) or a price is not positive.

**Proposition 41 (a pooled Gaussian score).** Suppose that, given the training prices, the evaluation law of the panel is a mixture of these Gaussian laws, with latent parameters. (a) If \(S\) is positive definite, \(D_{\mathrm{pool}} = \prod_{i,j} y_{ij}^{-1}\,(2\pi e)^{-nm/2}(\det S)^{-n/2}\prod_j \Delta_j^{-m/2}\), attained at \((\hat a, S)\); otherwise \(D_{\mathrm{pool}} = \infty\). The rank of \(S\) is at most \(n - 1\), so \(D_{\mathrm{pool}} = \infty\) on every path when \(m \ge n\); when \(n \ge m + 1\), it is finite almost surely. (b) \(E_{\mathrm{pool}}\) is finite and measurable, has conditional mean at most one given the training prices, and \(P(E_{\mathrm{pool}} \ge 1/\alpha) \le \alpha\); it is identically zero when \(m \ge n\), and positive on positive full-rank records when \(n \ge m + 1\). (c) With \(D_{G,i}\) the Gaussian supremum of Proposition 30 for name \(i\) and \(q_i\) its forecast factor, \(D_{\mathrm{pool}} = (\prod_i D_{G,i})(\det C)^{-n/2}\) and \(E_{\mathrm{pool}} = (\prod_i q_i/D_{G,i})(\det C)^{n/2}\), with \(\det C \le 1\); for equal correlations \(\rho\), the log score loses \(-(n/2)[(m - 1)\log(1 - \rho) + \log(1 + (m - 1)\rho)]\).

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound, with the Gaussian maximum (`uhler2017gaussian`, Section 3) proved here; the constant-elasticity kernels of the forecast enter as for Proposition 29. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) After the drift is profiled out, \(\log\det\Sigma + \operatorname{tr}(\Sigma^{-1}S) \ge \log\det S + m\), by \(\log t \le t - 1\) on the eigenvalues of \(S^{1/2}\Sigma^{-1}S^{1/2}\); if \(S\) is singular, \(\Sigma = S + \varepsilon I\) makes the density unbounded as \(\varepsilon \to 0\). The residuals sum to zero, so \(S\) has rank at most \(n - 1\); when \(n \ge m + 1\), singular \(S\) forces one price to be determined by the others, a null set. (b) The denominator dominates every null component, and the forecast is normalized. (c) \(\det S = (\prod_i S_{ii})\det C\), and the diagonal subfamily attains \(\prod_i D_{G,i}\). \(\square\)

The factors \(q_i/D_{G,i}\) in (c) are algebraic comparisons, not the per-name scores of Proposition 30, and their product is not asserted to be valid. In practice naive pooling loses to per-name testing. In a simulation with five names, one year of daily training and one year of evaluation, \(\alpha = 0.1\) and 1,000 panels per law, the forecast chose one power from \(\{1.25, 1.5, 2\}\) and per-name scales from \(\{0.2, 0.3, 0.45\}\). Under independent and one-factor geometric Brownian motions, neither the pooled test nor a per-name Bonferroni rule raised a false alarm. Against independent constant-elasticity names with \(p = 3/2\) and \(\kappa = 0.45\), the pooled test rejected in \(32\%\) of panels against \(43\%\) for the per-name rule. With a common factor of correlation \(0.5\), it rejected in \(0.1\%\) against \(37\%\). Sampling noise in the correlations alone cost a median of \(4.6\), about \(m(m - 1)/4\), and the common factor about \(175\), which a forecast with independent names cannot recover. A useful pooled score would need a forecast that models the dependence across names; one is tried next.

A forecast that models the dependence recovers some of the loss, but not enough. Keep the null and its denominator, and replace the forecast with independent names by a Gaussian-copula forecast with the same constant-elasticity marginals (`masarotto2012copula`, its equations 3, 4 and 8). At each evaluation step, map each name's next price through its fitted conditional distribution function and then the normal quantile function, giving normal scores \(z_j\), and multiply the product of the marginal densities by the Gaussian copula density \(c_R(z) = (\det R)^{-1/2}\exp(-z^{\mathsf T}(R^{-1} - I)z/2)\). The correlation matrix \(R\) is the correlation of the training normal scores, shrunk towards the identity by a fixed weight \(\gamma \in (0, 1]\), or the identity if a training score is degenerate. Let \(q_{\mathrm{cop}}\) be the resulting whole-path forecast, \(q_{\mathrm{mix}} = w_0 q + (1 - w_0)q_{\mathrm{cop}}\) for a fixed \(w_0 \in (0, 1)\), and \(E_{\mathrm{cop}}\), \(E_{\mathrm{mix}}\) the corresponding pooled scores.

**Proposition 42 (a copula forecast for the pooled score).** In the setting of Proposition 41: (a) \(R\) depends on the training prices only, has unit diagonal and \(R \succeq \gamma I\); each step of the copula forecast is a probability density with the fitted constant-elasticity marginals, so \(q_{\mathrm{cop}}\) and \(q_{\mathrm{mix}}\) are normalized. (b) \(E_{\mathrm{cop}}\) and \(E_{\mathrm{mix}}\) have every conclusion of Proposition 41(b). (c) \(E_{\mathrm{mix}} = w_0E_{\mathrm{pool}} + (1 - w_0)E_{\mathrm{cop}} \ge w_0E_{\mathrm{pool}}\), and on positive full-rank records \(\log(E_{\mathrm{cop}}/E_{\mathrm{pool}}) = -(n/2)\log\det R - \tfrac12\operatorname{tr}[(R^{-1} - I)\sum_j z_j z_j^{\mathsf T}]\), which can have either sign; if \(R = I\), all three scores coincide.

*Status.* An application of the Gaussian copula construction, cited as a hypothesis on each step's normalization and marginals, and of Proposition 41; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) The distribution function of each marginal is continuous and strictly increasing, so the normal scores are finite; the copula construction gives each step mass one with the stated marginals, and a chain of normalized steps is normalized. (b) Proposition 41(b) holds for any normalized forecast. (c) The forecasts differ by the factor \(\prod_j c_R(z_j)\), whose logarithm is the displayed gain. \(\square\)

With \(w_0 = 1/2\), the mixture loses at most \(\log 2\) against the forecast with independent names. In the simulation after Proposition 41, with shrinkage weight \(0.1\), none of the scores raised a false alarm under the Gaussian nulls. With a common factor of correlation \(0.5\) and \(p = 3/2\), \(\kappa = 0.45\), the copula score rejected in \(19\%\) of panels and the mixture in \(18\%\), where the forecast with independent names had none, against \(39\%\) for per-name Bonferroni. With independent names, the copula score rejected in \(17\%\), because its correlation is fitted to noise, the mixture in \(29\%\) and the independent forecast in \(32\%\), against \(44\%\).

Pooling across names is thus valid but does not beat per-name testing. The per-name scores with the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 remain the method, and they also name the bubbly names.

What happens when the true law is not in the catalogue? The practical answer is discouraging: at high sampling frequency the available robustness bounds become nearly vacuous. Misspecification is measured by a total-variation envelope: for a name without a bubble, \(\varepsilon_i(u)\) bounds the distance between the actual conditional law of its evaluation prices given training prices \(u\) and that of some null catalogue member chosen measurably from \(u\).

**Proposition 43 (robustness of the catalogue scores).** In the setting of Proposition 27, but with the actual law \(P\) arbitrary, suppose a valid envelope \(\varepsilon_i\) exists for each name without a bubble. (a) The rule \(E_i > 1/\alpha_i\), and the band rule, reject such a name with probability at most \(\min(1, \alpha_i + E_P[\varepsilon_i])\); with a deterministic \(e_i < \alpha_i\) bounding \(\varepsilon_i\), the threshold \(1/(\alpha_i - e_i)\) has size at most \(\alpha_i\); and the probability that some such name is rejected is at most the sum of these bounds, without independence. (b) For caps \(c_i\) fixed in advance, \(E_i^{\mathrm{rob}} = \min(E_i, c_i)/(1 + c_i\varepsilon_i)\) has mean at most one at every name without a bubble, so the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 applied to these scores keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\) under any dependence; a strict rejection at level \(\alpha_i\) requires \(\varepsilon_i < \alpha_i\) and \(c_i > 1/(\alpha_i - \varepsilon_i)\). (c) For Gaussian log returns with drift \(b\) and volatility \(\sigma_*\) over \(n\) increments of total length \(\tau\), against a null with volatility \(\kappa_0\) and no drift, the Bhattacharyya affinity is

$$A_G = \Big(\frac{2\sigma_*\kappa_0}{\sigma_*^2 + \kappa_0^2}\Big)^{n/2}\exp\Big(-\frac{(b + (\kappa_0^2 - \sigma_*^2)/2)^2\,\tau}{4(\sigma_*^2 + \kappa_0^2)}\Big),$$

and \(1 - A_G \le \mathrm{TV} \le \sqrt{1 - A_G^2}\), so \(\sqrt{1 - A_G^2}\) is a valid envelope. With \(\sigma_* = \kappa_0\) it is at most \(|b|\sqrt{\tau}/(2\kappa_0)\) for every \(n\); with \(\sigma_* \ne \kappa_0\) and \(\tau\) fixed, \(A_G \to 0\) as \(n \to \infty\), against every member of a finite volatility grid that omits \(\sigma_*\). (d) The raw scores are not e-values off the catalogue: in a two-point example at distance \(e\), the raw score's mean is \(1 + e[1/(2p) - 1/(2(1 - p))]\), unbounded as \(p \to 0\), and an arbitrarily small distance can push a raw level-\(\alpha\) test above \(\alpha\).

*Status.* Applications of Markov's inequality, the comparison of bounded functions and, for (b), the e-BH theorem stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the split-likelihood bound and the Gaussian computations are proved. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) Disintegrate \(P\) over the training prices; under the reference law the score has conditional mean at most one, Markov's inequality gives \(\alpha_i\), and comparing the bounded indicator under the two conditional laws adds \(\varepsilon_i\). (b) For \(0 \le Y \le c\), \(\int Y\,dH \le \int Y\,dG + c\varepsilon\), which gives the clipped mean, and e-BH applies. (c) The affinity is the product of one-step Gaussian Bhattacharyya coefficients; the bounds are \(\min \le \sqrt{hg}\) and Cauchy–Schwarz. (d) is a direct computation. \(\square\)

Numerically, from (c): with drift \(0.05\), \(\kappa_0 = 0.3\) and \(\tau = 1\), the envelope is about \(0.083\), so a nominal \(5\%\) test has size at most about \(13\%\); with \(\sigma_* = 0.31\) against \(\kappa_0 = 0.30\) over one year it is about \(0.080\) with 12 observations, \(0.356\) with 252 and \(0.999987\) with 19,656. These evaluate the formulas; they are bounds, not the test's actual false-alarm rates. Robustness at realistic sampling therefore needs a richer null family, or measured false-alarm rates outside the catalogue, not these bounds.

The same bounds hold for the constant-elasticity catalogues with absorption of Proposition 29, whose densities have an atom at zero and so fall outside Proposition 43's positive-density setting, and they can be stated so that data failures are kept rather than dropped. Let each observation be a nonnegative real or a symbol \(\bot\) for a missing or unparseable record, call a training record valid when every entry is a nonnegative real, and set \(\bar E_i = E_i\) when the training record is valid and the evaluation record is a nonnegative price vector, and \(\bar E_i = 0\) otherwise. For a name without a bubble, let the envelope \(\varepsilon_i(u)\) bound, at almost every valid training record \(u\) under the actual law, the total-variation distance between the actual conditional law of the evaluation record and a catalogue null reference, both on the full observation space, so that actual mass on failed records counts against the envelope.
