**Proposition 44 (robustness with absorption and retained failures).** In the setting of Proposition 29, with the actual law \(P\) arbitrary and \(J_i\) the event of a valid training record: (a) the rule \(\bar E_i > 1/\alpha_i\), and the band rule, which abstains on invalid records, reject a name without a bubble with probability at most \(\min(1, \alpha_i P(J_i) + E_P[1_{J_i}\varepsilon_i])\); with \(\varepsilon_i \le e_i < \alpha_i\) on valid records, the threshold \(1/(\alpha_i - e_i)\) has size at most \(\alpha_i\); and the probability of some false rejection is at most the sum of these bounds, without independence. (b) For caps \(c_i\) fixed in advance and any measurable screen \(M_i\) with values in \([0, 1]\), which may use the whole panel, \(M_i\min(\bar E_i, c_i)/(1 + c_i\varepsilon_i)\) has mean at most \(P(J_i) \le 1\) at every name without a bubble, so the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 applied to these scores, with the original \(m\), keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\) under any dependence. (c) Both conclusions hold with \(\bar E_i\) replaced by any measurable computed score that is almost surely at most \(\bar E_i\). (d) A valid record with a zero price at the end of training or in evaluation has \(\bar E_i \le 1\), so such a name is never flagged.

*Status.* An application of the support-aware split-likelihood step (proved), Markov's inequality, the comparison of bounded functions and, for the false-discovery bound, the e-BH theorem, with the constant-elasticity and lognormal kernels as for Proposition 29. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* At every training record the selected density, restricted to the support of a null reference, has mass at most one, so \(\bar E_i\) has reference mean at most one; then argue as in Proposition 43, with the comparison made on the full observation space, so that events on failed records are included. On invalid training records \(\bar E_i = 0\), which gives the factor \(P(J_i)\). (d) is Proposition 29(d). \(\square\)

A default or a failed record is thus zero evidence: the name stays in the panel, \(m\) is unchanged, and there is no guarantee conditional on the records that succeeded. Rounding computed scores without a one-sided bound need not preserve the guarantee. How much the raw scores inflate off the catalogue may depend on the direction of the misspecification, but this has not been measured in a registered experiment.

The same total-variation comparison governs screens with empirically calibrated thresholds. Fix \(n \ge 1\) and \(\alpha \in (0, 1)\), and fix the scoring protocol, including window lengths, selection and any fitted score or simulated reference, before the calibration and evaluation scores are drawn. Larger scores are more extreme. Given \(n\) calibration scores \(c\), let \(k = \lceil (n + 1)(1 - \alpha) \rceil\) and let \(q(c)\) be the \(k\)-th smallest of them, with repetitions kept, or \(+\infty\) if \(k > n\); an evaluation score \(S\) is flagged when \(S > q(c)\). Let \(U\) be the whole recorded calibration data, including any selection information, on a standard Borel space, with actual law \(\mu\), calibration scores \(C(U)\), and \(K(u, \cdot)\) the actual conditional law of \(S\) given \(U = u\). The realized threshold's risk is \(R(u) = K(u, \{s : s > q(C(u))\})\). All laws are those of the windows actually selected and evaluated. Total variation is the supremum over events of the difference in probabilities.

**Proposition 45 (calibrated screens under post-selection score laws).** (a) If the joint law of \((C(U), S)\) is within total variation \(\varepsilon\) of an exchangeable law on \(n + 1\) real scores, the flag probability is at most \(\min(1, \alpha + \varepsilon)\). In particular \(\varepsilon = 0\) when the selected windows are exchangeable and every score is the same measurable function of its window, whatever their dependence. (b) If instead, for a fixed score law \(\nu\), the law of \(C(U)\) is within total variation \(\eta\) of \(\nu^{n}\), and \(K(u, \cdot)\) is within \(\rho\) of \(\nu\) for \(\mu\)-almost every \(u\), the flag probability is at most \(\min(1, \alpha + \eta + \rho)\). (c) Under the premises of (b), let \(r = \lfloor \alpha(n + 1) - 1 \rfloor = n - k\) and, for \(E, \delta \in (0, 1)\), \(B = \sum_{j=0}^{r} \binom{n}{j} E^{j}(1 - E)^{n - j}\), with \(B = 0\) if \(r = -1\). If \(B \le \delta\), then \(\mu\{R > E + \rho\} \le \min(1, \delta + \eta)\), and with \(b = \min(1, E + \rho)\) and \(d = \min(1, \delta + \eta)\) the flag probability is at most \(b + (1 - b)d\). (d) Selection on past information alone does not supply these premises. Take \(n = 19\) and \(\alpha = 1/20\), and independent blocks, each with a fair bit observed before its window and a binary score equal to that bit with probability \(0.99\); retain calibration blocks whose bit is zero. An independent unselected evaluation block is flagged with probability \((1/2)(0.99)^{19} > 2/5\), and one retained by the same rule with probability \((1/100)(0.99)^{19} < 1/20\). The premises of (b) hold in the first design with \(\nu\) Bernoulli\((1/100)\), \(\eta = 0\) and \(\rho = 49/100\), and in the second with both budgets zero.

*Status.* An application of the exchangeable quantile lemma of conformal prediction (`tibshirani2019conformal`, Lemma 1) and the training-conditional guarantee of `vovk2013conditional` (Theorem 1(a)), each at the reference law used, with the total-variation comparison of Proposition 43. Machine-checked, conditional on those two cited results, which enter as hypothesis structures with only degenerate instances (Appendix A).

*Proof.* The \(k\)-th order statistic is a minimum of maxima of coordinates, so the flag event is Borel, and \(R\) is measurable with mean equal to the flag probability. (a) Under the exchangeable reference law the cited lemma, whose rank, tie and infinity conventions are those above, bounds the flag probability by \(\alpha\); the comparison of Proposition 43, applied to this one event, transports the bound. A common measurable map sends an exchangeable law of windows to an exchangeable law of scores. (b) The kernel premise moves the flag probability by at most \(\rho\) when \(K(u, \cdot)\) is replaced by \(\nu\). What remains is the integral of the bounded function \(c \mapsto \nu\{s > q(c)\}\) against the law of \(C(U)\), which moves by at most \(\eta\) when that law is replaced by \(\nu^n\); the result is the flag probability under the independent, hence exchangeable, law \(\nu^{n+1}\), at most \(\alpha\) by (a). (c) Let \(G\) be the set of \(c\) with \(\nu\{s > q(c)\} > E\). The cited guarantee gives \(\nu^n(G) \le \delta\) when \(B \le \delta\), so \(\mu\{C(U) \in G\} \le \min(1, \delta + \eta)\); and on the full-measure set of the kernel premise, \(R(u) \le \nu\{s > q(C(u))\} + \rho\), so \(\{R > E + \rho\}\) lies in \(\{C(U) \in G\}\) up to a null set. Splitting the mean of \(R\) on \(\{R \le b\}\) gives the last bound. (d) A retained block scores one with probability \(0.01\), independently. With \(n = 19\) and \(\alpha = 1/20\), \(k = 19\) and the threshold is the maximum, so a binary evaluation score is flagged exactly when all calibration scores are zero and it is one; the stated probabilities and total variations follow by direct computation. \(\square\)

The budgets are not estimated here; neither (b) nor (c) holds automatically for real windows. Equal lengths, no overlap, selection on the past and similar marginal laws do not supply them, as (d) shows: the violated premise is a comparable law of the evaluation score after selection, not the rank rule. The result gives no guarantee conditional on every calibration sample, none uniform over levels chosen afterwards, and no power, false-discovery, repeated-launch or anytime guarantee. Its null is a law of observations; reading it as the absence of a bubble needs a pricing interpretation. Sections 5.9 to 6.13 use this rank rule but establish none of its premises for real windows, and the interpolated, tie-inclusive thresholds of Section 6.5 are not instances of it.

Jumps are a misspecification that the Gaussian null of the free-drift score of Proposition 30 does not absorb. Contamination is a known danger for likelihood-ratio inference (`park2023robust`, Section 3.1, Example 3); here it can be budgeted. For a name without a bubble, suppose that at almost every training record \(u\) under the actual law, its evaluation law is \((1 - p)G + pJ\) for some \(p \le \rho_i(u)\), with \(G\) one of the Gaussian null laws of Proposition 30 and \(J\) an arbitrary law.

**Proposition 46 (jump-contamination budgets).** In the setting of Proposition 30, with this mixture premise at each name without a bubble: (a) the total-variation distance from \(G\) is at most \(\rho_i\), and \(E_i^{\mathrm{drift}} > 1/\alpha_i\) rejects with conditional probability at most \(\alpha_i + (1 - \alpha_i)\rho_i\), and unconditionally at most \(\alpha_i + (1 - \alpha_i)E[\rho_i]\); with \(\rho_i \le e_i < \alpha_i\), the threshold \((1 - e_i)/(\alpha_i - e_i)\) has size at most \(\alpha_i\). (b) For a cap \(c_i \ge 1\), \(\min(E_i^{\mathrm{drift}}, c_i)/(1 + (c_i - 1)\rho_i)\) has conditional mean at most one, so the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 applied to these scores, or to any measurable scores below them, including zero-masked ones with the original \(m\), keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\) under any dependence; such a score can reject at level \(\alpha_i\) only if \(\rho_i < \alpha_i\) and \(c_i > (1 - \rho_i)/(\alpha_i - \rho_i)\). (c) The premise holds when the log returns are Gaussian with drift and scale as in Proposition 30 plus jump terms independent of them, with \(p\) the probability of any jump; then a supplied no-jump probability \(e^{-\lambda\tau}\) gives \(\rho_i = 1 - e^{-\lambda\tau}\). (d) If the jumps may depend on the Gaussian part, the total-variation distance is still at most the probability of a jump, so the bounds of Proposition 43 hold, with normalization \(1 + c_i\rho_i\).

*Status.* An application of linearity of integration, Markov's inequality, the comparison of bounded functions and, for the false-discovery bound, the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the reference moment of the free-drift score is proved at every training record, and the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) Under \(G\) the score has mean at most one, so the rejection event has \(G\)-probability at most \(\alpha_i\) and \(J\)-probability at most one. (b) The capped score has \(G\)-mean at most one and is at most \(c_i\) under \(J\). (c) On the event of no jump the prices are those of the Gaussian law, by induction over the returns. (d) The two price maps agree when there is no jump. \(\square\)

The normalization \(1 + (c_i - 1)\rho_i\) needs the mixture form, which can fail for dependent jumps: for a reference law with mass \(1 - 1/c\) on a point where the score is zero and \(1/c\) on a point where it is \(c\), moving mass \(\rho\) to the second point gives a clipped mean of \(1 + c\rho\). However finely the window is sampled, \(\rho_i\) is the probability of any jump in it. In practice the budget is uninformative. In one simulation, of daily prices over two years with the first year for training, the catalogue of Proposition 29 with powers \(\{1/2, 1, 3/2\}\) and scales \(\{0.2, 0.3, 0.45\}\), and 20,000 paths per law, the truth was bubble-free geometric Brownian motion with drift \(0.05\) and volatility \(0.3\) plus independent compound-Poisson log jumps. Without jumps the free-drift score had mean \(0.38\); with jumps at rates from one every two years to ten a year, its mean ranged from about \(7\) to \(10^{12}\), so the raw scores must not enter the discovery list when jumps are possible. Their actual false-alarm rates at \(\alpha = 0.1\) stayed at or below \(1.6\%\), so the failure is of the e-value property, not evidence of large inflation of the per-name size. But even one jump every two years gives \(\rho \approx 0.39\) over a one-year window, above any usual \(\alpha\), so the clipped test can never reject, and its mean was about \(0.01\) to \(0.02\). The guarantee is valid but powerless over realistic windows; it becomes useful only when \(\lambda\tau\) is much smaller than \(\alpha\). A null family that models jumps is needed; that of Proposition 52 is valid but powerless.

Volatility that is random but constant within the evaluation window costs nothing, and volatility that changes within it can be budgeted. Let \(E_i\) be the free-drift score of Proposition 30 or the rectangle score of Proposition 32, chosen in advance. Suppose that, at almost every training record \(u\) under the actual law, a bubble-free name's evaluation law is a mixture, over a latent variable \(z\) with law \(\nu_i(u, dz)\), of laws with independent Gaussian log returns whose drift \(b_j(u, z)\) and volatility \(\sigma_j(u, z) > 0\) may vary across the steps \(j\), and compare each component with a constant reference law \((b_{\mathrm{ref}}, \kappa)(u, z)\) in the score's null.

**Proposition 47 (volatility-path budgets).** In this setting: (a) each component is within total variation \(\varepsilon = \sqrt{1 - A^2}\) of its reference, with

$$A = \prod_j \sqrt{\frac{2\sigma_j\kappa}{\sigma_j^2 + \kappa^2}}\;\exp\Big(-\sum_j \frac{\Delta_j\,[\,b_j - b_{\mathrm{ref}} + (\kappa^2 - \sigma_j^2)/2\,]^2}{4(\sigma_j^2 + \kappa^2)}\Big),$$

on prices as well as log returns, and \(\varepsilon = 0\) when the component equals its reference. (b) With \(\bar\varepsilon_i(u) = \int \varepsilon\,\nu_i(u, dz)\), the rule \(E_i > 1/\alpha_i\) has size at most \(\alpha_i + \bar\varepsilon_i\), conditionally and unconditionally; with \(\bar\varepsilon_i \le e_i < \alpha_i\), the threshold \(1/(\alpha_i - e_i)\) has size at most \(\alpha_i\); \(\min(E_i, c_i)/(1 + c_i r_i)\), for any measurable budget \(r_i \ge \bar\varepsilon_i\), has mean at most one, so the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\) for these scores under any dependence. If every component equals its reference, as when the volatility is random but constant in the window, the raw \(E_i\) has null mean at most one. (c) If all volatilities lie in \([s_{\min}, s_{\max}]\), \(|\log(\sigma_j/\kappa)| \le h\) and \(|b_j - b_{\mathrm{ref}}| \le B\), then \(\bar\varepsilon_i \le \sqrt{1 - e^{-L}}\) with \(L = n\log\cosh h + \tau[B/(2s_{\min}) + s_{\max}\sinh(h)/2]^2\).

*Status.* An application of the Gaussian affinity of Proposition 43(c), the comparison of bounded functions and the split-likelihood moment, integrated over the latent law, and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) The affinity is the product of one-step Gaussian coefficients, and on prices each step's coefficient does not depend on the current price. (b) The score has mean at most one under each reference, since its denominator dominates the reference density; compare bounded functions under each component and integrate over \(z\). (c) Write \(2\sigma\kappa/(\sigma^2 + \kappa^2) = 1/\cosh\log(\sigma/\kappa)\) and \((\sigma^2 - \kappa^2)/2 = \sigma\kappa\sinh\log(\sigma/\kappa)\). \(\square\)

The budget of the realized component cannot be used when that component is unobserved. The bound in (c) tends to one as sampling gets finer at any fixed \(h > 0\); it is sufficient, not evidence of failure. In the jump simulation's design, with 10,000 paths per law and \(\alpha = 0.1\), a latent even mixture of geometric Brownian motions with volatilities \(0.25\) and \(0.40\) gave the free-drift score a null mean of \(0.14\) and a false-alarm rate of \(0.03\%\), as the zero-budget case requires. But a switch from \(0.25\) to \(0.40\) halfway through the evaluation window gave a null mean of \(13\) and a false-alarm rate of \(2.8\%\), with budget one, and even a smooth seasonal volatility \(0.30 \pm 0.05\sin(2\pi t)\) gave a budget of \(0.91\) at daily sampling. So there is still no informative guarantee for genuine within-window variation. Volatility correlated with returns, as in Heston-type models with leverage, is outside this representation: in one such model, with correlation \(-0.7\), the score had null mean \(0.28\) and no false alarms, but this is a measurement, not a guarantee.

A single volatility switch can be put into the null, and it restores validity but leaves almost no power on daily data. In a second simulation of the same design, a switch from \(0.25\) to \(0.40\) in the middle of the evaluation window gave the free-drift score a null mean of \(17\), \(263\) for a switch a few days later, and \(83\) when the switch date was random; such heavy-tailed means vary between runs. Declare in advance a set \(K_i\) of cut points \(c\), each leaving at least two evaluation steps on either side, and add to the null every law whose log returns are independent Gaussian with drift \(b_L\) and volatility \(\kappa_L\) up to step \(c\) and \(b_R\), \(\kappa_R\) after it. Let \(D_{\mathrm{sw}}\) be the supremum of these densities over the cuts and parameters, and set \(E_i^{\mathrm{sw}} = \hat G_i/\max(G_{i,0}, D_{\mathrm{sw}})\), or \(0\) if \(\hat G_i = 0\) or the denominator is infinite.

**Proposition 48 (a single-switch null).** In the setting of Proposition 30, suppose that, given its training prices, each name without a bubble has an evaluation law that is a mixture of catalogue null laws and such switch laws, with the cut and parameters latent and possibly dependent on the training prices. (a) On positive paths, \(D_{\mathrm{sw}}\) is the maximum over \(c \in K_i\) of the product of the closed-form Gaussian maxima of Proposition 30(a) on the two segments; it is finite exactly when every permitted segment has positive residual variation, and the set where it is infinite has probability zero under every such law. A cut leaving a single step makes it infinite on every positive path. (b) The score is finite and measurable on every nonnegative array and has conditional mean at most one at every name without a bubble, so the per-name tests, p-values and the union bound hold, and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 has \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\), also after a zero-masked screen with the original \(m\). (c) On every path \(E_i^{\mathrm{sw}} \le E_i^{\mathrm{drift}} \le E_i\); enlarging \(K_i\) decreases the score; it is not identically zero, and absorbed names are never flagged.

*Status.* An application of the composite-null split-likelihood bound (`wasserman2019universal`), which is proved here, the Gaussian profile of Proposition 30 on each segment and the mixture integration of Proposition 47, and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) Given the cut, the density is a product over the two segments with separate parameters, so its supremum is the product of the segment suprema. A segment with zero residual variation has its returns on a line through the origin, a set of measure zero when it has at least two steps; a one-step segment always has zero residual. (b) The denominator dominates every null component, and the argument of Proposition 47 with zero budget integrates over the latent law. (c) Equal segment parameters recover the Gaussian laws of Proposition 30. \(\square\)

The guarantee repairs the within-window failure after Proposition 47, but on every path the score is at most the free-drift score, so it cannot gain power. In the same simulation, with cuts every month or at every permitted step, the null means fell below \(0.001\), with no false alarms. But power against \(p = 3/2\) fell from \(6\%\) to \(0\%\) at \(\kappa = 0.3\), and from \(20\%\) to at most \(0.3\%\) at \(\kappa = 0.45\). A Gaussian law with one switch fits the level-dependent volatility of a constant-elasticity path about as well as the alternative does, so on daily data a change of volatility over time and a dependence of volatility on the price level are nearly confounded. This echoes, at the level of finite samples and of a measured design, the mechanism of Proposition 20, where stochastic volatility makes one physical law compatible with both labels; it is not a proof that the two are unidentified. A usable test would need a restriction that separates the two, or more informative data. Repeated switches, switches driven by the returns themselves, general stochastic volatility, jumps and rounding are not covered.

The confounding comes from discrete sampling, not from the coefficient. Fix \(T > 0\), \(s_0 > 0\) and \(\beta \in \mathbb{R}\), and suppose the log price \(X\), with \(X_0 = 0\), has on \([0, T]\) the continuous representation \(X_t = M_t + B_t\), \(M_t = \int_0^t \sqrt{v_u}\,dW_u\), \(v_t = \exp(a(t) + \beta X_t)\), with \(B\) continuous, adapted and of finite variation, and \(a\) an unknown deterministic continuous function of total variation at most \(V\); existence of such a solution is a premise. The coefficient \(\sigma(t, s) = s\exp((a(t) + \beta\log(s/s_0))/2)\) then combines a time effect with a level dependence of elasticity \(1 + \beta/2\). Write \(Q(t) = [X]_t\), \(L = \log v\) and \([L, X]_T = ([L + X]_T - [L - X]_T)/4\). On a grid, given arrays \(x_j\), \(a_j\) with \(\sum_j|a_j - a_{j-1}| \le V\), errors \(|e_j| \le \varepsilon\) and \(\hat l_j = a_j + \beta x_j + e_j\), write \(d_j = x_j - x_{j-1}\), \(w = \max_j|d_j|\), \(H = |d_1| + |d_n| + \sum_{j<n}|d_j - d_{j+1}|\), and, when \(\sum_j d_j^2 > 0\), \(\hat\beta = \sum_j(\hat l_j - \hat l_{j-1})d_j/\sum_j d_j^2\) and \(r = (Vw + \varepsilon H)/\sum_j d_j^2\).

**Proposition 49 (level dependence is identified from a continuous record).** (a) Almost surely, simultaneously for \(t \le T\): \(Q(t) = \int_0^t v_u\,du\), \(Q(T) > 0\), \(Q\) has (one-sided at the ends) derivative \(v\), so \(L = \log Q'\), and \([L, X]_T = \beta Q(T)\); hence \(\beta = [\log Q', X]_T/Q(T)\), whatever the time effect. A Lipschitz \(a\) with constant \(\ell\) has total variation at most \(\ell T\). (b) \(|\hat\beta - \beta| \le r\), and for each nonconstant array \(x\) and each \(\beta\) the bound is attained by some admissible \(a\) and \(e\). (c) On the accuracy event, \(\beta \le 0\) implies \(\hat\beta \le r\) and \(\beta > 2r\) implies \(\hat\beta > r\); for measurable arrays whose accuracy event has probability at least \(1 - \delta\) under every null with \(\beta \le 0\), rejecting when \(\hat\beta > r\) has size at most \(\delta\), and its power at an alternative is at least \(1 - \delta - P(\sum_j d_j^2 = 0 \text{ or } \beta \le 2r)\).

*Status.* Conditional on the quadratic variation of a Brownian stochastic integral plus a continuous finite-variation term (`etheridge2018continuous`, Proposition 6.25), stated as a hypothesis with a vacuous instance; that a continuous finite-variation process alone has zero quadratic variation is proved. Part (a) is a statement about the supplied representation, not a test. Parts (b) and (c) are deterministic or need only the supplied accuracy event. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) Finite variation adds nothing to quadratic variation, so \([X]_t = \int_0^t v\), whose derivative is \(v > 0\); and \(L \pm X = a + (\beta \pm 1)(M + B)\), whose brackets are \((\beta \pm 1)^2 Q(T)\) because \(a\) is deterministic, continuous and of finite variation; polarizing gives \(\beta Q(T)\). (b) Summation by parts gives \(\sum_j(\hat l_j - \hat l_{j-1})d_j - \beta\sum_j d_j^2 = \sum_j(a_j - a_{j-1})d_j + \sum_j e_jc_j\) with \(\sum_j|c_j| = H\); choosing \(a\) and \(e\) with the signs of \(d\) and \(c\) attains the bound. (c) follows from (b). \(\square\)

It is an identification statement, not a test: no estimator of the log variance from discrete prices, no event on which its error is controlled, no minimax rate and no table of required sampling frequencies is supplied. In a simulation with \(\beta = 1.5\), a time effect \(a(t) = \log 0.09 + 0.5\sin 2\pi t\) of total variation 2 on \([0, 1]\), and 200 paths, the estimate with exact log variance recovered \(\beta\) at every grid, and the median guaranteed radius \(r\) shrank from \(1.36\) to \(0.24\) as the number of increments grew from 256 to 16,384. With a log-variance error of \(0.05\), the radius grew from \(4.3\) to \(23.4\), because the error term grows with the number of increments while \(\sum_j d_j^2\) does not. The bound gives a usable test only with a log-variance estimator whose error falls faster than \(n^{-1/2}\).

A time effect alone can also be made the null, with a budget on its variation. Let \(\Delta_j > 0\) be deterministic durations, \(R_j\) the \(n\) evaluation log returns, and \(V \ge 0\) a budget declared in advance, and let \(Z_V\) be the set of \(z \in \mathbb{R}^n\) with \(\sum_{j<n}|z_{j+1} - z_j| \le V\). The null is that, for some \(z \in Z_V\), the returns are independent with \(R_j \sim N(-\Delta_j e^{z_j}/2,\ \Delta_j e^{z_j})\), or a mixture of such laws: zero physical drift and a log variance that changes over time only. With \(A_j = R_j^2/(2\Delta_j)\), \(B_j = \Delta_j/8\) and \(f_j(s) = -s/2 - A_je^{-s} - B_je^{s}\), the log likelihood is \(\ell_R(z) = c_R + \sum_j f_j(z_j)\) for a constant \(c_R\); let \(P_V = \sup_{z \in Z_V} e^{\ell_R(z)}\). For \(u \in \mathbb{R}^{n-1}\) put \(c_1 = -u_1\), \(c_j = u_{j-1} - u_j\) for \(1 < j < n\), \(c_n = u_{n-1}\) (and \(c = 0\) if \(n = 1\)), \(h_j(c_j) = \sup_s(f_j(s) - c_js)\) and \(U_V(u) = c_R + V\max_j|u_j| + \sum_j h_j(c_j)\). For a normalized density \(q\) of the returns fixed before they are observed, set \(E_V = q/P_V\) and \(E_{\mathrm{cert}} = q\,e^{-U_V(u)}\), each zero where its denominator is infinite.

**Proposition 50 (a variation budget on a time-only variance).** (a) The null is the grid law of a driftless geometric Brownian motion run on a deterministic clock \(A(t) = \int_0^t e^{a(s)}\,ds\), with \(z_j\) the log of the clock's average rate over step \(j\): every continuous \(a\) of total variation at most \(V\) gives a \(z \in Z_V\), and every \(z \in Z_V\) arises from a clock rate constant between observations. (b) \(P_V\) is Borel and positive; if some return is nonzero it is finite with a unique maximizer, and if all returns are zero it is infinite; it is nondecreasing in \(V\), and at \(V = 0\) it is the common-scale profile, attained at \(e^{z} = 2\sum_jA_j/(n/2 + \sqrt{n^2/4 + 4\sum_jA_j\sum_jB_j})\). (c) Each \(h_j\) is explicit, finite or \(+\infty\), and for every feasible \(z\) and every \(u\), \(\ell_R(z) \le \log P_V \le U_V(u)\), with the gap \(U_V(u) - \ell_R(z)\) a sum of nonnegative terms; a finite certificate exists whenever some return is nonzero, and at \(V = 0\) one with zero gap. (d) Under every null, \(E[E_V] \le 1\) and \(P(E_V > 1/\alpha) \le \alpha\), and the same holds for \(E_{\mathrm{cert}} \le E_V\), for any choice of \(u\), and for any score that is zero on records without a certificate; a feasible pair with gap at most \(\eta\) gives \(e^{-\eta}E_V \le E_{\mathrm{cert}}\).

*Status.* An application of the composite-null split-likelihood bound (`wasserman2019universal`), which is proved here; the grid law of the time-changed price is derived from the independent Gaussian increments of Brownian motion in Mathlib. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) The clock's increment over step \(j\) is \(\Delta_j\) times the mean of \(e^{a}\) there, a value of \(e^a\) at an intermediate point, so consecutive \(z_j\) differ by at most the variation of \(a\) between those points; conversely a step rate realizes any \(z\). (b) Each \(f_j\) is strictly concave and tends to \(-\infty\) at both ends when \(A_j > 0\), and \(Z_V\) bounds every \(z_j - z_1\), so an upper level set is compact. (c) Fenchel–Young: \(\sum_j f_j(z_j) = \sum_j(f_j(z_j) - c_jz_j) + u \cdot Dz\), with \(Dz\) the vector of differences, and \(u \cdot Dz \le V\max_j|u_j|\) on \(Z_V\); each difference to the bound is a nonnegative term. (d) The denominator dominates every null density; Markov's inequality. \(\square\)

A numerical maximizer gives only a lower bound on \(P_V\), and so a score that may be too large; the certificate gives the upper bound the test needs. The scalar supremum is attained at \(e^s = 2A_j/(\sqrt{d^2 + 4A_jB_j} + d)\) with \(d = 1/2 + c_j\); the equivalent textbook form \((\sqrt{d^2 + 4A_jB_j} - d)/(2B_j)\) cancels catastrophically when \(d\) is large, and a naive evaluator then fails rather than erring conservatively. In a check with 40 daily returns, a constant-elasticity forecast with \(p = 2\) and \(\kappa = 0.45\), and 400 paths per law, the certificate gaps were at most about \(3 \times 10^{-7}\) in median. The certified score's mean stayed well below one under geometric Brownian motion (\(0.051\), \(0.017\) and \(0.002\) at \(V = 0\), \(0.5\) and \(2\)) and under a sinusoidal schedule of variation about 0.5, but it never exceeded 20 against the constant-elasticity alternative, even at \(V = 0\) (mean score \(1.18\)); the budget shrinks the evidence further. Rates, power, validity at every time and certified floating-point evaluation are not claimed, and a rejection does not identify a bubble: the null is a deterministic, time-only variance schedule with zero drift.

Without a floor on the volatility scale, no finite grid separates elasticities uniformly. Take, with \(P = Q\) and start \(S_0 = 1\), a fixed grid of durations \(\Delta_1, \dots, \Delta_n\) and two of the price laws of Proposition 28 at scale \(\kappa > 0\): \(P_{1,\kappa}\), geometric Brownian motion \(dS = \kappa S\,dW\), a martingale, and \(P_{2,\kappa}\), \(dS = \kappa S^2\,dW\), the reciprocal of a scaled three-dimensional Bessel process and a strict local martingale. Their elasticities are 1 and 2 and neither has a time effect. Write the prices in normalized coordinates, \(\log(S_j/S_{j-1}) = \kappa\sqrt{\Delta_j}\,z_j - \kappa^2\Delta_j/2\) with \(z\) standard normal under \(P_{1,\kappa}\), let \(L_\kappa(z)\) be the likelihood ratio of \(P_{2,\kappa}\) to \(P_{1,\kappa}\) in these coordinates, an explicit product over the steps, and let \(d(\kappa) = \int|L_\kappa - 1|\,d\Gamma_n\), with \(\Gamma_n\) the standard normal law on \(\mathbb{R}^n\).

**Proposition 51 (no uniform separation without a scale floor).** (a) \(L_\kappa\) is positive with mean one, \(L_\kappa(z) \to 1\) for every \(z\) and \(d(\kappa) \to 0\) as \(\kappa \to 0\); for every measurable test \(\psi\) with values in \([0, 1]\), even one that depends on \(\kappa\), \(|E_{2,\kappa}\psi - E_{1,\kappa}\psi| \le d(\kappa)\). (b) If \(E_{1,\kappa}\psi \le \alpha\) for every \(\kappa > 0\), then for every \(\varepsilon > 0\) some \(\kappa\) has \(E_{2,\kappa}\psi \le \alpha + \varepsilon\); the best worst-case power over scales is \(\alpha\), attained by rejecting at random with probability \(\alpha\). (c) For any sequence of finite grids and tests of size at most \(\alpha\) at every scale, there are scales \(\kappa_n < 1/n\) at which the power on the \(n\)-th grid is at most \(\alpha + 1/n\), so no sequence has worst-case power tending to one.

*Status.* Conditional on the reciprocal-Bessel transition density and its normalization, as in Proposition 28, and on the varying-dominator convergence theorem (`etheridge2018continuous`), each stated as a hypothesis with a vacuous instance; the coordinate identity for the geometric Brownian law is proved from Mathlib's Gaussian law and change of variables. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) Each factor of \(L_\kappa\) is the ratio of the two one-step kernels at the coordinates, and tends to one because the scaled difference quotient of \(1/S\) tends to \(-z_j\); \(L_\kappa\) is nonnegative with mean one, so pointwise convergence gives convergence in \(L^1\) (Scheffé), and \(|E_2\psi - E_1\psi| = |\int\psi(L_\kappa - 1)\,d\Gamma_n|\). (b) and (c) follow, the latter grid by grid. \(\square\)

The sharp bound in (a) is the total variation \(d(\kappa)/2\), since \(\int(L_\kappa - 1)\,d\Gamma_n = 0\). The same obstruction holds for any null and alternative classes containing these families, so for the time-and-level models of Propositions 49 and 50 with an unrestricted scale and any time budget. This is the pattern of the nonexistence results of Bahadur and Savage (`bahadur1956nonexistence`, which we have not read against this problem), alternatives that approach the null in total variation, made explicit for these kernels. Both laws depend on the grid only through \(\kappa^2\Delta_j\), so a small scale is the same as a short record: from a fixed start, a record of small integrated variance \(\kappa^2T\) cannot separate the elasticities, and a scale floor is a floor on \(\kappa^2T\). Numerically, with one step of unit length the total variation is \(0.381\) at \(\kappa = 1\) and \(0.075\) at \(\kappa = 0.2\), nearly linear in \(\kappa\); at \(\kappa = 0.5\) over a unit horizon, refining from 1 to 5, 25 and 125 steps raises the \(L^1\) distance from \(0.37\) to \(0.68\), \(1.20\) and \(1.35\), toward the continuous-record identification of Proposition 49. The statement gives no rate, does not say that the two families overlap, and says nothing with a positive scale floor, a continuous record or \(P \ne Q\); the bubble's size also vanishes as \(\kappa \to 0\), so it does not separate bubble values either.

Jumps can be put into the null in the same way as the switch of Proposition 48, with the same verdict: valid, and without power. In a further run of the daily design after Proposition 46, with volatility \(0.3\) and normal log jumps of mean \(-0.05\) and standard deviation \(0.1\) at one or five a year, the free-drift score had null means \(341\) and \(561\). Declare in advance a diffusion-scale floor \(\sigma_{\min} > 0\) and an intensity cap \(\Lambda \in [0, \infty]\), and add to the null every law whose log returns over each step are, independently, a Poisson mixture of normal laws, as in Merton's jump model (`merton1976jumps`): log drift \(a\), diffusion scale \(\sigma \ge \sigma_{\min}\), intensity \(\lambda \le \Lambda\), and normal jump sizes. On positive paths put \(M = \prod_j 1/(y_j\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{2\pi\Delta_j})\), \(\rho = 1 - e^{-\Lambda\tau}\) (one if \(\Lambda = \infty\)) and \(U = (1 - \rho)\min(D_G, M) + \rho M\), and let \(E_i^J\) divide the fitted likelihood by the largest of \(G_{i,0}\), \(D_G\) and \(U\).

**Proposition 52 (a Poisson-jump envelope).** In the setting of Proposition 30, with \(n \ge 2\) evaluation steps: (a) every such jump law has density at most \(U\) on every array; \(U\) is finite and measurable, increases with the cap and decreases with the floor, is at most \(D_G\) when \(\Lambda = 0\), and equals \(M\) on positive paths when \(\Lambda = \infty\). (b) Suppose that, given its training prices, each name without a bubble has an evaluation law that is a mixture of catalogue null laws, Gaussian laws and such jump laws. Then the score is finite almost surely, has conditional mean at most one, \(E_i^J > 1/\alpha_i\) has size at most \(\alpha_i\), and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\) under any dependence. (c) \(E_i^J \le E_i^{\mathrm{drift}} \le E_i\); raising the cap or lowering the floor lowers the score; with \(\Lambda = 0\) it is the free-drift score; and absorbed names are never flagged. (d) Truncating the Poisson series at \(K\) terms changes one step's density by at most \(P(\mathrm{Poisson}(\lambda\Delta) > K)/(\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{2\pi\Delta})\).

*Status.* An application of the composite-null split-likelihood bound (`wasserman2019universal`) with an upper bound on the null maximum, which is proved here, and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. The domination holds for the series formula itself, without its interpretation as Merton's law. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) In each step's series the term without jumps is a Gaussian density, and every term with jumps is a normal density with variance at least \(\sigma_{\min}^2\Delta_j\), hence at most \(1/(\sigma_{\min}\sqrt{2\pi\Delta_j})\). Expanding the product over steps, the all-zero-count term is at most \(e^{-\lambda\tau}\min(D_G, M)\), and the others sum to at most \((1 - e^{-\lambda\tau})M\); and \(1 - e^{-\lambda\tau} \le \rho\). (b) The denominator dominates every null component; integrate over the latent law as in Proposition 47. \(\square\)

The ceiling is the problem: for a year of daily data, the logarithm of \(M\) exceeds that of the Gaussian supremum by about \(230\), so the envelope is dominated by \(\rho M\) unless \(\rho\) is below about \(e^{-230}\). In the same simulation, with \(\sigma_{\min} = 0.2\), the score stayed valid under jumps at every cap, but power against \(p = 3/2\) was essentially zero for any cap of \(10^{-60}\) a year or more. Only caps of about \(10^{-120}\) a year, which exclude every real jump process, kept part of the free-drift score's power, at \(3\) to \(4\%\). The quantity \(\rho\) must be computed as \(-\mathrm{expm1}(-\Lambda\tau)\): computed directly, it underflows to zero for caps below about \(10^{-16}\) and silently removes the jump envelope. Like the single-switch null, this enlargement is valid but powerless. A usable jump null would need the exact supremum over the constrained jump family, or a much tighter bound than a per-step variance floor. Stochastic volatility, regime changes, jumps to zero and rounding are not covered.

Sharing the drift and scale across the steps tightens the envelope and recovers a little power. For a finite cap, put \(q_j = e^{-\Lambda\Delta_j}\), \(p_j = 1 - q_j\) and
$$F(a, s) = \prod_j \frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi\Delta_j}}\; s^{-n}\prod_j \Big[p_j + q_j\exp\Big(-\frac{(r_j - a\Delta_j)^2}{2s^2\Delta_j}\Big)\Big],$$
let \(V = \prod_j y_j^{-1}\sup_{a \in \mathbb{R},\, s \ge \sigma_{\min}} F(a, s)\) (and \(V = M\) for an infinite cap), and let \(E_i^{\mathrm{prof}}\) divide the fitted likelihood by the largest of \(G_{i,0}\), \(D_G\) and \(V\).

**Proposition 53 (a shared-parameter jump envelope).** In the setting of Proposition 52: (a) every jump law of Proposition 52 has density at most \(V\), and \(V \le U\); \(V\) is finite and measurable, so \(E_i^{\mathrm{prof}}\) has every conclusion of Proposition 52(b), and \(E_i^J \le E_i^{\mathrm{prof}} \le E_i^{\mathrm{drift}} \le E_i\), with equality to the free-drift score at \(\Lambda = 0\) and to \(E_i^J\) at \(\Lambda = \infty\). (b) The supremum of \(F\) is attained in an explicit rectangle \([a_-, a_+] \times [\sigma_{\min}, s_+]\) computed from the evaluation returns: \(a_\pm\) are the extreme return rates and \(s_+\) is determined by the value of \(F\) at the sample rate. (c) On any closed box, an explicit upper bound for \(F\) is obtained by putting the smallest scale in the power and the largest in the exponent, at the distance from each return to the box's drift range. For any finite cover of the rectangle by boxes, chosen measurably from the data, the largest box bound \(B_P\) gives a valid envelope \(\min(U, \prod_j y_j^{-1} B_P)\) and a valid score between \(E_i^J\) and \(E_i^{\mathrm{prof}}\); refining the cover does not lower that score, and the box bounds converge to the supremum as the boxes shrink.

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound with an upper bound on the null maximum, together with the extreme-value theorem and uniform continuity on compact sets, and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) In each step's Poisson mixture, the term without jumps is the Gaussian density at the law's own drift and scale, and every term with jumps is at most the ceiling at that scale; replacing the jump probability by its cap gives \(F\) at the law's parameters, and the count expansion of Proposition 52 gives \(V \le U\). (b) Moving the drift into the range of the return rates brings every return closer, and above \(s_+\) the factor \(s^{-n}\) alone keeps \(F\) below its value at the sample rate. (c) Each factor is monotone in the scale and in the distance from the drift range, and \(F\) is uniformly continuous on the compact rectangle. \(\square\)

Only returns that look like jumps now pay the ceiling. Any finite cover gives a valid score, so the computation may stop early, but values of \(F\) at chosen points are lower bounds on the maximum and must not be used as denominators. In the same daily design, with \(\sigma_{\min} = 0.2\) and 1,000 paths per case, jump nulls within the cap gave the refined score null means of at most \(0.02\), with no false alarms. Against \(p = 3/2\) with \(\kappa = 0.45\), its power was \(5.6\%\) at a cap of one jump a year and \(0.2\%\) at five, against zero for the envelope of Proposition 52 and \(21\%\) for the free-drift score, which does not cover jumps; with \(\kappa = 0.3\) it was \(0.6\%\) and \(0\), against \(5\%\). Adaptive refinement in the scale is needed in practice: with it, the remaining gap between the upper bound and the best point value was \(0.3\) to \(0.7\) in log terms on these one-year daily blocks, while a fixed grid of boxes leaves a much larger gap, because errors in the scale compound over the steps.

Jump and switch nulls are thus feasible for validity but leave little power on daily data. They repair the failures after Propositions 46 and 47, bringing raw null means of \(17\) to \(561\) back below one. But against \(p = 3/2\) on two years of daily data, power falls from \(5\) to \(21\%\) for the free-drift score to \(0\) to \(0.3\%\) with a switch, and to \(0.6\) to \(5.6\%\) with jumps capped at one a year, about zero at five. A switch or a jump can imitate level-dependent volatility on daily data, as in the mechanism of Proposition 20. Further gains would need a tighter bound on the jump family's supremum, an honestly small cap, or richer, intraday data.

Removing returns that look like jumps does not escape this. Fix a cutoff \(a > 0\) and let \(H_a(r) = r\) for \(|r| \le a\) and \(H_a(r) = 0\) otherwise. Let \(p\) be a strictly positive probability density of a return \(R\), \(q\) any probability density, and \(\rho_p = \int_{|r| > a}p\), \(\rho_q = \int_{|r| > a}q\). On the filtered value \(Y = H_a(R)\) take the reference measure \(\nu_a\), Lebesgue measure on \(\{0 < |y| \le a\}\) plus a unit mass at zero, and the densities \(p_a\) and \(q_a\) equal to \(\rho_p\) and \(\rho_q\) at zero, to \(p\) and \(q\) on \(\{0 < |y| \le a\}\), and to zero elsewhere.

**Proposition 54 (filtered returns lose Gaussian calibration).** (a) \(Y\) has density \(p_a\) with respect to \(\nu_a\), and the filtered forecast has density \(q_a\); both have total mass one. \(P(Y = 0) = \rho_p \in (0, 1)\), so \(Y\) has no Lebesgue density and is not a nondegenerate Gaussian, and changing \(p\) at zero does not change \(\rho_p\). (b) Take one return in Proposition 35 with \(\Delta = v = 1\), \(w_L = w_H = 1\) and a free drift, at the null with \(R\) standard normal, so that the profiled denominator is the constant \(1/\sqrt{2\pi}\), and the forecast \(N(0, \varepsilon^2)\). The unfiltered score has mean \(1/\sqrt{1 + \varepsilon^2} \le 1\), and the same score applied to \(Y\) has mean at least \(\rho_p/\varepsilon\). For every \(0 < \alpha < 1\), the cutoff \(a = (1 - \alpha)/2\) and any \(0 < \varepsilon < \alpha\) make its rejection probability at threshold \(1/\alpha\) exceed \(\alpha\). (c) If a family of strictly positive densities contains \(p\), and a measurable \(D_a > 0\) on the support of \(\nu_a\) dominates every filtered density of the family, then \(q_a(Y)/D_a(Y)\) has mean at most one; with \(D_a = p_a\) the mean is exactly one, and the ratio at zero is \(\rho_q/\rho_p\), not \(q(0)/p(0)\).

*Status.* Elementary measure theory and the split-likelihood bound, proved here with the supplied densities; nothing is assumed. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) For a Borel set \(C\), \(P(Y \in C) = \int_{C \cap \{0 < |y| \le a\}}p + \rho_p 1_{0 \in C}\), which is the integral of \(p_a\) against \(\nu_a\). (b) The score equals \(\varepsilon^{-1}e^{-y^2/(2\varepsilon^2)}\), which is \(1/\varepsilon\) at every filtered zero; a Gaussian integral gives the unfiltered mean, and \(\rho_p \ge (1 + \alpha)/2 > \alpha\) at the stated cutoff. (c) The denominator dominates \(p_a\), and \(q_a\) integrates to one against \(\nu_a\). \(\square\)

A score that treats filtered returns as unfiltered ones can thus lose calibration at any prescribed level, even when the unfiltered null is exactly Gaussian and the cutoff is known; at zero, the denominator must dominate the null family's tail masses, not its densities at zero. In a check at \(\alpha = 0.05\) with cutoff \(1.5\), so that \(P(|R| > a) = 0.134\), and \(\varepsilon = 0.04\), the filtered score rejected \(15.5\%\) of the time against \(2.1\%\) for the unfiltered score. The statement concerns one fixed observation and constructs no sequential filter. It refutes only that a filtered monitor inherits the unfiltered guarantee automatically, and says nothing about the false-alarm rates of particular filtered forecasts or adaptive cutoffs.

Rounding of quoted prices is a misspecification of the observation law, not of the price model, and it defeats both the total-variation envelope and the naive use of the catalogue densities. To model it, round log prices to a grid of step \(h > 0\), \(Q_h(x) = \exp(h\lfloor \log x/h + 1/2\rfloor)\), so that each observation reports the cell containing the price.

**Proposition 55 (rounded prices).** (a) For every law of a real observation and every atomless reference law, the law of the rounded observation is at total variation one from the reference, at every \(h > 0\); the same holds for vectors against a reference absolutely continuous with respect to Lebesgue measure. (b) Let the true price be geometric Brownian motion with volatility one from \(1\) over one unit of time, let the catalogue consist of this law and a reciprocal-Bessel alternative of scale \(\kappa\), and let the selector always choose the alternative. The score that evaluates the continuous densities at the rounded terminal price \(Y\) equals \(e^{1/8}(1 - e^{-2/\kappa^2})/\kappa\) on \(\{Y = 1\}\), which has null probability \(p_h = \Phi(1/2 + h/2) - \Phi(1/2 - h/2) > 0\), so its null mean is at least \(p_h e^{1/8}(1 - e^{-2/\kappa^2})/\kappa\), which tends to infinity as \(\kappa \to 0\). At \(h = 1/2\) and \(\kappa = 1/100\) the nominal \(5\%\) rule rejects with probability more than \(1/20\), while the ratio of the two laws' cell probabilities has null mean one and is below \(20\) on that cell. (c) In the setting of Proposition 28 with every observed price rounded and the start known, let \(g^h_\vartheta(v \mid u)\) be the ratio of the probabilities, under candidate \(\vartheta\), of the observed training and evaluation cells jointly and of the training cells alone, computed from the joint price density. These are strictly positive conditional probabilities summing to one, and with them, for any selector based on the training cells, the conclusions of Propositions 27 and 28 hold for the rounded data, and so does the false-discovery bound of Proposition 40. (d) This conditional law is not that of a chain started at the rounded training price: for geometric Brownian motion with volatility one, start \(1\), grid \(0, 1, 2\) and training cell \(0\), at every \(h > 0\) some evaluation cell has a different probability under the two.

*Status.* An application of the price kernels, stated as hypothesis structures with vacuous instances and the grid laws supplied as premises, of the split-likelihood bound (proved) and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; (a) and the constants in (b) are proved outright. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) A rounded law is concentrated on countably many points, which an atomless or absolutely continuous law gives measure zero. (b) On \(\{Y = 1\}\) the rounded price is \(1\), where the density ratio is the displayed value, and \(p_h\) is the normal probability of the cell. (c) The cell probabilities are the actual probabilities of the observed labels, so they are conditional densities against counting measure, and the split-likelihood argument applies. (d) Conditioning on the training cell leaves positive mass on latent prices above the representative, where the transition-density ratio against starting at the representative grows with the evaluation cell. \(\square\)

Numerically, \(p_h \approx 0.175\) at \(h = 1/2\), so the nominal \(5\%\) rule in (b) rejects with probability about \(0.175\); that grid is a counterexample, not a realistic tick. The correction in (c) is exact but not practical: its cell probabilities are integrals over the cells of the whole training prefix, since the conditional law of the evaluation prices averages over the unobserved exact training prices, and no practical scorer is given here. As a heuristic, not a bound, the size of the problem is set by the tick relative to the one-step spread of the narrowest catalogue law: a one-cent tick on a fifty-dollar stock is about \(0.02\%\) in log price, small against daily volatility, so the plug-in error is probably small for daily data, but it need not be for intraday data or for catalogues with tight alternatives.

For Gaussian nulls, the unobserved exact price inside the last training cell can be budgeted instead of filtered. Fix a finite catalogue \(\Gamma_i\) of Gaussian null laws with smallest volatility \(\kappa_{\min}\), the log tick \(h\) and the first evaluation step \(\Delta_1\). Let \(g^{\mathrm{restart}}_\theta(v \mid u)\) be the probability that the whole rounded evaluation block is \(v\) when prices follow \(\theta \in \Gamma_i\) from the representative of the last training cell, and set \(E_i^{\mathrm{restart}} = q_i(v \mid u)/\max_\theta g^{\mathrm{restart}}_\theta(v \mid u)\), for any normalized forecast \(q_i\) of the cell labels based on the rounded training record \(u\). Put \(\varepsilon^{\mathrm{round}} = \sqrt{1 - \exp(-h^2/(16\kappa_{\min}^2\Delta_1))}\).

**Proposition 56 (a rounding budget without filtering).** Suppose that a bubble-free name's future log returns, given its rounded training record and its exact last training price, are Gaussian under one of the catalogue laws. (a) The restarted probabilities are positive and sum to one, and the score has mean at most one under each restarted law. (b) At almost every rounded training record, the actual conditional law of the rounded evaluation block is within total variation \(\sqrt{1 - \exp(-h^2/(16\kappa^2\Delta_1))} \le \varepsilon^{\mathrm{round}}\) of the restarted law of its catalogue law, whatever the conditional law of the exact price within its cell and however many observations follow. (c) Hence \(E_i^{\mathrm{restart}} > 1/\alpha_i\) has size at most \(\alpha_i + \varepsilon^{\mathrm{round}}\), the threshold \(1/(\alpha_i - \varepsilon^{\mathrm{round}})\) has size at most \(\alpha_i\) when \(\varepsilon^{\mathrm{round}} < \alpha_i\), and \(\min(E_i^{\mathrm{restart}}, c_i)/(1 + c_i\varepsilon^{\mathrm{round}})\) has mean at most one, so the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\) for it under any dependence. (d) \(\varepsilon^{\mathrm{round}} \le e\) exactly when \(h \le 4\kappa_{\min}\sqrt{\Delta_1}\sqrt{-\log(1 - e^2)}\), and \(\varepsilon^{\mathrm{round}}\) tends to one as \(\Delta_1\) or \(\kappa_{\min}\) tends to zero.

*Status.* An application of the Gaussian affinity of Proposition 43(c), through the rounding map and integrated over the exact price, and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* Starting from the exact price instead of the representative moves only the first log return, by at most \(h/2\); the two Gaussian laws of the returns then have affinity at least \(\exp(-h^2/(32\kappa^2\Delta_1))\), which bounds total variation after the rounding map. The actual law is a mixture of such laws over the exact price, so the bound survives averaging. (c) follows as in Proposition 43. \(\square\)

Reading a one-cent tick as a log tick of one cent divided by the price, as the logarithmic grid requires, and with \(\kappa_{\min} = 0.2\), the budget is about \(0.002\) at daily sampling and \(0.018\) at five minutes on a hundred-dollar stock, \(0.020\) and \(0.17\) on a ten-dollar stock, and \(0.10\) and \(0.73\) on a two-dollar stock: essentially free for daily data on normally priced stocks. It applies only to this cell likelihood, not to the scores of Propositions 29–39, 41, 42, 48, 50, 52 and 53, which evaluate continuous densities at rounded prices and can fail by Proposition 55(b); computing it needs joint integrals over the cells of the evaluation block, and no scalable implementation is given. Constant-elasticity nulls, absorption, jumps and stochastic volatility are not covered.

A cheaper route avoids both the latent price and the joint cell integrals, at a cost that grows with every observation. For a Gaussian null law \(\theta\) and evaluation step \(j\), let \(K_{\theta,j}\) be the largest probability of the observed cell \(z_j\) over all exact starting prices in the previous cell \(z_{j-1}\), let \(B_\theta = \prod_j K_{\theta,j}\), and set \(E_i^{\mathrm{cell}} = q_i(v \mid u)/\max_{\theta \in \Gamma_i} B_\theta\), for a finite catalogue \(\Gamma_i\) of Gaussian null laws and any normalized forecast \(q_i\) of the cell labels based on the rounded training record.

**Proposition 57 (a Gaussian cell envelope).** Under the premise of Proposition 56: (a) \(K_{\theta,j}\) is a difference of two normal distribution functions evaluated at the start that puts the mean step nearest the middle of the target cell, clipped to the previous cell, and it is the supremum over starts in that cell; it lies strictly between zero and one. (b) At almost every rounded training record, the actual probability of the observed evaluation cells is at most \(B_\theta\) for the true law \(\theta\), whatever the conditional law of the exact price in the last training cell; so \(E_i^{\mathrm{cell}}\) is finite, has conditional mean at most one, and \(E_i^{\mathrm{cell}} > 1/\alpha_i\) has size at most \(\alpha_i\), and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 has \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\) for these scores under any dependence, with no total-variation budget and no clipping. (c) For a law with zero log drift and equal steps \(\Delta\), each row of transition maxima sums to \(S = 2\Phi(h/(2\kappa\sqrt\Delta)) > 1\), so the path envelope has total mass \(S^n\); and dividing the envelope by this excess is invalid: with one step from the representative of the last cell, a forecast concentrated on the central cell then has null mean \(S > 1\).

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound with an upper bound on the null probability and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) A normal cell probability, as a function of the mean, increases up to the cell's midpoint and decreases after it. (b) By induction along the path, from every exact start in the last training cell the probability of the remaining observed cells is at most the product of the step maxima; averaging over the exact start keeps the bound; then \(\sum_v (q_i/B_\theta) P(v \mid u) \le \sum_v q_i = 1\). (c) The row sum is a direct summation of normal distribution functions over the cells. \(\square\)

Computing the envelope needs one normal-distribution difference per step and null law. In a simulation with a geometric-Brownian null of volatility \(0.3\) and, as numerator, the true law's one-step forecast from each rounded price, the score kept its null mean below one. But its log evidence fell by about the logarithm of the total excess mass: about \(0.5\) over a year of daily data at a log tick of \(10^{-4}\) (about one cent on a hundred-dollar stock, on the logarithmic grid), \(5\) at \(10^{-3}\), and \(30\) and about \(280\) over a month of five-minute data, against a rejection threshold of \(\log 20 \approx 3\) at the \(5\%\) level. The two routes are complementary: this one is cheap and nearly free for daily data with fine ticks, but ruinous at high frequency or with coarse ticks, while the restarted cell likelihood of Proposition 56 has a small budget at high frequency but needs joint cell integrals. Neither covers five-minute data cheaply as it stands; the refinement of Proposition 58 reduces the envelope's cost. Constant-elasticity nulls, absorption, jumps and stochastic volatility are not covered, and rounding to a fixed number of cents differs from this logarithmic grid.

Subdividing the cells makes this valid rounded-price likelihood affordable; it does not create power. At level \(\ell\), split each cell into \(K = 2^\ell\) equal subcells. For each subcell \(a\) of the cell observed at step \(j\) and each null law \(\theta\), define backward along the observed path \(U^\ell_{\theta,n,a} = 1\) and
$$U^\ell_{\theta,j,a} = \min\Big(1,\ \sum_{d} W^\ell_{\theta,j,a,d}\,U^\ell_{\theta,j+1,d},\ U^{\ell-1}_{\theta,j,\lfloor a/2\rfloor}\Big),$$
where \(W^\ell_{\theta,j,a,d}\) is the largest probability of reaching subcell \(d\) at step \(j + 1\) from an exact price in subcell \(a\), and the last term is omitted at level zero. Let \(B^\ell_\theta = \max_a U^\ell_{\theta,0,a}\) and \(E_i^{\ell} = q_i/\max_{\theta \in \Gamma_i} B^\ell_\theta\), with the numerator and catalogue of Proposition 57.

**Proposition 58 (subdividing the cell envelope).** Under the premise of Proposition 56: (a) \(W^\ell_{\theta,j,a,d}\) is the clipped-midpoint difference of normal distribution functions of Proposition 57(a), for subcells of any width, and lies in \((0, 1]\). (b) Every \(U^\ell_{\theta,j,a}\) lies in \((0, 1]\) and bounds the probability of the remaining observed cells from every exact price in its subcell; hence, at almost every rounded training record, the actual probability of the observed evaluation cells is at most \(B^\ell_\theta\) for the true law, at every level simultaneously. (c) \(B^0_\theta\) is the envelope of Proposition 57, and \(B^{\ell+1}_\theta \le B^\ell_\theta\), so \(E_i^{\ell+1} \ge E_i^\ell\). (d) For any level \(L(u, v)\), even one chosen after seeing the evaluation cells, \(E_i^{L}\) has conditional mean at most one and \(E_i^L > 1/\alpha_i\) has size at most \(\alpha_i\), and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\) under any dependence.

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound with an upper bound on the null probability and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (b) For any one-step law and any finite partition of the next cell, the probability of the remaining path is at most \(\sum_d W_d U_d\), by induction backward along the path; the parent bound is valid by the previous level, and the minimum of valid bounds is valid. (c) The parent term gives monotonicity. (d) Since every level dominates, so does any choice among them, and \(\sum_v (q_i/B^L_\theta) P(v \mid u) \le 1\). \(\square\)

The cost is of order \(nK^2\) normal-distribution differences per path and null law. In the simulation after Proposition 57, each level roughly halved the loss of log evidence. At level five it fell from \(5.3\) to \(0.18\) over a year of daily data at a log tick of \(10^{-3}\), and from \(30\) to about \(1\) over a month of five-minute data at \(10^{-4}\), at about \(1.7 \times 10^6\) one-step probabilities per path and null law. A loss below \(0.1\) at five minutes would need about level eight, roughly \(10^8\) evaluations per path. The limits stated after Proposition 57 still apply.

Propositions 56–58 assume a logarithmic grid of fixed width; real quotes are rounded to a fixed number of cents, and can be zero. For a tick \(\delta\), let the quote of a price \(x \ge 0\) be \(\delta\lfloor x/\delta + 1/2\rfloor\), so that the cells are \([0, \delta/2)\) and \([\delta(z - 1/2), \delta(z + 1/2))\) for \(z \ge 1\). Suppose that, given the rounded training record, the exact last price lies in the last observed cell with an arbitrary law, possibly with an atom at zero; that from a positive price the future log returns are independent Gaussian under one catalogue law, with \(\mu\) and \(s^2\) the step's mean and variance; and that from zero the price stays at zero. For a source interval \([a, b)\) and a target \([c, d)\), let \(W\) be one if \(a = c = 0\); \(\Phi((\log d - \log a - \mu)/s)\) if \(c = 0 < a\); and otherwise the difference of normal distribution functions at the log midpoint of the target clipped to \([\log a + \mu, \log b + \mu]\), unbounded below when \(a = 0\). Run the backward recursion of Proposition 58 with \(W\) on subcells of equal price width, the first subcell of the zero cell containing zero.

**Proposition 59 (cent ticks and zero quotes).** (a) \(W\) lies in \((0, 1]\) and is the supremum of the one-step probability of reaching the target over every exact source price, including zero; the product of these maxima along the path, and each level of the recursion, bounds the probability of the observed cells from every exact price in the last cell, and the bounds decrease with the level. (b) So the score with any normalized forecast of the labels, at a level chosen measurably from the data, has conditional mean at most one and size at most \(\alpha\), and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\); the product of one-step cell probabilities from the representatives \(\delta z\), or \(\delta/4\) at a zero quote, is such a forecast. (c) A positive cell's log width \(\log((y + \delta/2)/(y - \delta/2))\) at quote \(y\) is asymptotic to \(\delta/y\) but not equal to it, and the zero cell has no finite log width. (d) From a positive price \(\delta/4\) inside the zero cell, the next quote is positive with probability \(1 - \Phi((\log 2 - \mu)/s) > 0\), and from an exact zero with probability zero; on an all-zero path from a zero quote, the bound is one at every level.

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound with an upper bound on the null probability and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the Gaussian cell results of Propositions 57 and 58 are reused. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) For a positive target, the normal cell probability is unimodal in the log start, so its supremum over the source is at the clipped midpoint; a zero target is reached most easily from the lowest source price, and from an exact zero with certainty. The recursion and its monotonicity are as for Proposition 58, the trapped zero entering through the first subcell. (b) As for Proposition 57, by averaging over the exact last price. (c) and (d) are direct computations. \(\square\)

So the earlier results do not apply to cents without this change. A zero quote does not reveal an exact zero, but at daily volatility the probability in (d) is about \(10^{-295}\), so zero quotes are effectively absorbing there. In a simulation with a geometric-Brownian null of volatility \(0.3\), one year of daily data and a cent tick, the loss of log evidence fell from \(10.3\) at level zero to \(0.72\) at level four for a stock starting at five dollars, and from \(77.5\) to \(8.6\) for one starting at fifty cents. So penny stocks need deeper refinement, at a cost of order \(nK^2\). An implementation of this score in outward-rounded ball arithmetic (`pythonflint080balls`) returns a value no larger than the exact score, conditional on that library and on the code; it is not machine-checked. Gaussian nulls only are covered: not constant-elasticity or default nulls, jumps, stochastic volatility, or real quote rules such as bid and ask prices and changes of tick size.

The model-mismatch budgets of Propositions 43 and 47 carry over to the cent-tick score without any extra cost for rounding. Let \(E_i\) be the cent-tick score of Proposition 59, at any level, with a finite Gaussian catalogue \(\Gamma_i\). Suppose that, given its rounded training record, a bubble-free name's evaluation law is a mixture, over a latent exact last price \(x\) in the last observed cell and a latent variable \(z\), of laws that start at \(x\), stay at zero if \(x = 0\), and otherwise have independent Gaussian log returns with drift \(b_j\) and volatility \(\sigma_j > 0\) at step \(j\), all rounded to cents. Let \(\varepsilon(u, x, z)\) be the smallest over \(\theta \in \Gamma_i\) of the affinity budget of Proposition 47(a) for that component, zero at \(x = 0\), and let \(\bar\varepsilon_i(u)\) be its average over the latent law.

**Proposition 60 (model mismatch for the cent-tick score).** In this setting, with a supplied measurable \(e_i \ge \bar\varepsilon_i\): (a) there is a reference mixture \(G_i\), with the same latent law and each positive component replaced by its closest catalogue law, such that the rounded actual law is within total variation \(\bar\varepsilon_i\) of \(G_i\) and \(E_i\) has mean at most one under \(G_i\). (b) Hence \(E_i > 1/\alpha_i\) has size at most \(\alpha_i + e_i\), conditionally and unconditionally; the threshold \(1/(\alpha_i - e_*)\) has size at most \(\alpha_i\) when \(e_i \le e_* < \alpha_i\); and \(\min(E_i, c_i)/(1 + c_i e_i)\) has mean at most one, so the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\) for it, and it can reject only if \(e_i < \alpha_i\) and \(c_i > 1/(\alpha_i - e_i)\). (c) If every positive component is a constant-parameter catalogue law, possibly a different one for each component, then \(\bar\varepsilon_i = 0\) and the raw score has mean at most one; and the uniform envelope of Proposition 47(c) bounds \(\bar\varepsilon_i\) without reference to the tick or the level.

*Status.* An application of Propositions 43 and 47 and the domination of the cent-tick envelope from every exact starting price, and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) From each exact start, the score has mean at most one under every catalogue law, by the domination from every exact start and the normalization of the forecast. The actual and reference components start from the same price and pass through the same rounding map, so their distance is at most that of the Gaussian return laws, which is the budget of Proposition 47(a); averaging over the latent law gives (a). (b) follows as in Proposition 43, and (c) as in Proposition 47. \(\square\)

The budget is as weak as that of Proposition 47. In a check with cent ticks from one dollar, fifteen daily steps, a single null law with volatility \(0.30\), subdivision level two and 300 paths, a true volatility of \(0.30\) gave budget zero and a false-alarm rate of \(1.3\%\) at \(\alpha = 0.1\). A true volatility of \(0.40\) gave a budget of \(0.68\), above \(\alpha\), so the clipped test could not reject at all; its mean was \(0.001\). Rounding may shrink the true distance, but this sufficient budget does not exploit that. It gives no informative guarantee for volatility that changes within the window; stochastic volatility outside this representation, jumps and constant-elasticity or default nulls are not covered.

Misspecification bounds thus exist, but they are useful only for small, slow departures from the null. An error in the drift costs a budget that does not grow with the sampling frequency. Errors in the volatility, volatility that changes within the window, and jumps cost budgets that grow with the frequency and quickly leave the clipped test without power, while the raw scores lose validity. Rounding, by contrast, is handled for Gaussian nulls: cent ticks and zero quotes are covered, and the computation is affordable for intraday data. Nulls enlarged to absorb jumps and switches are valid but leave little power on daily data.

Quotes can also carry errors before they are rounded. Fix an error bound \(a \ge 0\). Under the *support contract*, each pre-rounding price \(Y_j \ge 0\) differs from the latent price \(X_j\) by at most \(a\), with errors that may be dependent across dates and on the path. The premise is now stated given the noisy, rounded training record: the catalogue law and the exact last price have an arbitrary joint law given that record, and from a positive last price the latent future has that law's independent Gaussian log returns; from zero it stays at zero. An unconditional Gaussian specification alone does not supply this. Under the stronger *uniform contract*, also \(Y_j = \max(0, X_j + \varepsilon_j)\), with \(\varepsilon_1, \dots, \varepsilon_n\) independent and uniform on \([-a, a]\) given the record and the latent path. Widen each observed cell by \(a\) on each side, and run the backward recursion of the cent-tick envelope on subcells of the widened cells. Under the support contract each step's factor is the cent-tick maximum; under the uniform contract it is multiplied by the supremum, over the subcell, of the explicit probability that a uniform error moves the latent price into the observed cell.

**Proposition 61 (bounded and uniform observation noise).** (a) Under each contract, with its own recursion, the actual probability of the observed evaluation cells is at most the resulting bound \(D\), with \(0 < D \le 1\), at almost every training record and at every level; so the score with any normalized forecast of the labels, at any level chosen measurably from the data, has conditional mean at most one and size at most \(\alpha\), and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\). (b) The bounds decrease with the level, and the uniform bound is at most the support bound, so the uniform score is at least the support score for the same forecast; at \(a = 0\) both recursions are the cent-tick recursion. (c) On a path of zero quotes from a zero quote, the support bound is one at every level, and the uniform bound is \(g^n\) with \(g = \min(1, 1/2 + \delta/(4a))\) for tick \(\delta\). In particular, with \(a = \delta\), \(n = 12\) and an exact price of zero, which satisfies the support contract, the uniform score of the forecast concentrated on the all-zero path is \((4/3)^{12} > 20\) with probability one: used under the support contract alone, the uniform score has size one.

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound with an upper bound on the null probability and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) A bounded error puts the latent price in the widened cell. The probability of the observed labels is at most (support) or equal to (uniform) the expected product of emission factors along the latent path, and that expectation is bounded on every subcell by backward induction, as for Proposition 58. (b) Each emission factor is at most one, and the parent bound gives monotonicity. (c) From zero the latent path stays at zero, where the uniform emission of the zero cell is \(g\). \(\square\)

A quote of zero is thus not evidence of default under noise. Noise is expensive. In a check with a five-dollar geometric-Brownian null of volatility \(0.3\), a cent tick, errors of up to half a tick, 60 daily steps and the cent-tick forecast, the median log evidence at subdivision level three was \(-0.4\) without noise, \(-42.3\) under the support bound and \(-14.1\) under the uniform law. The support bound costs about \(\log 2\) a day at this error size, close to \(n\log 2\) over \(n\) steps. The uniform law recovers most of that, and was still improving with the level. Neither mode supplies a real bound on the noise, and jumps and stochastic volatility are not covered.

A much stronger declaration removes almost all of that cost. Suppose that each error is exactly \(+a\) or \(-a\), like a bid–ask bounce: \(Y_j = \max(0, X_j + aH_j)\) with a sign \(H_j \in \{-1, +1\}\). Given the previous sign \(h\) and the latent move from \(x\) to \(y\), the next sign is positive with probability \(\rho\,1\{h = +1\} + (1 - \rho)\,\mathrm{logistic}(\beta\log(y/x))\), for declared \(\rho \in [0, 1)\) and \(\beta\), with the logistic replaced by \(1/2\) at a zero price. As before, the law is stated given the noisy training record, with an arbitrary joint posterior for the catalogue law, the exact last price and the current sign. Given the sign, each label pins the latent price to the cell shifted by the sign times \(a\), one tick wide, rather than to a cell widened by \(2a\). Run the backward recursion over feasible sign and subcell pairs, multiplying the cent-tick transition bound by an explicit upper bound on each sign transition over the pair of subcells and keeping the parent of the same sign, and let the bound be the smaller of this one and the support bound of Proposition 61.

**Proposition 62 (persistent and return-dependent noise signs).** (a) Under the declared law, the actual probability of the observed evaluation cells is at most the bound at almost every training record and every level, and the bound lies in \((0, 1]\); so the score with any normalized forecast of the labels, at a level chosen measurably from the data, has conditional mean at most one and size at most \(\alpha\), and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\). (b) The bound decreases with the level, and the score is at least the support score of Proposition 61. With \(\beta = 0\) the sign stays the same with probability \((1 + \rho)/2\); with also \(\rho = 0\) the signs are independent fair signs; and with \(a = 0\) the bound is the cent-tick bound, for every \(\beta\). (c) With \(a\) equal to one tick, \(\beta = 0\) and an all-zero label path of length \(n\), the bound is \(((1 + \rho)/2)^n\), which is the actual probability from an exact zero with a negative sign. In particular, with \(\rho = 0.9\) and \(n = 5\), a forecast concentrated on the all-zero path scores \(32 > 20\) against the bound for independent fair signs, \((1/2)^5\), on an event of probability \(0.95^5 \approx 0.77\): treating persistent signs as independent gives a nominal \(5\%\) test of size about \(0.77\).

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound with an upper bound on the null probability and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance; the cent-tick transition bound and the support bound of Proposition 61 are reused. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) A label from sign \(t\) puts the latent price in the cell shifted by \(t\). The probability of the label path is an integral over the returns of a sum over sign paths, bounded on every subcell by backward induction, since each sign transition is at most its upper bound and the cent-tick bound covers the price step. The support bound holds because the returns' marginal law is still Gaussian, the sign weights summing to one. (b) Each row of sign bounds sums to at least one, which gives the reduction at \(a = 0\). (c) From zero the latent path stays at zero, where every step's sign stays negative with probability \((1 + \rho)/2\). \(\square\)

In the noise check after Proposition 61, with signs from the declared rule with \(\rho = 0.5\) and \(\beta = 0\), the median log evidence at level three was \(-0.4\), essentially the noiseless level, against \(-42.3\) under the support bound. The gain comes entirely from the strong declaration: errors of exactly \(\pm a\), with a known radius and a known sign rule. That is an empirical premise that free price data cannot check, and estimating \(\rho\) or \(\beta\) from training gives no conditional coverage. A bounded but continuous error still costs about \(\log 2\) a step.

The declared variance clocks extend to rounded prices, over a compact parameter box. Declare in advance a finite set \(\mathcal V\) of clocks, as in Proposition 34, and a closed box \(\mathcal B = [b_-, b_+] \times [w_-, w_+]\) of drifts and variances, with \(w_- > 0\). For a subbox \(B = [b_L, b_H] \times [w_L, w_H]\), a clock increment \(v\) and price intervals \(S = [a, d_S)\) and \(T\), let \(W_{\mathrm{clock}}(B; S, T)\) be the largest normal probability of reaching \(T\) over log means in \([\log a + b_L\Delta - w_Hv/2,\ \log d_S + b_H\Delta - w_Lv/2]\) and scales in \([\sqrt{w_Lv}, \sqrt{w_Hv}]\). It is a difference of normal distribution functions at a clipped mean with an explicit scale, with the trapped-zero conventions of the cent-tick bound. Run the backward recursion of the cent-tick envelope with \(W_{\mathrm{clock}}\) for each subbox of the \(h\)-fold bisection of \(\mathcal B\) and each clock, at cell level \(\ell\), and let \(D^{h,\ell}\) be the maximum over subboxes and clocks.

**Proposition 63 (rounded variance clocks over a compact box).** Suppose that, given the whole rounded training record, the exact last price, the clock and \((b, w)\) have an arbitrary joint law supported on the last observed cell, \(\mathcal V\) and \(\mathcal B\), and that, given these, the future log returns are independent normal with mean \(b\Delta_j - wv_j/2\) and variance \(wv_j\), rounded to cents. (a) \(W_{\mathrm{clock}}\) lies in \((0, 1]\) and bounds the one-step probability of reaching \(T\) from every price in \(S\), including zero, and every \((b, w) \in B\); for a single parameter it is the cent-tick bound. (b) \(D^{h,\ell}\) lies in \((0, 1]\), bounds the probability of the observed cells at almost every training record, and decreases in \(h\) and in \(\ell\). (c) With any normalized forecast of the labels and any levels \((h, \ell)\) chosen measurably from the data, the score has conditional mean at most one and size at most \(\alpha\), and the e-BH rule of Proposition 40 keeps \(\mathrm{FDR} \le q\,m_0/m\). On an all-zero path from a zero quote the bound is one; with the calendar clock alone and singleton boxes, the recursion is the cent-tick recursion.

*Status.* An application of the split-likelihood bound with an upper bound on the null probability and, for the false-discovery bound, of the e-BH theorem, stated as a hypothesis with a degenerate instance. Machine-checked (Appendix A).

*Proof.* (a) A normal cell probability is unimodal in the mean, and in the log scale it increases up to \(\sqrt{(r^2 - u^2)/(2\log(r/u))}\) and decreases after it, where \(u < r\) are the distances to the cell's ends; so the maximum over the rectangle is at the clipped mean and clipped scale. (b) By induction backward along the path, as for Proposition 58; a child box lies in its parent's rectangle, which gives monotonicity. (c) As for Proposition 58. \(\square\)

Only the computation is refined; the declared family is not narrowed. Refining the parameter box removes most of its cost. In a check with a five-dollar geometric-Brownian null of volatility \(0.3\), a cent tick, 60 daily steps and the cent-tick forecast at the true parameters, the median log evidence was \(-0.67\) for the true parameter alone.
