| Truth | Clock score, five-minute | Clock score, hourly | Drift-robust, calendar time, five-minute | Clock-aware forecast without the clock null, five-minute |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| Gaussian on the declared clock | 0.000 | 0.000 | 0.000 | 0.000 |
| Gaussian, stronger U-shape (undeclared) | 0.000 | 0.000 | 0.001 | 0.325 |
| Gaussian, overnight share 0.5 (undeclared) | 0.000 | 0.000 | 0.000 | 0.263 |
| \(p = 1.5\), \(\kappa = 0.3\) | 0.845 | 0.106 | 0.070 | 0.985 |
| \(p = 1.5\), \(\kappa = 0.45\) | 0.963 | 0.296 | 0.002 | 0.995 |
| \(p = 2\), \(\kappa = 0.3\) | 0.997 | 0.537 | 0.326 | 1.000 |
| \(p = 2\), \(\kappa = 0.45\) | 0.998 | 0.771 | 0.003 | 0.999 |

*Scope.* Rejection rates at \(\alpha = 0.05\) over 250 sessions (about a year), exact prices, constant-elasticity alternatives in clock time, no weekends or noise. 2,000 paths per null cell and 1,000 per alternative (standard errors at most 0.011 and 0.016). The undeclared clocks are outside every score's null.

A declared clock leaves useful power: a year of five-minute bars detects these alternatives 85 to 100% of the time, with no false alarm on any tested null; over 60 sessions the power is 0.03 to 0.65, and with hourly bars over a year 0.11 to 0.77. Ignoring the clock cost power rather than false alarms on these paths: the calendar-time score found at most a third of the alternatives. A forecast that follows the clock, without a null that does, false-alarms on 26 to 40% of undeclared-clock paths at five-minute sampling.

Real intraday prices are rounded to the cent. Applied to rounded prices as if they were exact, which no result here licenses (Proposition 55), the clock score raised no false alarm above 0.002 on any null and kept its power on a $50 price; on a $5 price its five-minute power over a year fell from 1.000 to 0.492 (paired difference \(-0.508 \pm 0.016\)). The valid rounded-price score of Proposition 63 is far weaker. Its best hourly power over a year, with a declared volatility box \(\kappa \in [0.20, 0.45]\) and the deeper refinement, is 0.297 against the exact-price clock score's 0.772 (paired difference \(-0.475 \pm 0.027\)); with a wide box it stays at or below 0.10. A narrow box gives false alarms when the volatility lies outside it: a Gaussian null with \(\kappa = 0.60\) was flagged on 18 to 24% of paths (600 paths; 95% Wilson interval \([0.21, 0.28]\) at one setting). The most powerful rounded score costs about 10 seconds per path for a year of hourly bars, against 0.06 seconds for the exact-price score.

A common clock can be learned from other names, but here that bought nothing. On daily panels of 20 names sharing one of 15 four-block clocks, a reference panel identified the true clock in every one of 200 panels per cell (95% upper bound 0.018 on a wrong exclusion), but excluding the other clocks never changed a null name's denominator, and the selected score of Proposition 36 found no more bubbles than the full clock catalogue (paired differences in true-discovery proportion between \(-0.005 \pm 0.004\) and \(0.000 \pm 0.004\)). The comparison does show the cost of a wrong clock: under a common non-calendar clock the drift-robust test on calendar time had a false-discovery rate of 0.345 on all-null panels, while the full clock catalogue stayed at or below 0.001 with true-discovery proportions of 0.53 to 0.83. With name-specific switches instead of a common clock, selection from the reference panel was harmful: false-discovery rate \(0.255 \pm 0.031\) against \(0.065 \pm 0.017\) for the full catalogue.

Resetting the nuisance parameters each day buys robustness at the cost of all the power at these horizons. On hourly bars over 20, 60 and 250 sessions, the daily-reset score of Proposition 37 rejected in no null or alternative cell. Its denominator exceeds the whole-record clock profile by about 1.1 to 1.2 nats per day, combining each day's drift, scale and clock shape. The whole-record clock profile has power over 250 sessions (0.32 to 0.77 against \(\kappa = 0.45\) or \(p = 2\)) but false-alarms under volatility switches between days (\(0.061 \pm 0.005\)) and within days (\(0.073 \pm 0.006\)). Budgets on switches sit between the two, and in the following comparison they keep the robustness and lose the power.

| Truth | Common profile (Proposition 34) | Joint budget \(K = 1\) | Joint budget \(K = 3\) | Free daily drift, \(K = 3\) (Proposition 38) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| constant Gaussian | 0 | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| three scale switches, common drift | 0.0235 | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| three joint drift and scale switches | 0.0190 | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| three scale switches, drift redrawn daily | 0.0225 | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| \(p = 2\), \(\kappa = 0.45\) | 0.2735 | 0.0045 | 0 | 0 |

*Scope.* Rejection rates at threshold 20 (a zero is an observed count); 60 training and 60 evaluation days of seven exact increments each; 2,000 records per row (standard error at most 0.011). The joint budgets are those of Proposition 39; the switching nulls are covered at \(K \ge 3\) and not at \(K = 1\), and the daily-redrawn drift is outside the joint budgets.

The joint budget cuts the alternative's profile penalty from 42 nats (free daily drift) to 12.5, but restores no power: 9 of 2,000 rejections at \(K = 1\), none at \(K = 3\), against 547 for the common profile, which has no guarantee under any of the switching nulls.

### 5.5 An implied-volatility clock

The implied-volatility null of Proposition 35 was run on daily simulated records: 126 training returns, then 252 or 504 scored returns monitored at every step. The score's forecast is a mixture of 28 local-lognormal forecasts whose variance scales the previous day's implied volatility by the price level to a power \(p - 1\), \(p \in \{1, 1.25, 1.5, 2\}\), with Bayesian weights and a drift fitted on past returns only. The implied volatility is a synthetic, predictable proxy, not an option-implied volatility. The primary band for the realized-to-implied variance ratio is \(w \in [0.3, 1.0]\), which covers the published ratio near 0.45, with \([0.25, 1.25]\) and \([0.5, 1.0]\) reported separately. Controls inside the null have constant drifts \(b \in \{-0.1, 0, 0.1\}\), ratios \(w\) from 0.25 to 1.25, and implied volatility that is constant, switches between 0.15 and 0.40 about every 20 days (revealed a day ahead), or rises with the price level.

| \(p\) | \(\kappa = 0.2\) | \(\kappa = 0.4\) | \(\kappa = 0.8\) | Switching background, \(\kappa = 0.2\) | Switching background, \(\kappa = 0.4\) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1.25 | 0.0 / 1.0 | 1.3 / 8.8 | 11.8 / 44.4 | | |
| 1.5 | 1.2 / 9.9 | 14.0 / 45.8 | 46.3 / 84.9 | 7.9 / 26.9 | 31.1 / 74.1 |
| 2 | 12.8 / 44.7 | 47.8 / 86.9 | 88.0 / 99.5 | 32.5 / 73.3 | 75.0 / 97.5 |

*Scope.* Percentage of records crossing \(1/\alpha = 20\) at any time over one / two years of scored daily returns, primary band. The first three columns are exact constant-elasticity prices with implied volatility tracking only the initial scale; the last two are a local-lognormal model whose background variance switches between 1 and 4 times the base about every 20 days, with implied volatility tracking the background but not the level. 2,000 records per cell (standard error at most 0.011); synthetic implied volatility; exact prices.

Inside its contract the score is calibrated: the 72 control cells whose ratio lies inside the primary band cross at most 1.45% at \(\alpha = 0.05\) (3.4% at \(\alpha = 0.10\)), including a revealed switching scale and an implied volatility that rises with the level; the other two bands give at most 1.15% and 0.95%. A ratio below the band is the costly error: controls with \(w = 0.25\) crossed the primary band 12 to 61% of the time, and the band \([0.5, 1]\) flagged 94 to 100% of controls with \(w \le 0.3\). Outside the null, Merton jumps (diffusion share 0.83) crossed 7.7 to 17.4% and switching within the day 3.8 to 8.3%, growing with the horizon, while Heston volatility with implied volatility built from its state crossed at most 0.1%. When implied volatility tracks the level dependence fully, the score absorbs it: on the same exact constant-elasticity price records, power falls to at most 0.2% in every cell, and with partial tracking to 0 to 66%. Proposition 35(c) proves the absorption exactly for the discrete local-lognormal law; for exact constant-elasticity transitions these rates are numerical evidence. Level dependence that implied volatility does not track is detected even with a switching background that it does track: 27 to 98% over two years and 8 to 75% over one, at nominal \(\alpha\) and in-band false alarms of at most 1.3%. A threshold calibrated empirically on switching controls raises that power but is not a guarantee: held out, it gave pooled false alarms of 3 to 6%, but 12.2% in the worst in-band cell. On the same paths GSADF crossed 4 to 42% of every kind of control, and the drift-robust test, which ignores implied volatility, flagged 9 to 53% of the controls whose implied volatility rises with the level.

*Scope.* A synthetic implied volatility that reveals the background state exactly and predictably, with a constant ratio inside the band; real implied volatility is noisy and its ratio varies. Exact prices; floating-point scores, not certified. Non-bubble departures also fire, so a rejection is a departure from the null, not necessarily a bubble.

### 5.6 Sequential monitoring

The monitored score of Proposition 69, with a predictable local-lognormal forecast, was piloted on hourly bars rounded to the cent: 10 training sessions, then 20 monitored sessions with a check at the end of each, certified denominators over the box of drifts in \([-0.5, 0.5]\) and variances in \([0.01, 0.64]\), and 100 paths per law. Under a null inside the box the score never crossed \(1/\alpha\) at \(\alpha = 0.05\) (95% upper bound 0.037). Under volatility switching between sessions, outside the null, it crossed once in 100 paths. Against late-starting constant-elasticity bubbles (\(p = 1.5\) or \(2\), \(\kappa = 0.45\), from the sixth monitored session) it detected none within 15 sessions of onset, whether the forecast was refitted on the growing history or frozen; refitting raised the median final log score from about \(-21\) to \(-11\). A weighted scan over restarts at three declared dates with weights \(1/3\) (Proposition 70), on the same paths, raised the median final log score further (\(-9.0\) against \(-12.7\) for \(p = 1.5\)), but no restart's score, weighted or not, reached \(1/\alpha\) on any path. For the selected forecasts these misses are not numerical: an enclosure of every score at every check certified that none of the ideal procedures crosses. Many denominators reached their computation budget and kept valid but looser bounds; a 30-session record with 20 certified checks costs about 15 seconds per path.

*Scope.* A feasibility pilot: one horizon, one box, hourly sampling, 100 paths per law (standard error at most 0.05), size evidence from the in-box null only. It shows no detection for this forecast and design, not a limit on what any forecast could detect.

### 5.7 Numerical checks of Sections 3.1 to 3.3

The wealth and fund results were also checked numerically, at the cases computed.
- **Theorem 1.** For constant-elasticity prices absorbed at zero, the defect \(1 - E_Q[V_T^\pi]\), computed by two numerical methods that agree to \(2.5 \times 10^{-6}\), is positive exactly in the cells the theorem predicts, including \(\beta = 0.5\) with \(\pi \in \{0.25, 0.4\}\), where the conjecture fails. It falls continuously to zero as \(\pi\) approaches \(1/2\). For Brownian motion absorbed at zero and \(\pi = 1/4\) it is 0.1535 at \(T = 1\) and 0.3917 at \(T = 5\); for \(\sigma(x) = 1 + x^2\), which is strict and reaches zero, it is positive on both sides of \(\pi = 1/2\).
- **Finite grids.** A fund rebalanced daily, weekly or monthly over a martingale price that reaches zero has zero defect for every exposure, while the continuous wealth's defect is positive (Proposition 11). Over a strict price that does not reach zero, the grid defect lies above the continuous one and approaches it as the grid refines: 0.0624, 0.0535 and 0.0491 for monthly, weekly and daily rebalancing at \(\beta = 1.5\), \(\pi = 0.75\), against 0.0455. With the price stopped on leaving \((1/5, 5)\), the distance between grid fund and continuous wealth shrinks at about the rate \(N^{-1/2}\) in \(N\) rebalancing dates, consistent with the convergence of Proposition 13; on paths absorbed at zero it is unresolved. The bubble sizes behave as uniform integrability requires in every case computed (Proposition 14).
- **Liquidated leveraged funds.** With a liquidation floor monitored continuously, leveraged long funds on two strict prices keep a defect well above zero at daily rebalancing: 0.40 to 0.96 for exposures 1.5 to 3 with the floor at zero, and 0.36 to 0.50 with the floor at half the starting value (standard error at most 0.009), while a geometric Brownian control is within 1.23 standard errors of zero and inverse funds are zero within error. The clipped bounds of Proposition 16 confirm these as lower bounds, within 0.011 of the continuous values, and remain positive (0.16 to 0.96) when the floor is monitored only at every 64th fine point. The bound \(\pi^N\delta_T\) of Proposition 15 is vacuous beyond one interval.
- **The visited integral.** The left side of Proposition 23, computed from \(n\) sampled prices with a kernel local time, has relative error falling from 16 to 43% with a year of daily prices to 2 to 4% with a year of one-minute prices, with a residual bias of about \(-2\%\) and a delta-method interval that undercovers as \(n\) grows (coverage 0.57 at the finest sampling for Brownian motion).

*Scope.* Each item is evidence for the cases computed, at the stated horizons and grids, not a proof.

### 5.8 Recovering basket contributions in synthetic panels

The estimator and compatibility test of Proposition 78 were run in its synthetic sampling experiment, with \(N = M \in \{10^4, 10^5, 10^6\}\) physical panels and pricing draws, 1,000 replicates per cell, \(\alpha_{\mathrm{reg}} = \alpha_{\mathrm{price}} = 0.025\), and both the common radius and the per-cell radius. Two models were used. The first is a two-name terminal-law model with a lognormal pricing law, conditional weights of Beta form and an analytic Lipschitz bound, a compatible null at the boundary \(\theta_1 = \omega_1\), and a tilted alternative with \(\theta_1 = 0.646\) against \(\omega_1 = 0.5\); it satisfies the proposition's premises. The second is a three-name basket priced by exact laws, one constant-elasticity name with \(p = 2\) and \(\kappa = 0.40\), whose recovered contribution is \(b_1 = 0.0041\), and two geometric Brownian names, under the restriction and under weight tilts that exceed \(\omega_i\) by at most 0.019. Its Lipschitz bound is a Monte Carlo finite difference rather than a supplied bound, so its rows are diagnostic.

| Model, cell | \(N = M = 10^4\) | \(10^5\) | \(10^6\) |
|---|---|---|---|
| terminal-law null, both radii | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 | 0 / 0 |
| terminal-law alternative, common / per-cell radius | 0 / 0 | 0 / 100 | 100 / 100 |
| price basket, restriction and all tilts, both radii | 0 | 0 | 0 |

*Scope.* Rejection rates in percent over 1,000 replicates (95% Wilson interval for a zero rate: 0 to 0.4%). Synthetic terminal panels with a supplied pricing law; floating-point evaluation; the terminal-law model is not a process model.

Under the proposition's premises the test made no null rejection in 3,000 replicates per rule, the lower bound covered the target in every replicate, and power appeared from \(N = 10^5\) with the per-cell radius and from \(10^6\) with the common radius; these fixed-partition runs illustrate the size and power statements, they do not prove them. On the price basket neither rule rejects anything, and the separations, at most 0.019, are far below the radii (median common radius 0.16 to 1.0 across cells and sample sizes). Recovery has a persistent bias at fixed bins: about \(+0.0035\) on the constant-elasticity name and \(-0.0016\) to \(-0.0019\) on the others, at every sample size and with 20 or 40 cells, the same size as the contribution \(b_1 = 0.0041\) being recovered. The estimator with the true conditional means on the same pricing sample has bias at most \(10^{-4}\), so the error comes from the fixed-range histogram regression rather than the pricing sample. In the terminal-law model, most of that bias comes from assigning \(\omega\) outside the fixed range (0.0015 of a first-coordinate bias of 0.0016 at the null), which shrinking the cells cannot remove; consistency needs the range to grow as well as the cells to shrink, as Proposition 78(b) requires. The only rigorous Lipschitz bound known for this basket is of order \(10^{16}\), which would saturate every radius.

*Scope.* Non-rejection is not acceptance of the restriction of Proposition 77; the test is a necessary-compatibility test only, and no two-sided interval for the contributions is claimed.

### 5.9 A repaired score

Section 6.8 traces the drift-robust test's silence to its construction: a forecast that is often itself a null law, and a forecast scale fixed on training data against a null refitted on evaluation data. A repaired score gives both sides one model for the nuisance parameters, so that they differ only in the level exponent. Returns follow an asymmetric conditional-variance recursion with standardized Student-t innovations, with variance multiplied by \((S_{t-1}/S_0)^{\beta}\); the null fits \(\beta \in [-10, 0]\) and the alternative \(\beta \in [0, 10]\), each with the same fitter for drift, the recursion's parameters and the degrees of freedom \(\nu \in \{6, 10\}\) on the 252 scored daily returns after 126 training returns. The score is a monotone transform of the fitted log-likelihood gap. It is a numerical likelihood ratio from a finite fitter, not an e-value, so it was calibrated empirically by the conservative rank rule of Proposition 45, on 499 independent calibration paths per reference law, and evaluated on 500 paths per cell. The same rule calibrated the drift-robust score, GSADF, and a likelihood ratio at known nuisance parameters, all on the same paths.

| Cell (calibrated on) | Repaired score | Drift-robust | GSADF | Known nuisance |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| null, two-regime volatility (itself) | 7.4 | 4.6 | 3.4 | 4.0 |
| null, Heston with leverage (itself) | 6.4 | 0.2 | 3.2 | 4.6 |
| \(\beta = 0.25\), \(\nu = 6\) (\(\beta = 0\) reference) | 10.0 | 4.8 | 4.6 | 19.2 |
| \(\beta = 0.5\), \(\nu = 6\) | 19.4 | 4.6 | 8.0 | 43.6 |
| \(\beta = 0.25\), \(\nu = 10\) | 10.8 | 4.6 | 5.0 | 30.8 |
| \(\beta = 0.5\), \(\nu = 10\) | 22.0 | 4.4 | 6.4 | 53.6 |
| CEV \(p = 1.5\) with Heston volatility (Heston) | 8.6 | 0.4 | 3.8 | 6.4 |

*Scope.* Flag rates in percent at \(\alpha = 1/20\) over 500 evaluation paths (standard error about 1 point at 5%; Wilson intervals in the record); each cell reuses one realized calibration sample, so its interval is conditional on that cutoff, and intervals are not simultaneous across cells or scores. Simulated daily returns from a statistical conditional-return model; the known-nuisance ratio fixes the reference law's parameters and is not an oracle bound.

Under calibration on the matching law, the repaired score flags 3.4 to 7.4% of paths in all ten null laws, including laws outside its family (an extreme negative exponent, Student-t with \(\nu = 4\)) and Merton jumps, two-regime volatility and Heston volatility. The edge case is two-regime volatility at 7.4% (5.4 to 10.0%), conditional on its one cutoff; the training-conditional bound of Proposition 45(c) puts the chance that a realized cutoff's risk exceeds 7% at about 0.03, if the reference windows are independent draws from the evaluated law. The drift-robust score's matched rates are often far below \(\alpha\) because many calibration windows score exactly zero, the null-member forecasts of Section 6.8, so its cutoff lands on a tie. Against in-model level alternatives the repaired score flags 10 to 11% at \(\beta = 0.25\) and 19 to 22% at \(\beta = 0.5\), against 4 to 8% for the drift-robust score and calibrated GSADF; on the same paths, flags by the repaired score alone outnumber flags by GSADF alone 45 to 18, 73 to 16, 50 to 21 and 93 to 15, a finite-case advantage, not uniform superiority. These rates are modest, two to four times \(\alpha\), and against a constant-elasticity diffusion with \(p = 1.5\) and Heston volatility the score flags 8.6% against 6.4% on Heston itself: the level effect is weak and easily absorbed by a flexible volatility state, as Section 6.8 found on real data.

Transporting the calibration to a mismatched law is mostly conservative: cutoffs from the \(\beta = 0\) reference give 1.0 to 3.2% on negative exponents, lower persistence, jumps and Heston. But on two-regime volatility they give 9.0% (6.8 to 11.8%), so validity on real data rests on the premise of that remark that the reference windows share the evaluated window's law after selection; matching the volatility regime alone does not establish it. A sequential version, with forecasts refitted from past returns only, had a negative endpoint log ratio on all but one of 744 diagnostic paths (medians \(-5.0\) to \(-11.4\)), dominated by the cost of fitting the nuisance from the past; the one exception, at 7.52 on a \(\beta = 0.5\) path, is uncertified, crossings before the endpoint were not computed, and no sequential test is validated.

*Scope.* A pilot in simulation only; no certified profile or sequential e-value; the fitter's boundary selections are numerical near-ties, not features of the data. Whether calibration on real reference windows meets the premise above is the decisive open question.

### 5.10 A daily statistic with a market scale

Section 6.11 found that controlling a stock's own slowly moving volatility understates a level effect. A daily statistic was therefore built whose volatility scale comes from the market instead. The variance of a daily log return is \(w\, m_t^{g} \exp(\beta x)\), where \(m_t\) is the mean squared SPY return over the previous 20 sessions, normalized once on training data; \(x\) is the log price relative to the start of scoring; and \(\beta\) is the full exponent of variance in the price, so that a CEV diffusion with exponent \(p\) has \(\beta = 2(p - 1)\). Innovations are Student-t. The nuisance \((\mu, w, g, \nu)\) is fitted at \(\beta = 0\) on the 252 prior returns and then frozen, so that only \(\beta\) differs between the two sides. The batch score compares the best \(\beta \ge 0\) with the best \(\beta \le 0\) over 252 scored days. A daily monitor accumulates an equally weighted mixture over eight values of \(\beta\) from 1/8 to 16 against the profiled \(\beta \le 0\) maximum and alarms at its first crossing. Each is calibrated by the conservative rank rule of Proposition 45 on 499 paths of a null law, and evaluated on 500. GSADF and the repaired score of Section 5.9 were calibrated the same way on the same paths; the repaired score was run on only 100 calibration and 100 evaluation paths per cell, for runtime.

The laws are of three kinds. In the statistic's own family, the market's log variance is autoregressive and the stock's variance is \(2.25\) times the market's times \(\exp(\beta x)\), with \(\beta \in \{-1, 0, 1, 2\}\). CEV diffusions with \(p \in \{1, 1.5, 2\}\) were simulated with constant volatility, with a stochastic-volatility factor that also drives the market, or with the stock's own stochastic volatility, each at a correlation \(\rho \in \{-0.7, 0, 0.7\}\) between price and volatility shocks. And the ten null laws of Section 5.9 were rerun with a market attached. Each alternative is calibrated on the null with the same volatility, correlation and market (\(p = 1\) for the CEV cells).

| Law (calibrated on its null) | Daily statistic | No market scale | GSADF | Repaired score |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| own family, \(\beta = 1\), \(\nu = 6\) | 39 | 19 | 7 | 27 |
| own family, \(\beta = 2\), \(\nu = 6\) | 66 | 41 | 11 | 52 |
| own family, \(\beta = 2\), \(\nu = 10\) | 71 | 32 | 14 | 21 |
| CEV \(p = 1.5\), constant volatility | 41 | 42 | 6 | 11 |
| CEV \(p = 2\), constant volatility | 72 | 74 | 10 | 31 |
| CEV \(p = 2\), market volatility, \(\rho = 0\) | 23 | 12 | 11 | 44 |
| CEV \(p = 2\), own volatility, \(\rho = 0\) | 10 | 11 | 10 | 16 |
| CEV \(p = 2\), own volatility, \(\rho = 0.7\) | 20 | 20 | 8 | 28 |

*Scope.* Percentages of 500 evaluation paths flagged at \(\alpha = 1/20\) by the batch score (standard error at most 2.2 points); the repaired score's column uses 100 paths and a 100-path calibration (standard error up to 5 points), and its matched null rates scatter from 1 to 18% (that tier omitted a few early simulation failures in three null laws). Each cell reuses one realized calibration sample. Discretized diffusions (10 steps a day; 50 steps change the fitted \(\beta\) by at most 0.07 in the median) are not certified strict-local-martingale samples, and a Student-t return model is not a pricing law.

In its own family the statistic recovers a known exponent. Every estimate is finite, and the median error is at most 6% of the truth. The positive share is at least 0.88 in every cell but one. In that cell \(\beta = 1\) is already active in the training year, and only 79% of estimates are positive, against 90% with the true nuisance; the fit at \(\beta = 0\) plausibly absorbs part of the effect, though that cause is not isolated. On constant-volatility CEV it reports 1.02 at \(p = 1.5\) and 1.89 at \(p = 2\). Under calibration on the matching law, its flag rate averages 4.85% (batch) and 4.61% (monitor) over 22 null laws, with four single-cell rates whose Wilson intervals lie above 5% (7.0 to 8.8%) and one stress law, an exponent of \(-40\), whose 15% simulation failures leave it uninformative. An average near nominal is not a guarantee for every law. Its power is 37 to 72% in its own family and on constant-volatility CEV, far above GSADF over these 252-day windows (2 to 14% in every alternative). Without the market scale the power roughly halves in its own family and under market-driven volatility. Under the stock's own stochastic volatility, power at \(p = 2\) is only 10 to 20%. The monitor's rates are a little lower than the batch score's. Among alarmed paths its median first alarm comes on day 130 to 170 of 252, against 24 to 135 for GSADF, which alarms far less often.

The fitted exponent is not specific to a level effect. With no level effect (\(p = 1\)) and the stock's own volatility correlated \(+0.7\) with its price, the median estimate is 2.54 (76% positive), and \(-2.58\) at \(\rho = -0.7\). The market-driven cells give 1.00 and \(-1.12\). An independent implementation on fresh paths gives 2.58. So a positive daily estimate is equally consistent with correlated stochastic volatility, the limit found within the day in Section 6.11. The calibration does not transport across volatility laws either. A cutoff set on the in-family \(\beta = 0\) null flags 8 to 70% of paths when applied to nulls with the stock's own volatility clustering, positive correlation or regime switching (for example 33% on the two-regime law of Section 5.9, and 63% under own volatility at \(\rho = 0.7\)), though only 3.8% under Merton jumps. A real-data reading therefore needs references with the target's own kind of volatility history, and even then the transport error is unknown.

*Scope.* Simulation only. The gates on recovery and calibration were declared in advance but are exploratory and not simultaneous. Matched calibration is the exchangeable case of the rank rule for complete simulated paths, and the transported rates measure misspecification.

## 6. Real-data results

This section applies the tests to real prices. Three caveats govern every number in it. The labels are consensus, not ground truth: a "positive" episode is one that the cited literature or the press calls a bubble, not one shown to be a strict local martingale under any pricing law, and a "negative" control is calm by a rule fixed before its window, not certified free of bubbles. A flag rate on positives is therefore not power, and one on negatives is not size. And every application lies outside the guarantees of Section 3.5: real prices are rounded, are not known to follow a law in any null of that section, and are not known to have \(P = Q\). Each series is normalized by its first price in the window, and the tests are those of Section 5 with the same catalogue and split; all designs, episode lists and control rules were fixed before any test was run on them.

### 6.1 The data and their limits

Free daily closes cover most listed names, but not those that failed. Reconstructing the S&P 500 membership month by month, 57 of 506 members in January 2020 have no free daily series, falling to 40 of 505 by December 2021, 27 of 503 in January 2023 and none today. About one member in ten is missing in 2020–21, against one in 33 over 2023–26. The missing names are not random: about half of the 2020–21 losses were acquisitions, and the losses include failed banks such as Silicon Valley Bank, First Republic and Signature. For scale, Greenwood and Sammon (`greenwood2022disappearing`) list 750 S&P 500 deletions between 1980 and 2020 and match 749 of them to licensed price data; free daily prices exist for few of those names.

Daily data see the range of prices visited but not the time spent near its top. In a two-week sample of one-minute bars, SPY spanned 2.1% of its level and QQQ 3.6%; ten daily closes show only 1.4% and 1.8%. Within 1% of the one-minute maximum, QQQ spent 429 regular-hours minutes (11% of the time), which daily closes record as 2 days of 10. A volatility tail can be estimated only on the visited range, and the local-time weights of Proposition 23 are thinnest at its top.

The option and fund inputs of Sections 3.3 and 4 are measurable only coarsely. On end-of-day SPY chains, the largest-strike call at one to three months is quoted at the one-cent floor on most dates, at median strikes of 1.07 to 1.41 times the price, so these data bound the far wing only to the tick and cannot show how call prices decay in the strike. For eight leveraged and inverse exchange-traded funds on QQQ and SPY, the cost beyond the expense ratio, measured against the total-return underlying, is 0.6 to 1.7% a year for the leveraged long funds and 0.1 to 1.1% for the inverse ones (standard errors 0.1 to 0.4% a year), and tracking error compounds to about 0.5 to 1.2% a year; against the price index instead, dividends would be misread as cost.

### 6.2 Consensus episodes on daily data

The positive controls are nine episodes: the Nasdaq indices in 1995–2001; surviving dot-com leaders; US homebuilders and US financials in 2003–08; the 2021 meme stocks; the 2026 run in SK Hynix; the Nikkei in 1986–90; the Shanghai Composite in 2014–15; and the 2020–21 growth names. Each has 1 to 8 names, 33 in all. Negative controls follow a mechanical rule using only data before each window: for each episode's window length, the three calmest non-overlapping start dates by trailing S&P 500 volatility and drawdown (one for the longest windows), each with two indices and five Dow members, giving 103 names in 15 windows. Each window is tested in full, and also cut at the first name's maximum; that end date is chosen by the outcome, so the cut windows are outside every guarantee.

| Set | Window | Names | Drift-robust, 5% | \(E\), 5% | SADF, 5% | GSADF, 5% | JKP Method 2 | JKP Method 1 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| positive | full | 33 | 0.06 | 0.27 | 0.76 | 0.64 | 1.00 | 0.06 |
| positive | to the peak | 32 | 0.09 | 0.31 | 0.72 | 0.62 | 0.94 | 0.09 |
| negative | full | 103 | 0.00 | 0.01 | 0.16 | 0.19 | 0.85 | 0.04 |
| negative | to the peak | 103 | 0.00 | 0.02 | 0.18 | 0.19 | 0.91 | 0.09 |

*Scope.* Shares of names flagged, on adjusted daily closes. The binomial standard errors of the differences between positives and negatives are 0.03 to 0.09; names within a row, and rows sharing windows, are dependent. The surviving-leader, financial and meme rows contain only survivors, and the 1990–94 negative windows lost 11 to 13 Dow members to missing data.

The drift-robust test flags no negative control, and almost no positive one. Its largest log score on a negative is 1.07, against the threshold \(\log 20 = 3.00\). It flags two positives in full windows (BlackBerry and SK Hynix) and three to the peak (GameStop, Koss and BlackBerry), all from the meme and SK Hynix rows, and none of the dot-com, housing, financial, Japanese, Chinese or growth names. The flagged meme windows contain the January 2021 squeeze, a jump no catalogue law has, and in Section 5.2 Merton jumps produced false alarms; these data cannot tell a strict local martingale from a jump. The raw score \(E\) flags 27 to 31% of positives and 1 to 2% of negatives. SADF and GSADF separate the groups most (differences 0.43 to 0.66, standard error about 0.09), but flag 13 to 19% of the calm controls at nominal 5%, mostly in the long bull markets after 2009 and in 2014–18: they detect persistent upward trends, which calm rising markets also have. JKP's Method 2 flags almost everything and Method 1 almost nothing. The panel t test flags no positive row in full windows; cut at the peak it flags six of nine positive rows and three or four of 15 negative windows, because cutting at a maximum manufactures an explosive end.

Adding the names that actually collapsed does not change this. Ten collapsed names with free daily data were added to the dot-com and financial rows: InfoSpace, CMGI, JDS Uniphase, VA Linux and theglobe.com; Lehman, Wachovia, Countrywide, Merrill Lynch and Freddie Mac. The drift-robust test flags none of the ten in either window, with log scores from \(-1.6\) to \(-2{,}473\). \(E\) flags three of the five dot-com names and none of the financials, as it did the survivors. SADF and GSADF flag three or four of the five dot-com names and GSADF three of the five financials, but they flag 14 to 43% of the matched calm controls, so they separate the groups only weakly; JKP's Method 2 says bubble on most names in every group. The early split-adjusted prices of some of these names are rounded to the cent, with many zero returns, and the larger raw and explosive-root statistics rest partly on those segments.

JKP's published verdicts could be checked at daily frequency for two of their four dot-com stocks. On daily closes, Method 2 calls InfoSpace a bubble, as JKP did on tick bid prices, but with fitted exponents of 1.27 and 1.14 against their 6.17 and 3.58; it also calls GeoCities a bubble, where JKP found none. With 945 and 202 daily prices the method has two and one grid points, so these verdicts are near-degenerate and are not evidence about JKP's tick-data results. The drift-robust test flags neither name, and SADF and GSADF flag both.

### 6.3 Hourly data

Free hourly bars cover about two years, which contains one positive control, SK Hynix, and one calm negative window of seven names. Hourly, the drift-robust test on calendar time flags SK Hynix in both windows (log scores 138.6 and 125.5), but it also flags Coca-Cola in the calm window (log score 12.8, kept by e-BH), a false alarm under the consensus labels that does not recur in the cut window. The first hour of the session carries 60 to 69% of the day's variance time in these data, a clock that the calendar-time null ignores; Section 5.4 found that ignoring the clock cost power in simulation, and here it also produced a false alarm. The declared-clock score of Proposition 34, which has no guarantee for rounded prices, flags nothing in either group, and on SK Hynix is strongly negative (\(-5{,}762\)): its forecast, fitted on the first half, chose a null-type exponent, and the price then rose about thirteenfold. SADF and GSADF flag SK Hynix and no calm name at 5%. The certified filtered score of Propositions 66–68 also flags nothing among the calm names; it certified each two-year denominator to within 0.7 nats in 24 to 52 seconds, or stopped at its computation budget with valid bounds.

Recent run-ups were then tested on the same hourly window: 21 names that the press called bubbles or manias (among them GameStop and AMC in 2024, Super Micro, Palantir, Nvidia, the quantum-computing and nuclear names, and the crypto-treasury companies), 8 further crypto-linked and mega-cap names, and 98 calm large caps over the same dates. The registered rule for calm windows before the run-ups left none, so the controls overlap the positives in time.

| Group | Names | Drift-robust | Clock | Certified | SADF | GSADF | JKP 2 |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| bubble-narrative names | 21 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 12 | 18 | 20 |
| crypto-linked and other | 8 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 4 | 6 | 8 |
| calm large caps | 98 | 0 | 0 | 0 | 4 | 13 | 58 |

*Scope.* Counts of names flagged at 5% on hourly bars over about two years, by the drift-robust test, the declared-clock score, the certified filtered score, SADF, GSADF and JKP's Method 2; e-BH rejected nothing in any group. Press labels; rounded prices, for which only the certified score has a guarantee, and that only inside its declared box.

The likelihood tests flag nothing, but on the positives this silence is mostly a limitation of the forecast. Their scores on the positives are far below zero, down to \(-106{,}126\), because the forecast's scales stop at 0.45 at the starting price while these names moved several times faster. In a follow-up registered before it was run, on these already inspected records, with the forecast's scales widened up to 4 and everything else unchanged (the larger of the old and new scores is never used), the scores rise by orders of magnitude, and most named run-ups then look like a Gaussian law with a free scale. The drift-robust test still flags nothing. The clock score flags only GameStop (log score 21.5), whose 2024 window is dominated by jumps: about half its hourly realized variance is jump-like (the bipower variation of Barndorff-Nielsen and Shephard `barndorffnielsen2005econometrics`, which separates the continuous part of the variation from jumps, is 0.49 of realized variance), one hourly return carries 27% of it and five carry 69 to 78%, and its volatility in the second half was 2.23 against 0.65 in the first. At hourly sampling a burst of volatility can be mistaken for a jump (`bajgrowicz2009jumps`), so this split is descriptive. The certified score flags seven names, but every one has volatility above its declared box's 0.80 (from 0.91 to 3.64), so these are failures of the box, as for the out-of-box null of Section 5.4, not evidence of bubbles. The calm controls stay unflagged in every column. GSADF separates the groups best (18 of 21 against 13 of 98) and JKP's Method 2 not at all, neither with a guarantee here.

Daily evidence does not accumulate on high-range days. Splitting each hourly record into daily contributions under the daily-reset score of Proposition 37, a day's contribution is positive on at most 1% of days in any band of daily range, and it grows more negative as the range widens, from about \(-4\) on days with a range under 2% to \(-760\) on days above 20% among the positives. GameStop's and AMC's days with a range of 10% or more sum to \(-852\) and \(-1{,}464\). With this forecast, the most volatile days carry the most evidence against a bubble, not for one; whether a forecast that can follow larger scales changes that was not tested.

*Scope.* One positive and seven negatives for the first comparison, and press labels for the second, so no rate is estimable; rounded hourly bars, with 23 to 54% of the closes from sub-penny trades in the first comparison.

### 6.4 An unselected universe

The separations of Section 6.2 are selected: the episodes and their cut-off ends were chosen with hindsight. To remove that selection, the drift-robust score \(E^{\mathrm{drift}}\), the raw score \(E\) and the GSADF statistic were computed on every window of a rolling grid fixed in advance: the 503 current S&P 500 constituents, windows of 13, 24 and 72 months starting on the first trading day of every month from January 1995, each kept when the name's adjusted daily closes cover at least 99% of the window and of the 126 trading days after it. That gives 416,380 name-windows. The outcomes are the maximum drawdown over the next 63 and 126 trading days, with means 0.13–0.14 and 0.18–0.19, and the forward log return. Each score enters a regression as its percentile rank among the windows of its start date. Standard errors are Driscoll–Kraay with \(1.5L\) monthly lags for window length \(L\), longer than both the horizon and the overlap of adjacent windows; the out-of-sample \(R^2\) uses coefficients from starts before 2011 on starts from 2011, with a block-bootstrap standard error.

| Window length | Drift-robust | \(E\) | GSADF |
|---|---|---|---|
| 13 months | \(-0.027\) [\(-4.3\)]; \(-0.001 \pm 0.002\) | \(-0.007\) [\(-0.7\)]; \(-0.004 \pm 0.001\) | \(+0.028\) [6.4]; \(+0.007 \pm 0.001\) |
| 24 months | \(-0.023\) [\(-2.3\)]; \(-0.003 \pm 0.004\) | \(-0.004\) [\(-0.3\)]; \(-0.001 \pm 0.001\) | \(+0.034\) [6.8]; \(+0.009 \pm 0.001\) |
| 72 months | \(-0.017\) [\(-2.0\)]; \(-0.006 \pm 0.001\) | \(-0.002\) [\(-0.3\)]; \(-0.001 \pm 0.000\) | \(+0.046\) [8.4]; \(+0.013 \pm 0.002\) |

*Scope.* For the 126-day forward maximum drawdown: the change in drawdown from the lowest to the highest rank, its Driscoll–Kraay t in brackets, then the out-of-sample \(R^2\) with its bootstrap standard error. Current constituents only; adjusted daily closes; no guarantee for any of the scores on real prices.

| Window length | Period of window end | Windows | Drift-robust | \(E\) | GSADF |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 13 months | 1995–99 | 14,780 | 0.17% | 4.2% | 21.2% |
| 13 months | 2003–07 | 22,740 | 0.11% | 1.7% | 12.9% |
| 13 months | 2009–19 | 58,664 | 0.08% | 0.6% | 12.9% |
| 13 months | 2023–26 | 19,291 | 0.15% | 1.6% | 11.5% |
| 24 months | 1995–99 | 11,183 | 0.38% | 5.0% | 27.7% |
| 24 months | 2003–07 | 22,371 | 0.23% | 1.9% | 16.1% |
| 24 months | 2009–19 | 57,883 | 0.10% | 0.7% | 14.6% |
| 24 months | 2023–26 | 19,201 | 0.18% | 2.0% | 13.9% |
| 72 months | 2003–07 | 20,277 | 0.25% | 2.8% | 33.9% |
| 72 months | 2009–19 | 54,052 | 0.13% | 0.7% | 23.9% |
| 72 months | 2023–26 | 18,566 | 0.06% | 0.6% | 41.7% |

*Scope.* Share of windows flagged at the nominal level: a log score above \(\log 20\), or GSADF above its simulated 95% critical value. Windows overlap heavily, so these shares are descriptive.

The drift-robust test almost never fires: it flags 0.11%, 0.17% and 0.24% of all windows at the three lengths, and at most 2.5% in any single year (72-month windows ending in 2000, 285 of them). Where it ranks windows, the ranking does not warn: a higher rank goes with smaller forward drawdowns, its out-of-sample \(R^2\) is negative for every drawdown outcome, and for returns it is at most \(0.0001\). Its log score is negative on 90 to 92% of windows and exactly zero on 7 to 10%, so the rank mostly orders how strongly a window favours the null. The raw score \(E\) flags 1.7 to 2.1% of windows, up to 13.4% for 13-month windows ending in 2000, and has no association with the outcomes (\(|t| \le 1.4\), out-of-sample \(R^2 \le 0\)). GSADF flags 15.3%, 19.0% and 33.2% of windows, 13 to 24% in the calm market of 2009–19, and 44 to 52% of the windows ending in 2020 and 2021. Its rank has a small, clear and out-of-sample association with forward drawdowns: 2.2 to 4.6 percentage points from the lowest to the highest rank, \(t\) between 6.4 and 8.6, and out-of-sample \(R^2\) of 0.006 to 0.014. It also goes with slightly higher forward returns (\(t\) from 1.9 to 2.4), which fits momentum rather than a crash warning. With all three scores together, GSADF's coefficient is unchanged, the two likelihood scores split by collinearity (their ranks correlate 0.77 to 0.95), and the joint out-of-sample \(R^2\) is below GSADF's alone.

On the records of Section 6.2, a comparison that is optimistic because those episodes were chosen with hindsight, the probability that a consensus positive outscores a calm control is 0.16 for the drift-robust score (95% bootstrap interval 0.05 to 0.34), 0.30 for \(E\) (0.05 to 0.64) and 0.84 for GSADF (0.70 to 0.995). At the controls' 99th percentile, the three flag 6.1%, 27.3% and 45.5% of the positives. The drift-robust score ranks the consensus bubbles below the calm controls: its log score is more negative on them.

*Scope.* Current constituents only: names removed from the index or failed, which tend to have large drawdowns, are absent, which the design expected to work against finding drawdown predictability; its effect on any particular estimate is not established. The per-window scores and outcomes and the headline slopes, out-of-sample \(R^2\) and flag rates were reproduced independently; the combined regression, the bootstrap standard errors and the comparison on the episode records were not recomputed. GSADF is an explosive-root statistic, not a strict-local-martingale test.

### 6.5 Empirical critical values for GSADF

GSADF's nominal critical values come from a homoskedastic random walk, and on the calm controls above they flag 12 to 30% of records. Its critical values can instead be set from the calm windows themselves. Each record's GSADF statistic was turned into a p-value against 2,000 simulated random walks of the record's own length, and the threshold at level \(\alpha\) is the \(\alpha\)-quantile of the calm records' p-values, separately for daily and hourly data. Size is measured on held-out windows: each calm window's names are flagged at the threshold computed from the other windows (14 daily or 13 hourly), and the interval comes from a bootstrap that resamples whole windows. The records are those of Sections 6.2 and 6.3, with no new data: 103 calm and 33 positive daily names, the 10 collapsed names, and 98 calm and 29 run-up hourly names.

| Records | \(\alpha\) | Held-out size | 95% interval | Nominal, calm | Positives |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| daily, full | 0.05 | 6.8% | 1.9–12.5% | 21.4% | 63.6% of 33 |
| daily, full | 0.10 | 10.7% | 4.9–17.5% | 30.1% | 63.6% of 33 |
| daily, to the peak | 0.05 | 6.8% | 1.0–14.3% | 19.4% | 59.4% of 32 |
| hourly | 0.05 | 7.1% | 2.0–13.3% | 12.2% | 76.2% of 21 |
| hourly | 0.10 | 10.2% | 4.1–18.4% | 24.5% | 85.7% of 21 |

*Scope.* Held-out size by leaving one calm window out, with a window-cluster bootstrap interval; the nominal critical value's flag rate on the same calm records; the positives' flag rate at the threshold calibrated on all calm windows. Only 15 daily and 14 hourly calm windows, which are dependent; consensus labels chosen with hindsight; the to-peak ends are chosen by the outcome.

With empirical thresholds (p-values at or below 0.0135 daily and 0.0084 hourly at \(\alpha = 0.05\)), GSADF's held-out false-alarm rate falls to about its nominal level, and every interval covers \(\alpha\). These thresholds are empirical calibrations, not guaranteed sizes: the calm windows were selected by their own trailing volatility and drawdown, a past-only selection that does not by itself make a calibrated threshold valid (Proposition 45(d)), and the thresholds are interpolated quantiles rather than the conservative rank rule of that proposition. It still flags 59 to 64% of the daily consensus positives and 76% of the hourly run-ups, and at most a 5% false-alarm rate on calm records it reaches 64%, 59% and 76% of them. Most of that separation comes from records whose statistic exceeds every simulated null: 15 of 33 daily and 12 of 21 hourly positives, against at most 2 calm records. Among the collapsed names, it flags 4 of the 5 dot-com names and 1 of the 5 financials; among the hourly crypto-linked names, 4 of 8. The probability that a positive's p-value is below a calm one's, optimistic because the positives were chosen with hindsight, is 0.82 for daily full windows (95% interval 0.68 to 0.99), 0.82 to the peak (0.63 to 0.99) and 0.91 hourly; the hourly figure has no reliable interval, because all 21 hourly positives come from one list and the bootstrap cannot vary them as a group. On the same labelled records, the drift-robust score ranks the positives below the calm controls (Section 6.4), so calibrated GSADF is the benchmark the likelihood scores do not meet.

*Scope.* GSADF tests an explosive autoregressive root, not a strict local martingale; the p-values rest on simulated nulls of 2,000 replications, which were spot-checked, not regenerated, in the reproduction.

The calibrated threshold was then applied, unchanged, to the 416,380 windows of the unselected universe of Section 6.4, whose GSADF p-values are computed the same way. These windows are unlabelled, so a flag rate there is not a false-alarm rate.

| Window length | All windows | 1995–99 | 2003–07 | 2009–19 | 2023–26 | Highest year |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| 13 months | 8.2% | 11.7% | 6.0% | 6.4% | 5.5% | 2020: 30.9% |
| 24 months | 10.9% | 16.1% | 8.5% | 7.4% | 6.8% | 2021: 34.1% |
| 72 months | 23.0% | | 23.4% | 14.8% | 31.6% | 2021: 38.2% |

*Scope.* Share of windows flagged by period of the window's end at the calibrated threshold for \(\alpha = 0.05\) (p-value at most 0.0135); the nominal rates are in the second table of Section 6.4. Overlapping windows; current constituents only.

Against the nominal rates of 15.3%, 19.0% and 33.2% over all windows, calibration roughly halves the flag rate at 13- and 24-month windows, to 6 to 7% in the calm years 2009–19, close to the held-out 5% target, but it still flags about 15% of 72-month windows in those years. The calm windows behind the threshold are shorter than 72 months, and a random-walk p-value is not free of window length on real prices, so the calibration does not transfer evenly across lengths. Used as a 0/1 flag, calibrated GSADF keeps the association of Section 6.4: windows it flags have forward maximum drawdowns about 3 percentage points larger than unflagged ones (for example 0.166 against 0.138 over 63 days for 13-month windows), with Driscoll–Kraay \(t\) between 3.1 and 5.5, an out-of-sample \(R^2\) of 0.005 to 0.011 (bootstrap standard errors 0.003 to 0.012, so not distinguishable from zero at 24 months), and no reliable association with forward returns (\(t \le 1.7\)). It is a modest risk screen, not a reliable bubble detector.

### 6.6 Jumps behind the flags

A flag can come from a jump rather than from a sustained change in the price's dynamics, and a monitor should say which. Every flag at 5% reported in Sections 6.2 and 6.3 by the drift-robust score, \(E\), SADF, GSADF or the declared-clock score, 185 in all, was described by three statistics of its record's log returns: the jump share \(1 - BV/RV\), with bipower variation \(BV = (\pi/2)\sum_j|r_j||r_{j-1}|\) and realized variance \(RV\); the share of \(RV\) in the five largest squared returns; and the ratio of the second half's volatility to the first half's. Each was compared with the same statistics on that data set's calm controls, and with 2,000 Gaussian paths of constant volatility as a no-jump reference. Then a jump filter stated in advance set to zero every return larger than four local bipower standard deviations, from 42 neighbouring returns, the price path was rebuilt, and every record, positives and controls alike, was scored again with the same code.

| Records | Column | Flags | Survive the filter |
|---|---|---|---|
| daily consensus episodes, full windows | SADF / GSADF / \(E\) / drift-robust | 25 / 21 / 9 / 2 | 23 / 20 / 7 / 1 |
| daily consensus episodes, to the peak | SADF / GSADF / \(E\) / drift-robust | 23 / 20 / 10 / 3 | 21 / 19 / 6 / 1 |
| daily collapsed names | SADF / GSADF / \(E\) | 9 / 11 / 5 | 6 / 8 / 3 |
| hourly SK Hynix, full window | SADF / GSADF / drift-robust | 1 / 1 / 1 | 1 / 1 / 0 |
| hourly SK Hynix, to the peak | SADF / GSADF / drift-robust | 1 / 1 / 1 | 1 / 1 / 1 |
| hourly run-ups | SADF / GSADF | 16 / 24 | 14 / 22 |
| hourly GameStop, widened forecast | clock score | 1 | 0 |

*Scope.* A flag survives when the same column still flags the record after the detected jump returns are set to zero. The filter is a stated heuristic without a proven size, it uses returns on both sides of each date, and by Proposition 54 zeroing returns breaks the scores' calibration, so the filtered scores are descriptive only. In an independent reimplementation, which read the filter's window slightly differently, two survival counts differ by one and the reading is unchanged. The certified score's flags of Section 6.3, all outside its box, were described but not rescored. Consensus labels.

Most flags are not explained by jumps: 156 of the 185 survive the filter, including 137 of the 153 explosive-root flags and 16 of the 24 flags of \(E\). The exceptions are where earlier sections pointed: GameStop's widened clock-score flag, whose record has a jump share of 0.51 and five returns carrying 69% of its variance; SK Hynix's full-window hourly flag by the drift-robust test; and several daily flags of \(E\). What sets the flagged daily records apart from the calm controls is rising volatility, not jumps: their median rank among the controls is 0.55 in jump share and 0.63 in the top-five share, but 1.00 in the second-half to first-half volatility ratio, and 69% of daily flags lie above the controls' 95th percentile of that ratio. On hourly records jumps and volatility changes both contribute, and neither separates the flags cleanly. The controls themselves are far from the no-jump reference: daily calm records have a median jump share of 0.07, against about zero for Gaussian paths, and hourly ones 0.28 to 0.31, inflated because bipower variation pairs the regular-hour returns with the much larger overnight return. Filtering does not improve calibration: on the daily calm controls it raises GSADF's flag rate from 19.4% to 25.2% and SADF's from 15.5% to 21.4%, and on the hourly controls it lowers GSADF's from 13.3% to 7.1% but raises SADF's from 4.1% to 8.2%. The drift-robust test's hourly false alarm on Coca-Cola disappears after filtering, and SADF then flags another calm name.

### 6.7 Index data with implied volatility

The implied-volatility null of Proposition 35 was applied to daily index closes, with the published implied-volatility index as the scale: SPX with VIX from 1990, and NDX with VXN from 2001. The return from the close of day \(t - 1\) to the close of day \(t\) uses the volatility index's close on day \(t - 2\), because the index's own close is published after the return interval begins; the close of day \(t - 1\) was run as a sensitivity and gave the same counts. VIX values before September 2003 were reconstructed later from historical option quotes, so SPX windows before then are a retrospective analysis, not a record of what could have been computed at the time. The score, bands and forecast are those of Section 5.5. Rolling windows of 126 training and 252 scored returns start each quarter, and a window is called calm, before any score, when its scored year has a drawdown below 10% and does not overlap a recession.

| Index | Windows | Calm windows | Crossings, primary band [0.3, 1.0] | Crossings, band [0.5, 1.0] | GSADF flags, all / calm |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| SPX | 141 | 65 | 0 | 1 at 5%, 2 at 10% (not calm) | 13.5% / 0% |
| NDX | 97 | 14 | 0 | 0 | 0% / 0% |

*Scope.* Anytime crossings of \(1/\alpha = 20\) in any window. Overlapping quarterly windows, so the counts are descriptive; the calm label uses the scored year's own drawdown, a hindsight label that does not give a size guarantee (Proposition 45(d)).

The score never crosses at \(\alpha = 0.05\) or \(0.10\) in the primary band or in \([0.25, 1.25]\), in calm or stressed windows, including 2008 and March 2020; its largest running maximum is \(1.54\) on SPX and \(1.30\) on NDX, against \(\log 20 = 3.00\). The crossing in the narrow band at \(\alpha = 0.05\) is an SPX year scored from April 2017 that contains the volatility spike of February 2018, after a year in which realized variance was low against implied; a ratio below that band or a jump would each be outside the null, but the crossing does not identify which, or a bubble. Through the episodes it never crosses either: on SPX from 1999 to 2001 its maximum is \(-0.06\) and its final value \(-5.14\), and on SPX and NDX in 2020–21 its finals are \(-6.98\) and \(-2.83\). GSADF flags the 1999–2001 SPX record and 19 SPX windows, scored in years ending 2001–03 and 2015–19, but no NDX window. The ratio of realized to implied variance, over each window's scored year, has median \(0.62\) on SPX and \(0.74\) on NDX; 21% of SPX windows fall below 0.5, 6 to 16% exceed 1, four SPX and two NDX windows exceed 1.25, outside every band (windows ending in late 2008 and in 2020), and none falls below 0.3; a sample ratio inside or outside a band neither certifies nor refutes a constant conditional ratio. So under these fixed forecasts, bands, thresholds and windows, no departure is detected from level dependence that the implied-volatility index already prices, the outcome the absorption of Section 5.5 leads one to expect. No calibration of the score on historical indices follows from the zero count, and the two indices and the overlapping windows are not independent trials. It is not evidence of no bubble, nor that implied volatility prices all level dependence, nor that individual stocks had no bubble.

*Scope.* Two indices; daily closes, with no rounding model; floating-point scores, not certified; retrospective VIX before 2003; VXN starts after the 2000 peak, so the NDX dot-com episode cannot be tested.

### 6.8 Why the drift-robust test is silent

The drift-robust test almost never fires on real prices (Section 6.4). A diagnosis on data already in hand separates two explanations: no signal, or a score built so that it cannot show one. It uses the 33 consensus positives and 103 calm controls of Section 6.2 and 3,000 sampled 24-month windows of the unselected universe of Section 6.4, of which 297 are run-ups (the top tenth of their start date by return). First, the log score is split exactly into four terms: the misfit of the training-chosen scale \(\kappa\) on the evaluation half; the level dependence, the fitted exponent against geometric Brownian motion with each at its best scale on the evaluation half; the drift, zero drift against a fitted one; and the enlargement of the null.

| Records | Log score | Scale misfit | Level dependence | Drift | Null enlargement |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| consensus positives | \(-229.6\) | \(-228.2\) | \(-1.47\) | \(-0.32\) | 0.00 |
| calm controls | \(-18.1\) | \(-17.6\) | \(+0.33\) | \(-0.50\) | 0.00 |
| unselected windows | \(-12.1\) | \(-10.6\) | \(+0.27\) | \(-0.28\) | 0.00 |
| run-ups | \(-18.4\) | \(-16.7\) | 0.00 | \(-1.06\) | 0.00 |

*Scope.* Medians in nats; the four terms sum to the log score on every record. Daily adjusted closes; consensus labels; survivorship as in Section 6.4.

Two features of the construction keep the score silent. First, the forecast chosen on the training half is often itself a null law, with \(p \le 1\): in 15 of 33 consensus positives, 88 of 103 calm controls, 2,287 of 3,000 unselected windows and 192 of 297 run-ups. The null contains it, so the score cannot exceed one on those records. Second, the scale misfit: the forecast's scale is chosen on the training half from \(\{0.2, 0.3, 0.45\}\) with zero drift, while the null refits its scale and drift on the evaluation half. This term is the largest negative one in 76 to 83% of records and, in the median, 88 to 99% of the score, which is why the score is most negative on the consensus bubbles. It is a retrospective penalty for the fit of the scale: it does not separate a change of volatility between the halves from the coarseness of the three-point grid, and refitting the scale on the evaluation half is a diagnostic, not a valid repaired forecast.

Removing that asymmetry does not reveal a strong level signal. Even in sample, with every parameter fitted on the evaluation half, a zero-drift constant-elasticity alternative with \(p > 1\) and a free scale beats the drift-robust null, whose constant-elasticity scales are then also free, by more than \(\log 20\) in only 4 to 9% of records, and on the consensus positives it loses by 38 nats in the median. A local-lognormal model with free drift, scale and variance exponent \(\beta \in [-20, 20]\) (variance proportional to the price to the power \(\beta\), twice the exponent of the volatility), on bounded domains, beats the same model restricted to \(\beta \in [-20, 0]\) by more than \(\log 20\) in 12.1% of consensus positives and 11.7% of calm controls, 7.5% of unselected windows and 14.1% of run-ups; it gains anything beyond numerical tolerance in only 26 to 37% of records, and the fitted \(\beta\) is negative in the median in every group. A regression of monthly realized variance on the price level, controlling for the market's, gives a pooled elasticity of \(-0.27\) (record-clustered standard error 0.03) on the unselected windows, the leverage effect, about zero on run-ups (\(+0.05 \pm 0.04\)) and on the consensus positives (\(+0.02 \pm 0.08\)), and \(+0.59 \pm 0.10\) on the calm controls. So a shared model for the nuisance parameters on both sides removes the asymmetric fit of the scale, but that alone does not guarantee that the score fires more often, and in these daily data the level-elasticity signal these models target is weak and not specific to the consensus bubbles. A finite family failing in sample does not show that every bubble signal is absent, and no size or calibration follows from an in-sample comparison with \(\log 20\).

*Scope.* A diagnosis, not a test: the in-sample comparisons use the evaluation data and are not e-values, and their optimizers are not certified globally (four records hit the variance-collapse bound and are counted as zero). Realized variance is a noisy proxy. One finite family failing to separate does not show that no bubble signal exists.

### 6.9 The repaired score on real data

The repaired score of Section 5.9, with its fitter unchanged, was applied to daily real data together with the drift-robust score and GSADF, all three calibrated by the conservative rank rule on one fixed reference: 999 windows ending in 1995–2010, judged calm on their 126 training returns alone (the stock's annualized volatility at most 0.30 and drawdown at most 0.15, and the S&P 500's at most 0.20 and 0.10 on the same dates), at most one per date within a name. Each window has 379 consecutive prices, 126 training and 252 scored returns, ending on a month start. The evaluation sample is 10,000 windows drawn at random from the unselected universe of Section 6.4, without any filter; windows whose whole input starts in 2011 or later form the holdout, and earlier ones are retrospective. The consensus records of Section 6.2 were rebuilt to the same fixed length at their original end dates.

| Windows | Number | Repaired score | Drift-robust | Calibrated GSADF |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| all | 10,000 | 4.9 | 0.8 | 5.9 |
| holdout, calm starts | 1,899 | 7.5 | 1.1 | 5.2 |
| holdout, other starts | 3,134 | 3.5 | 0.5 | 6.2 |
| retrospective | 4,967 | 4.7 | 0.9 | 5.9 |
| consensus positives (of 33) | 30 scored | 3 | 0 | 6 |
| calm controls (of 103) | 103 | 20 | 2 | 6 |

*Scope.* Percentages of windows flagged at the 5% reference cutoff, except the last two rows, which are counts. All windows share one calibration, so flags are dependent and no binomial interval is given; the rates are empirical, not certified sizes, because no real-data law for the reference windows is established. Current S&P 500 constituents; daily adjusted closes.

The repaired score fires on real data, unlike the drift-robust score, whose cutoff falls on its tie at zero so that it flags almost nothing. Over the four periods used earlier its 5% flag rate is 4.0 to 4.9%, but by year it ranges from 1.4% to 16.2% (55 of 339 windows ending in 2018). Calm windows after 2010, judged by the same predicate as the reference, flag at 7.5%, so the 1995–2010 reference does not transport cleanly; that rate at one shared cutoff neither estimates a total-variation budget nor refutes the marginal guarantee, but it shows that a zero budget cannot simply be assumed. The score shows no useful prediction of forward drawdowns: regressed as a rank within its end date, its coefficient on the 126-day forward maximum drawdown has Driscoll–Kraay \(t = 0.96\), and its out-of-sample \(R^2\) on the holdout is about zero; its return slope at 126 days is negative and marginal (\(t = -1.81\)). GSADF keeps the clear positive association of Sections 6.4 and 6.5 (\(t = 4.9\) and \(5.5\), out-of-sample \(R^2\) about 1%), and in a regression with all three scores the repaired score adds nothing; the drift-robust score's rank goes with smaller drawdowns (\(t\) about \(-3\)). On the consensus records, optimistically, the repaired score ranks the bubbles below the calm controls (AUC 0.34, bootstrap interval 0.19 to 0.57, which includes one half) and flags 3 of 33 positives against 20 of 103 controls, in line with the near-zero or positive variance elasticities of Section 6.8; GSADF at the 1% cutoff flags 5 positives and no control.

So the repaired score, calibrated empirically, fires at about the nominal rate on average but shows no useful prediction of drawdowns and does not separate the consensus episodes, for this fixed daily algorithm; nonsignificance does not show that no signal exists, and a sequential version remains uncertified.

*Scope.* Ranks within end date give tied scores distinct ordinal ranks in symbol order (the repaired score is exactly zero on 6,550 windows); an average-rank variant gives drawdown \(t\) of 0.8 to 0.95 and return \(t\) of \(-1.4\), not a replacement result. The consensus inputs differ from the original episode windows in length, and three positives lack a complete history. The Student-t normalization of the model's density is still being checked against its source.

### 6.10 The level signature within the day

Daily data could not see a variance that rises with the price (Section 6.8). Five-minute consolidated-tape bars, regular session only, were used to look for it within the day, on the run-ups of Section 6.3 and on calm controls over the same dates. The positives are the 2020–21 meme stocks (GameStop, AMC, Koss, BlackBerry), the 2019–22 growth names (ARK Innovation, Tesla, Zoom, Peloton) and 20 of the 21 bubble-narrative names of 2023–26 (one had too short a history); the controls are SPY, DIA and five calm Dow members for each of the 12 distinct windows, 84 records in all. Returns between consecutive five-minute bars exclude the open and the overnight move and are divided by a diurnal clock, the slot's mean squared return over the name's prior year (or over the record itself for 7 recently listed names). With \(\beta\) the exponent of variance in the price, three estimates were made on each record. Within the day, a Gaussian quasi-likelihood with a free variance for every day estimates \(\beta\) from moves within each session alone, removing all variation across days. Across days, the log of each day's deseasonalized variance is regressed on the log opening price, controlling SPY's variance on the same day and a smooth volatility state, an exponentially weighted average of past days with a half-life of 20 sessions. And an in-sample check compares \(\beta \in [-20, 20]\) with \(\beta \in [-20, 0]\), a null that allows leverage, with variance proportional to the state, the clock and \(S^{\beta}\).

| Group | Records | Within-day \(\beta\) | \(t > 2\), \(t < -2\) | Across-day \(\beta\) | Check above \(\log 20\) |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| meme stocks, 2020–21 | 4 | 2.35 | 4, 0 | 0.65 | 3 or 4 |
| growth names, 2019–22 | 4 | \(-3.59\) | 0, 4 | \(-0.01\) | 1 |
| narrative names, 2023–26 | 20 | 1.48 | 12, 4 | \(-0.04\) | 7 |
| SPY and DIA | 24 | \(-20\) | 0, 24 | \(-1.56\) | 0 |
| calm Dow members | 60 | \(-5.94\) | 0, 28 | \(-0.23\) | 13 |

*Scope.* Medians of the estimated variance exponent per group, and counts of records. The \(t\) statistics are model-based, from the quasi-likelihood's curvature; the across-day errors are Newey–West. Within-day estimates for 23 of the 24 index records stop at the search bound of \(-20\), which does not show that the exponent lies below it. The meme row's count in the last column is 4 in the original run and 3 in an independent reimplementation, which puts GameStop's 2020–21 statistic at 2.0 rather than 3.02. Declared bar closes, a median 50% of them sub-cent for the positives and 30% for the controls; not a rounding-exact likelihood.

Within the day, the meme and narrative run-ups show the sign a strict local martingale needs. Variance rises with the price in all four meme stocks (\(t\) about 5 to 10) and in 13 of the 20 narrative names, while calm names and the indices show the usual intraday leverage effect, variance rising as the price falls, down to the search bound for the index funds. Each positive's estimate exceeds the mean of its own window's controls in 26 of 28 records, a median gap of 11, understated where the controls are censored. The growth names, and Nvidia, Palantir and MicroStrategy among the narrative names, show leverage instead. Across days, with the stock's own volatility state controlled, the elasticity is much smaller, with medians from \(-0.46\) (controls) to 0.65 (meme stocks), and the across-day \(t\) exceeds 2 in only 4 of 28 positives against 11 of 84 controls. The in-sample check exceeds \(\log 20\) in 11 or 12 of the 28 positives (39 to 43%, depending on conventions that the design leaves open) against 13 of 84 controls and no index record; the smooth state absorbs most of the slow co-movement of level and variance, so the check is weaker than the within-day estimate. Section 6.11 qualifies both readings: the own-state estimators understate a simulated level effect, and a return–volatility correlation alone produces a large within-day estimate. Measured from onset to peak, the run-up segments alone are weaker still (median within-day \(\beta\) of 0.2 to 0.6 over 19 segments, sensitive to where each segment starts).

A positive within-day elasticity does not identify a level-dependent diffusion coefficient. An inverse leverage effect in attention-driven trading, volatility rising on up-moves through order flow, would produce the same sign, and this design does not separate the two. Tick discreteness is argued against rather than tested: bid–ask bounce adds variance in proportion to the squared tick-to-price ratio, which pushes \(\beta\) negative, not positive. A strict local martingale can also arise through the correlation of price and volatility in a stochastic-volatility model rather than through the level (`sin1995complications`), which a level elasticity does not measure.

*Scope.* Descriptive and in-sample, not a test: the check uses each record's own data, and no false-alarm rate is estimated. Consensus and press labels; survivors and current listings only; five-minute closes, which see the diurnal pattern only through a fitted clock. The sub-cent shares were computed on split-adjusted closes, which can create sub-cent values before a split; the group medians are the same on raw closes.

### 6.11 Checking the estimators

Two features of the estimators could explain the weak signals of Sections 6.8 and 6.10: controlling the stock's own smooth volatility state may absorb a slow level effect, and the daily estimators of Section 6.8 fit one power law across run-up and crash. Both were checked on simulated paths with a known answer, using the same estimator code. Each path has two years of 78 five-minute bars a day, the first used only for the diurnal clock, which is U-shaped; an overnight move carrying a fifth of a day's variance; and log-normal stochastic volatility, mean-reverting with a half-life of 20 sessions, an annual level of 0.40 and a stationary standard deviation of 0.5 in log variance, multiplied by \((S/S_0)^{\beta}\). Its shocks have correlation \(\rho\) with the price's. There are seven cells of 20 paths each; a path whose log price leaves \([-5, 5]\) is kept as a failure (three in the \(\beta = -2\) cell, none elsewhere).

| True \(\beta\), \(\rho\) | Within-day | Across-day, own state | Check, own state | Daily fit (Section 6.8) |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| \(-2\), 0 | \(-2.52\) | \(-1.24\) | \(-1.03\) | \(-1.84\) |
| 0, 0 | \(-0.09\) | 0.43 | 0.37 | 0.32 |
| 0, \(-0.7\) | \(-4.14\) | \(-1.07\) | \(-0.38\) | \(-1.03\) |
| 1, 0 | 1.10 | 1.02 | 0.73 | 0.64 |
| 2, 0 | 2.74 | 1.08 | 1.02 | 2.01 |
| 2, 0, drift 0.8 | 1.89 | 1.36 | 0.79 | 1.78 |

*Scope.* Median recovered \(\beta\) over 20 simulated paths per cell (17 in the first row); with 20 paths a median has a standard error of about 0.2 to 0.4. An independent reimplementation with 100 paths gives medians of \(-0.01\) to 0.14 at \(\beta = 0\) (standard errors 0.08 to 0.19) and, at \(\beta = 2\), 1.95 within the day, 1.36 and 0.83 for the two own-state estimators and 1.87 for the daily fit (standard errors 0.13 to 0.21). One volatility model and one clock; Gaussian overnight moves.

The estimators recover a true level effect in sign and roughly in size. The daily fit of Section 6.8 recovers \(\beta = 2\) (1.87 to 2.01), so its pooling across a whole record is not what hid the signal there. The estimators that control the stock's own state come out below the truth at \(\beta = 2\), at about two-thirds across days and two-fifths for the check; no version without the state was simulated alongside, so the cause of the shortfall is not isolated. At \(\beta = 0\) the estimates are centred near zero but spread widely, so a single record's sign is weak evidence. The within-day estimate is not specific to the level: with \(\beta = 0\) and a return–volatility correlation of \(-0.7\) it is negative on every path, with median \(-4.1\). The within-day contrast of Section 6.10, calm names strongly negative and the meme run-ups positive, is therefore equally consistent with a difference in the sign of that correlation.

On the real records of Section 6.10, the same quantities were recomputed controlling only SPY's same-day variance and the diurnal clock, not the stock's own state. SPY's same-day variance is a descriptive covariate, not a forecast available in real time.

| Group | Records | Across-day \(\beta\) | Check \(\beta\) | Check above \(\log 20\) | Daily correlation |
|---|---|---|---|---|---|
| meme stocks, 2020–21 | 4 | 0.78 | 1.31 | 4 | 0.26 |
| growth names, 2019–22 | 4 | \(-0.02\) | \(-0.02\) | 2 | \(-0.04\) |
| narrative names, 2023–26 | 20 | \(-0.02\) | 0.24 | 10 | 0.21 |
| run-up segments | 19 | 0.51 | 0.75 | 15 | 0.11 |
| calm controls | 84 | \(-0.47\) | \(-0.75\) | 21 | \(-0.14\) |
| SPY and DIA | 24 | \(-0.67\) | \(-1.12\) | 3 | \(-0.17\) |

*Scope.* Medians over records and counts of records. The last column is the median correlation, per record, between a session's open-to-close return and the change in its deseasonalized log realized volatility from the previous session, over 68 to 858 session pairs per record, with no per-record interval. Run-up segments run from the record's low to its peak, chosen with hindsight. The no-state estimators were not checked in simulation.

Without the own state, the across-day association is positive in the meme stocks and in 84% of the run-up segments, and negative in calm controls. But the meme median was already 0.65 with the state controlled, and only 2 of the 4 meme slopes rose; the check also changes how SPY enters, and no matched simulation of the no-state estimators isolates what the state removed, so the attenuation is shown in simulation, not on these records. The check exceeds \(\log 20\) in 21 of 84 controls, a reminder that it is in-sample, not a test; its null fit reports a bound hit in 64 of 131 fits, at an end the output does not record. At the daily scale, the meme and most narrative run-ups have a positive correlation between returns and changes in estimated volatility, and calm names a negative one (positive in 19 of 28 positives against 18 of 84 controls), in the ranges that simulated level effects (0.05 to 0.17) and leverage (\(-0.18\)) produce; the five-minute and hourly versions have small medians in every group, and no rejection rule was set at any scale.

None of this identifies a mechanism. The measured correlation is between finite-interval returns and estimated volatility changes, not the instantaneous correlation of drivers in Sin's stochastic-volatility model (`sin1995complications`), whose strictness condition is specific to that model; and by Proposition 20, one physical law of prices can admit both a pricing measure under which the price is a martingale and one under which it is strict, so no measurement of the physical law alone settles the label. The measurements are consistent with a level-dependent diffusion, with positively correlated stochastic volatility, and with other mechanisms such as order flow, or the high uncertainty about future profitability that Pástor and Veronesi (`pastor2004there`) argue could explain both the valuations and the high return volatility of Nasdaq stocks in the late 1990s.

*Scope.* Simulation with one volatility model, a discretized hybrid of a level effect and log-normal stochastic volatility that is not Sin's model and not a certified strict-local-martingale sample; design-used episodes and hindsight segments; five-minute closes with sub-penny prints; descriptive diagnostics, not tests.

### 6.12 A real-time monitor on five-minute data

Sections 6.10 and 6.11 suggest a monitor that looks for the level effect within each session against a volatility state that moves only slowly. One was built from fixed choices and replayed on the five-minute bars, as if run live with a one-minute delay. At each monthly launch it fits the nuisance on the 252 prior sessions alone, under \(\beta = 0\): the drift, the initial variance state and the degrees of freedom of Student-t innovations. The model has a prior-year diurnal clock and a day-level variance state that updates once per completed session with a fixed half-life of 20 sessions. It then freezes these and scores the next 21 sessions, 1,617 five-minute checkpoints. The level is measured from each session's first bar, so the variance at a checkpoint is the clock times the state times \((S/s_d)^{\beta}\), with \(s_d\) that session's first price. The evidence at each checkpoint is the log ratio of an equally weighted mixture over eight values of \(\beta\) from 1/8 to 16, accumulated over all sessions so far, to the numerically profiled maximum over \(\beta \le 0\) on the same prefix. A missing bar ends the launch's supported prefix. The alarm is the first checkpoint whose transformed evidence strictly exceeds a cutoff set by the conservative rank rule of Proposition 45, from the path maxima of 499 calm-start references in 2017–18, at most one per name per quarter; the cutoff corresponds to a log ratio of about 10. All targets start in February 2019 or later, after every reference has finished. GSADF was run in monitoring form on daily closes, over the 252 prior sessions and the sessions so far, with its alarm at the first session end above its own reference cutoff at the same rank.

| Launches | Number | Monitor | Calibrated GSADF |
|---|---|---|---|
| run-up records, all | 449 | 19.6 | 40.5 |
