| meme stocks, 2020–21 | 52 | 46.2 | 67.3 |
| narrative names, 2023–26 | 233 | 21.0 | 45.1 |
| growth names, 2019–22 | 164 | 9.1 | 25.6 |
| same-date calm members | 805 | 4.2 | 0.0 |
| same-date SPY and DIA | 322 | 0.6 | 0.0 |
| unselected 100-name draw | 8,600 | 5.0 | 5.4 |

*Scope.* Percentages of launches with an alarm within 21 sessions, at a reference cutoff of rank 475 of 499 (\(\alpha = 1/20\)). Launches that fail the prior fit stay in the denominators (109 of the run-up launches and 609 of the draw), every one for lack of 200 sessions with at least 70 bars in the prior year. Control rows reuse the same name and launch across episode windows: over the 610 distinct name-launches the monitor alarms on 30 (4.9%). Rates are empirical, not certified, because the premise of the rank rule, that the references and the targets share a law after selection, is not established; the run-ups were already used in Sections 6.10 and 6.11. Current listings only.

The monitor fires on real five-minute data. On the run-up records it alarms on a fifth of launches and on almost half in the meme stocks; on calm controls over the same dates its empirical rate is near the nominal 5% (4.2% of members, 0.6% of SPY and DIA), and on the unselected draw it is 5.0%, ranging by year from 2.3% (2022) to 9.8% (2020). As an alarm it is weaker than calibrated GSADF on the episodes. GSADF alarms on twice as many run-up launches and on no calm control. It alarms strictly before the record's peak in 20 of 29 records, with a median lead of 57.5 sessions, against 14 for the monitor, with a median lead of 142. But the two do not see the same evidence: 163 of GSADF's 182 run-up alarms come at the first monitored session, from the 252 prior closes, while the monitor's evidence starts at launch, so this is a comparison of procedures, not of speed on equal information. The monitor's early alarms are often far from the top: Tesla alarmed in January 2020 for a peak in November 2021, and in 7 records the first alarm precedes the run-up's onset. Nine records never alarm, seven of them because every launch failed the prior fit.

The monitor's score over a completed window carries modest information about drawdowns after it. On 8,298 launches of the unselected draw with forward data, its rank within the launch date has Driscoll–Kraay \(t\) of 4.55 and 3.47 for the maximum drawdown over the next 63 and 126 sessions, with out-of-sample \(R^2\) of 0.39% and 0.31% on launches from 2023 (block-bootstrap standard errors 0.08 and 0.09). Together with GSADF, out of sample, it explains 1.39% and 1.74%, against 1.05% and 1.49% for GSADF alone. Those gains of 0.34 and 0.25 points have no paired uncertainty, and the full-sample \(t\) values are not out-of-sample tests. Neither score predicts forward returns. This concerns ranks of the whole window's score with outcomes after the window, not alarms. Ties in the rank (8.4% of scores) are broken in symbol order; with averaged ranks the \(t\) values are 4.48 and 3.39.

*Scope.* A counterfactual replay on split-adjusted closes, assuming that splits and the calendar were known in time. The score is a numerical likelihood ratio, not an e-value, and no sequential guarantee is claimed. The calibration is per launch, so it gives no control over repeated launches, a record or a panel. The run-ups are labelled by consensus and the press, and the same-date controls are descriptive.

### 6.13 The daily market-scale statistic on real data

The daily statistic of Section 5.10 was replayed on real adjusted daily closes from 1994 to 2026, unchanged. Its universe is the current S&P 500 constituents of Section 6.4, and it was also run on the consensus and collapsed-name episodes of Section 6.2 and the 2024–26 run-ups of Section 6.10. Each name is launched on the first session of every month, with 20 sessions to start the market scale, 252 prior sessions for the fit and 252 scored sessions. Calibration uses only the past. For each target month, the references are up to 499 universe launches whose scored year ended strictly before the target's launch and that pass the calm-start predicate of Section 6.9 on their training returns; they are taken in a fixed hash order, at most one per name per quarter. Their scores give the strict rank cutoff at \(\alpha = 1/20\), separately for the batch score, the monitor's path maximum and GSADF's path maximum. The first year has no completed reference, so its 3,197 launches cannot flag; from 1996 the cutoffs are stable (batch 0.94 to 0.96, GSADF 2.88 to 3.25).

| Launch period | Daily statistic, all | Calm starts | GSADF, all | Calm starts |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1994–99 | 6.3 | 8.9 | 8.2 | 6.4 |
| 2000–02 | 1.8 | 0.0 | 7.1 | 8.8 |
| 2003–07 | 4.3 | 4.9 | 4.5 | 3.4 |
| 2008–09 | 0.9 | 0.2 | 5.9 | 3.9 |
| 2010–19 | 2.0 | 2.8 | 6.2 | 5.2 |
| 2020–21 | 1.4 | 1.5 | 14.7 | 5.3 |
| 2022–26 | 2.4 | 2.3 | 4.9 | 3.3 |
| all, 153,716 launches | 2.9 | 3.8 | 6.7 | 4.8 |

*Scope.* Percentages of launches flagged by the batch score and by GSADF at the reference cutoff (the monitor's rates are within 0.8 points of the batch score's). Periods classify launch dates, and the following year is scored. All launches share overlapping references, so flags are dependent and no binomial interval is given; there are no null labels, so these are flag rates, not false-alarm rates. Current constituents; a retrospective replay.

The statistic flags fewer launches than GSADF overall, and far fewer in turbulent years (0.9 to 1.9% of launches in 2000–02, 2008–09 and 2020–21, where GSADF reaches 14.7%). Its rate is highest for launches in the 1990s, 8.9% of calm starts, so calm references from earlier years do not give it a uniform rate even on calm starts, which are themselves references for later months. Without null labels, neither the low rates in turbulent years nor the high ones in the 1990s show which flags are false, or that the statistic is specific to bubbles; Section 5.10 found that its estimate responds to correlated stochastic volatility.

Its rank within the launch date has a small in-sample association with deeper drawdowns over the next 63 and 126 sessions, for the monitor (Driscoll–Kraay \(t\) of 3.4 and 3.4) more than for the batch score (\(t\) of 2.7 and 2.3, or 2.0 and 1.8 when the two-thirds of batch scores that are exactly zero share an averaged rank instead of being ordered by row). Out of sample, on launches whose prior year starts in 2011 or later, its \(R^2\) is 0.03 to 0.16%, within two bootstrap standard errors of zero, against 0.93 to 0.98% for GSADF. Together the two explain 1.02% and 0.98% against GSADF's 0.98% and 0.93%, gains with no paired uncertainty. Neither predicts returns. On the 9,840 windows of Section 6.9 where both can be computed, its batch score has a rank correlation of 0.34 with the repaired score; it is significant for later drawdowns in sample (\(t\) about 2.1 to 2.2) where the repaired score is not, though that is not a test of the difference.

On the episodes it often alarms early, sometimes years early, and long leads are not by themselves useful timing. Of the 30 consensus positives with a US calendar and a complete prior year, the monitor alarms before the peak on 15, a median of 377 sessions before, and GSADF on 14, a median of 142. The monitor alarms before the peak on the Nasdaq indices from 1995, on all four large homebuilders 377 to 411 sessions before, and on Lehman and Countrywide among the collapsed names. It alarms only after the peak on the 2007 financials of the consensus list. Of the 18 run-ups of 2024–26 with a daily prior year, it alarms before the peak on 7 and GSADF on 10. On the consensus negatives it alarms on 4.8% of 3,556 launches (GSADF 3.9%), and on at least one launch for 35 of 91 records. The two do not see the same evidence: GSADF's path includes the prior year, and 315 of its 650 alarmed episode launches alarm on the first scored day, against none for this monitor. Against the five-minute monitor of Section 6.12, on the 29 records both cover, both alarm on 14, where the daily monitor's first alarm is earlier on one and the five-minute monitor's on 13; the daily monitor alone alarms on 5, the five-minute monitor alone on 6, and neither on 4. Peaks are defined differently for three records, and the two use different data, horizons and information.

*Scope.* A retrospective replay on current constituents; calibration is empirical, and stable cutoffs from calm references do not establish the premise of the rank rule, nor control over repeated launches or combined scores. The episodes were already used in earlier sections, three consensus records (two on non-US calendars, one before SPY's history) and four collapsed names could not be scored, and one market scale (SPY) was used.

### 6.14 Summary

On real prices, no procedure tested both keeps false alarms down and finds the consensus bubbles. Most of the flags that the tests do raise reflect volatility rising through the window rather than jumps (Section 6.6). Scaled by the implied-volatility index, the score is silent on SPX and NDX, including 1999–2001 and 2020–21, while GSADF flags the dot-com SPX record (Section 6.7). The drift-robust test's silence comes from its construction, a training-chosen forecast that is often itself a null law, and a forecast scale fixed on training data against a null refitted on evaluation data, but even in sample a level-elasticity alternative fires on the consensus bubbles no more often than on calm controls, 12.1% against 11.7% (Section 6.8). A repaired score that shares the nuisance model on both sides fires at about the nominal rate on real data, but shows no useful prediction of drawdowns and flags calm controls more often than consensus bubbles (Sections 5.9 and 6.9). At five-minute resolution, variance rises with the price within the day in the meme run-ups and most press-labelled run-ups of 2023–26, the opposite of the leverage seen in calm names and the indices (Section 6.10); the estimators recover a known level effect in simulation, but the within-day estimate also responds to the correlation of returns with volatility, and at the daily scale that correlation is positive in these run-ups and negative in calm names, which identifies no mechanism (Section 6.11). A real-time monitor built on that within-day effect fires on real five-minute data at near-nominal empirical rates on calm controls, and its window score adds a little to GSADF's prediction of drawdowns, but as an alarm it is weaker than calibrated GSADF on the run-ups (Section 6.12). A daily statistic that takes its volatility scale from the market flags fewer launches than GSADF, especially in turbulent years, and often alarms years before consensus peaks, but its drawdown association is small and does not survive out of sample (Sections 5.10 and 6.13). The drift-robust test flags no calm control on daily data, and on daily, collapsed-name and hourly data it flags only the most extreme short episodes, where a jump explanation cannot be excluded; on hourly data, with a calendar clock that ignores the intraday pattern, it also flagged one calm name; this is the behaviour Section 5.2 predicts for daily data, not a finding about the pricing laws of these episodes. The existing tests flag more positives but also flag calm controls at the rates measured above: GSADF 13 to 19% of calm daily names and 13 of 98 calm hourly ones, and JKP's Method 2 most names whatever their label. On an unselected universe the selected separation of Section 6.2 does not carry over: the drift-robust test fires on about 0.1 to 0.2% of windows and its ranking does not predict drawdowns, while GSADF fires on 12 to 52% of windows by period and its ranking predicts about 1% of the variation in forward drawdowns out of sample. With critical values set from the calm windows (Section 6.5), GSADF's held-out false-alarm rate on calm records is about nominal, and it still flags 59 to 76% of the consensus positives; on the unselected universe that threshold flags 6 to 7% of shorter windows in calm years but about 15% of 72-month ones, and its flag carries the same small drawdown signal. What the simulations and the data leave open is the volatility that changes within a window, which the null either ignores, and false-alarms, or absorbs, and loses its power.

## 7. Related work

**Martingale criteria for stochastic exponentials.** The general criterion we apply is `mijatovic2010martingale`, Theorem 2.1. Their Proposition 4.2 writes the discounted price as \(s_0 Z\) with \(b(x) = \sigma(x)/x\), the case \(\pi = 1\) of our representation, and their Corollary 4.3 is the JKP criterion. That criterion has several earlier proofs, which `mijatovic2010martingale` (p. 14) lists; we cite it in their form, which needs no local boundedness of \(\sigma\) or \(1/\sigma\). The explosion test behind both is Feller's. We use it in the endpoint form recalled in `mijatovic2010martingale`, equations (20)–(23), and the global form of `karatzas2015distribution`, Appendix A. Karatzas and Ruf `karatzas2015distribution` give the Feller test function for the drift family \(s\,b\) with \(b(x) = a\,s(x)/x\) in closed form (their equation (7.4)), which is exactly the auxiliary diffusion here with \(a = \pi\); their Proposition 7.1 records the case \(a = 1\) (our \(\pi = 1\)) and the exit of \(S\) at zero used in Corollary 2. Neither source states the classification of the constant-proportion exponential across \(\pi\), or the zero-endpoint correction. For uniform integrability over an infinite horizon, `mijatovic2010martingale` (Theorem 2.3) gives the one-dimensional criterion, and Ruf (`ruf2015martingale`, Proposition 3.1 and Theorem 3.3) characterizes both the martingale property and uniform integrability of stochastic exponentials of functionals of a possibly multidimensional, time-dependent equation, through an energy integral under an auxiliary measure that always exists; Propositions 4 and 5 apply the two to constant and to time-varying exposure.

**Strict local martingales and bubbles.** The bubble definition is that of `jarrow2007complete`. Kardaras, Kreher and Nikeghbali `kardaras2015strict`, Section 3.1, use the same auxiliary drift \(\mu + \sigma b\) and point back to the general criterion; their discussion of positive diffusions imposes a divergent integral at the lower endpoint, which is consistent with our finding that zero must be checked separately. The literature on investment when an asset has a bubble, for example `herdegen2013strict` and `herdegen2016strong`, may contain special cases of Theorem 1; we have not yet compared it. Stochastic portfolio theory studies rebalanced portfolios in markets where the deflator, and with it the discounted prices, can be strict local martingales (`fernholz2008diversity`, Section 9; `karatzas2017trading`). Its setting is relative arbitrage without a common pricing measure under which every price is a local martingale, so its results do not transfer to Theorem 1 or to Section 4.2 without an argument, and none is given here.

**Bubble detection and identification.** Jarrow, Kchia and Protter `jarrow2011detect` test for bubbles by estimating \(\sigma\) nonparametrically on the visited range and extrapolating its tail; Proposition 7 makes exact the obstruction they identify, and their extrapolation methods are the kind of structural assumption that Proposition 7 shows to be necessary. The nonparametric estimators such tests rely on recover \(\sigma\) only where the price has been: the precision of the estimator of `florenszmirou1993estimating` at a level is governed by the local time there, and the kernel rates of `jacod2000parametric` hold pointwise and on compact sets. Bandi and Phillips `bandi2003fully` build their estimators on the occupation formula, which they state for a general continuous semimartingale (their Lemma 3); Proposition 23 uses only that formula, so it needs none of the recurrence assumptions of their estimation results, and its denominator is the semimartingale local time rather than their chronological local time. Nothing in that literature reaches the tail that decides the JKP integral (the visited part is identified exactly through local time, Proposition 23), which is the observation Propositions 7 and 17 formalize. Tests for an explosive autoregressive root `phillips2012multiple` target a different notion of bubble, in which the price grows explosively relative to fundamentals; a strict local martingale need not have an explosive root, and Theorem 18 says nothing about those tests. When volatility varies over time, the limiting distribution of these tests depends on the variance function, and Kurozumi, Skrobotov and Tsarev (`kurozumi2021time`) restore a pivotal limit by deforming the time domain; this fits the over-rejection of GSADF under level-dependent volatility in Section 5.2. Greenwood, Shleifer and You (`greenwood2017bubbles`) study bubbles as observed run-ups and crashes: for US industry portfolios from 1926 and international sectors, a sharp run-up does not on average predict low returns, but it does predict a higher probability of a crash, a drawdown of 40% within two years, and the run-up's volatility, turnover, issuance and price path help forecast it. Obayashi, Protter and Yang (`obayashi2015lifetime`) date bubbles and their lifetimes by smoothing a daily JKP signal from intraday prices with a hidden Markov model. Section 5 compares these four procedures with the tests of Section 3.5 in simulation, and Section 6 on real prices. Pooling a tail across names has precedent only for the tail index of returns (`kelly2013tail`) and for repeated paths of one process (`ellamintsa2025minimax`); neither supplies the common-exponent model of Proposition 26. For merging e-values across names, Vovk and Wang (`vovk2021values`, Proposition 3.1) show that under arbitrary dependence the arithmetic mean essentially dominates every symmetric merging function, so the evidence does not accumulate with the number of names, while the product of independent e-values is an e-value (their Section 4). This is consistent with the negative pooling result after Proposition 41, whose null allows any correlation across names. In the panel bubble literature, Liu, Phillips and Yu (`liu2022panel`) gain power by aggregating clusters of similar series, and note that a factor-based panel approach allowing cross-section dependence does not enhance discriminatory power; their tests concern an explosive root, not a strict local martingale. We use the term identified set as in the partial-identification literature (for example `chernozhukov2007estimation`), but define it directly as the image of the compatible class; no estimation or inference result from that literature is used, and we are not aware of an application of identified sets, or of a testing impossibility result, to bubbles in the strict-local-martingale sense. Classical results on the nonexistence of nonparametric tests (`bahadur1956nonexistence`) may anticipate the abstract form of Theorem 18; we have not yet checked their hypotheses against this problem. The bounded-stopping and supermartingale facts behind Theorem 18 and Proposition 19 are as recalled in `hulley2019weak`, Section 2.

**Calibrated likelihood inference under misspecification.** Propositions 27–42 and 50 apply split-likelihood universal inference (`wasserman2019universal`) and e-values with e-BH (`wang2020false`), whose guarantees assume the model is correct. Park, Balakrishnan and Wasserman (`park2023robust`, Section 3.1) show that when it is not, the split likelihood-ratio set can fail to cover even the Kullback–Leibler projection of the true law, because likelihood ratios are unbounded; their robust construction targets other projections, which need not carry a bubble label. This is consistent with the nearly vacuous bounds of Proposition 43, and we found no source linking a projection's exponent to the true law's bubble label. Approximate likelihoods for discretely sampled diffusions (`aitsahalia1998maximum`) and for stochastic volatility (`aitsahalia2004maximum`, which replaces the latent volatility by the implied volatility of a short-dated at-the-money option) give accuracy, not the finite-sample validity that Proposition 27 needs. Microstructure noise at high frequency (`aitsahalia2005ultra`) and jumps, for which a test exists (`aitsahalia2009testing`), are further departures from the catalogues' noiseless diffusion observations. Jumps also change what a bubble criterion must look at: Hulley and Ruf (`hulley2019weak`, Example 3.2) construct a nonnegative pure-jump martingale that is not uniformly integrable because its positive jumps grow combinatorially, and their conditions involve the jumps at exit times, not only a volatility function, so the diffusion criteria behind our labels do not cover jump models. A null enlarged by jumps or regime switches has a mixture likelihood that can be unbounded; the split-likelihood test needs only an upper bound on the null maximum, and its authors suggest powered likelihoods for robustness and smoothed likelihoods when the likelihood is unbounded (`wasserman2019universal`, Section 6). We found no source giving a likelihood test with a finite-sample guarantee for a jump-diffusion or regime-switching null for a bubble. Lions and Musiela (`lions2007correlations`) give necessary and sufficient conditions, through parameters such as the correlation, for classical stochastic-volatility models to have integrable solutions. Dandapani and Protter (`protter2019strict`) note that such results depend at one key stage on Feller's test and so apply only in one dimension, and use Lyapunov functions, in the manner of Khasminskii's test for explosions, to give sufficient conditions for components of a multidimensional system with stochastic volatility to be strict local martingales or martingales.

**Intraday structure and observation noise.** The high-frequency literature treats microstructure noise and rounding as problems for estimating integrated variance, not as threats to the size of a test. Aït-Sahalia, Mykland and Zhang (`aitsahalia2003often`) show that modelling the noise explicitly restores the benefit of sampling as often as possible, and that the volatility estimator keeps the same variance even if the noise distribution is misspecified. Li and Mykland (`li2007volatility`) model the contamination as a Markov kernel on the latent price, including additive error and rounding, and find that the robustness of the two-scales estimator deteriorates as the kernel becomes less smooth, as with rounding. On magnitudes, Aït-Sahalia and Yu (`aitsahalia2008high`, Table 6) estimate an average noise standard deviation of 5 basis points across NYSE stocks from 1995 to 2005, from transaction data. The two-point error with a persistent sign of Proposition 62 is the bid–ask structure written by Diebold and Strasser (`diebold2010correlation`) as the efficient price plus half the spread times the trade direction, and Jacod, Li and Zheng (`jacod2013statistical`) find real noise moderately positively autocorrelated, which is why persistence belongs in such a declaration. Lou, Polk and Skouras (`lou2019overnight`) find that overnight and intraday returns behave as different components, in their means, so an overnight gap is not just a longer intraday step. We found no source giving a test with a finite-sample guarantee under declared noise, a deterministic time-of-day pattern or overnight gaps. The exact cell-probability correction of Proposition 55 is the forward, or filtering, recursion of a hidden Markov model with interval observations. Particle filters exist for diffusions observed at discrete times, with an Euler discretization (`moral2001monte`) or without time discretization (`fearnhead2007particle`), but they approximate conditional expectations of the state under observation densities. We found no certified bound on the likelihood of interval observations of a diffusion, which is what the denominators here would need. On clocks shared across names, which Proposition 36 assumes, Campbell, Lettau, Malkiel and Xu (`campbell2000have`) find that firm-level volatility rose relative to market volatility over 1962–97, so that correlations among individual stocks fell. Herskovic, Kelly, Lustig and Van Nieuwerburgh (`herskovic2014common`) find that a single factor explains 35% of the time variation in firm-level idiosyncratic volatility. So a market clock leaves a large firm-level remainder, whose volatilities share a further common factor; both bear on that premise. Nonparametric estimators of a time-varying volatility assume it constant over an interval of time homogeneity (`mercurio2004statistical`) or a smooth path observed with noise (`hoffmann2012adaptive`); neither lets the volatility also depend on the price level, so neither addresses the near-confounding of time variation and level dependence found after Proposition 48 and in Section 5.3. Tests of the form of a volatility function exist. Dette and Podolskij (`dette2008testing`) test a parametric null for \(\sigma(t, X_t)\), such as constant volatility, from discrete observations, and detect local alternatives at rate \(n^{-1/2}\) in a fixed direction; Bull (`bull2015semimartingale`) detects deviations of volatility-like processes from a parametric null at the near-optimal rate \(n^{-1/4}\), up to logarithms, when the deviation is itself a semimartingale. Neither has a nonparametric time effect as a nuisance, which is the problem of Propositions 49 and 50. Spot-variance estimators converge pointwise at about \(n^{-1/4}\) (`mykland2008inference`), slower than the \(n^{-1/2}\) that the array bound of Proposition 49 needs on the grid where the bracket is computed.

**Sequential testing and real-time monitoring.** The monitored score of Proposition 69 is a filtered, conditional version of the running sequential likelihood ratio of Wasserman, Ramdas and Balakrishnan (`wasserman2019universal`, Section 8, Theorem 9). That ratio has a non-anticipating plug-in numerator and a null maximum in the denominator, with a type-I guarantee uniform over time; they note that with nuisance parameters or conditional likelihoods the underlying process may be only a supermartingale. Ramdas, Grünwald, Vovk and Shafer (`ramdas2023game`, Section 3.2.1) trace the plug-in method to Wald and note that unsmoothed maximum-likelihood plug-ins can assign zero probability to what happens. They also recall (Section 2.9) that e-values and e-processes may be averaged with weights fixed before the data. Proposition 71 instead funds new tests, launched at stopping times from one budget, each scoring only the data after its launch; with fixed dates and weights it reduces to such an average. Ramdas, Ruf, Larsson and Koolen (`ramdas2020admissible`) show that admissible anytime-valid inference must rest on nonnegative martingales. In the bubble literature, Whitehouse, Harvey and Leybourne (`whitehouse2023real`) state the multiple-testing problem of real-time monitoring, in which repeating a test as each observation arrives inflates its size. The real-time detectors of Phillips, Shi and Yu (`phillips2013realtime`) compare a scan statistic at each date with a critical value sequence and control false positives asymptotically, by letting that sequence grow, rather than bounding the probability of ever crossing in finite samples as Propositions 69–71 do. For the daily scales of Propositions 37–39, Pérez-Ortiz, Lardy, de Heide and Grünwald (`perezortiz2024statistics`) show that the likelihood ratio of the statistic invariant under a group is a growth-rate-optimal e-statistic, a Bayes factor with a right Haar prior. With the scaling group this removes an unknown scale without estimating it; it applies to scale shifts of a fixed within-day structure, not to volatility that depends on the price level within a day. Shin, Ramdas and Rinaldo (`shin2023detectors`) build e-detectors, sums of e-processes started at consecutive times, with nonasymptotic bounds on the average run length between false alarms and, in some cases, on the delay after a change; the restarts of Propositions 70 and 71 are e-processes started at declared or stopping times, with a bound on ever crossing over a fixed horizon rather than a run length. In change-point detection, Lorden and Pollak (`lorden2005nonanticipating`) apply the same Robbins–Siegmund idea as the plug-in forecasts here, estimating the unknown post-change parameter from past data only inside CUSUM and Shiryaev–Roberts rules, which keeps the martingale property under no change; Mei (`mei2006sequential`) treats unknown parameters in the pre-change law, minimizing the frequency of false alarms uniformly over them for a required delay, the detection-side counterpart of the composite nulls of Section 3.5.

**Empirically calibrated thresholds.** The calibrated thresholds of Proposition 45 and of Section 6.5 are conformal in form. Without exchangeability, Barber, Candès, Ramdas and Tibshirani (`barber2023conformal`) bound the loss of coverage by a weighted total-variation distance between the data vector and its versions with the test point swapped with each calibration point, with no assumption on the joint law; no value of that distance is known for overlapping windows of one price path. For counterfactual inference with time series, Chernozhukov, Wüthrich and Zhu (`chernozhukov2017exact`) obtain approximate validity under stationary weakly dependent errors by permuting blocks, and Gibbs and Candès (`gibbs2021adaptive`) give up the guarantee for each new window in exchange for a long-run frequency of errors that holds under arbitrary distribution shift. For many test points, Bates, Candès, Lei, Romano and Sesia (`bates2023testing`) show that conformal p-values are positively regression dependent on the inliers when the test points are independent of each other and of the calibration data, so the Benjamini–Hochberg rule applies; overlapping windows do not meet that premise, which shows only that the theorem does not apply. None of these gives a guarantee for calm windows selected from the realized path.

**Stochastic volatility and incomplete markets.** Sin `sin1995complications` shows that in a class of stochastic-volatility models the price can be a strict local martingale under one pricing measure while an equivalent measure makes it a martingale; In that model the sign of \(a \cdot \gamma\), which decides strictness, is the sign of the correlation between price and volatility shocks. Proposition 20 applies Sin's construction with the physical law held fixed. Sin treats the price itself, not constant-proportion wealth, so it does not anticipate Theorem 1, but it is the nearest source for the stochastic-volatility question that Theorem 1 leaves open. The measure-dependence of bubbles in incomplete markets is discussed by Jarrow, Protter and Shimbo (`jarrow2007complete`; `jarrow2007asset`, unread), and their treatment may already contain the qualitative point.

**Option prices under bubbles.** Which option prices are observed matters once the underlying can be strict. Under expectation pricing, put-call parity fails by exactly the defect, \(E_Q[(X_T - K)^+] - E_Q[(K - X_T)^+] = E_Q[X_T] - K\) (`kardaras2015strict`, Example 4.8), and this residual is what Proposition 72 uses. Carr, Fisher and Ruf price by minimal superreplication instead, which adds the defect to the call and restores parity (`carr2012hedging`, Corollaries 3.7 and 3.8); under that operator the residual vanishes and the test has no power. American calls differ again: parity holds with the American call in place of the European one, and early exercise can be optimal (`bayraktar2011strict`, Section 2.2), and European prices need not preserve convexity in the presence of bubbles (`ekstrom2009bubbles`). Every option-based statement in this paper is therefore made under a declared operator: European expectation pricing in Section 4.1, the American exercise value in Section 4.3. The restriction of Proposition 77, a pricing kernel that depends on the index level alone, is the kind of object Rosenberg and Engle (`rosenberg1997option`) estimate as an empirical pricing kernel, the state price per unit probability, from a daily cross-section of S&P 500 option premia and a GARCH-type physical model; that is an estimate under a parametric physical law, not a test of the restriction, and we know of no estimator for the killed-weight version beyond the synthetic one of Proposition 78.

**Leveraged funds.** A continuously rebalanced leveraged fund with leverage \(\beta\) is the exponential \(\mathcal{E}(\beta\int dS/S)\), and its options are priced in `leung2017leveraged` under the assumption that \(S\) is a strictly positive martingale. Theorem 1 treats \(\beta = \pi > 0\) for local volatility without either assumption, but only in this continuous-rebalancing idealization. Funds traded on exchanges rebalance daily and pay fees and financing costs; `avellaneda2009path` gives the exact daily relation between fund and underlying returns with these terms. Section 3.3 uses it. The threshold \(1/2\) and the lower-endpoint case of Theorem 1 hold at most as continuous-rebalancing limits and should not be read as properties of such funds (Propositions 11 and 12). For a strictly positive price, the finite-grid funds do converge to the continuously rebalanced wealth (Proposition 13), without assuming the price is a martingale, which `leung2017leveraged` assumes for the continuous model; for prices that can reach zero no limit is known. Jarrow `jarrow2010understanding` analyses the risk of leveraged funds; we have not read it, and we do not claim that the discrete-rebalancing results of Section 3.3 are new.

## 8. Outlook

What is not done:

- **The wealth identification (W).** We classify the stopped exponential \(v_0 Z^\pi\). We do not prove that it is the wealth of an admissible constant-proportion strategy, and we do not define holdings after the price reaches zero. A different convention for wealth at default is outside Theorem 1.
- **Exact endpoint values.** The Feller test functions of the auxiliary diffusion satisfy \(\tilde v_\pi(\infty) = I_\infty/(2\pi - 1)\) for \(\pi > 1/2\) and \(\tilde v_\pi(0) = I_0/(1 - 2\pi)\) for \(0 < \pi < 1/2\), by monotone convergence from (4) and (5). The proof of Theorem 1 uses only the equivalent integrability tests, and these identities are not recorded as results here; the formal proof computes the limits as integrals but does not state the identities.
- **Other exposures.** For Theorem 1, \(\pi \le 0\) (short and inverse positions) and time-dependent or random \(\pi\) are not covered; Proposition 5 gives sufficient conditions for bounded predictable exposure and a classification only for geometric Brownian motion with a deterministic schedule. Two independent assets, each with its own endpoint test, are treated in Proposition 6; correlated assets and liquidation through default are open. Section 3.3 treats one holding interval for every real \(\pi\) and finite grids over a martingale price for \(0 \le \pi \le 1\). The uniform integrability needed for bubble sizes to converge in Proposition 13 is proved when the limit is a martingale (Proposition 14). The limit for prices that can reach zero, convergence of bubble sizes when the continuous limit is strict (\(1/2 < \pi < 1\)), exposures above one on a grid without a liquidation rule, inverse funds whose floor is monitored discretely or whose price gaps, random grids, and a test across exposures that reaches inverse funds, or leveraged funds without the continuously monitored floor, or that does not assume jointly covering intervals for expectation prices, are open.
- **Uniform integrability.** Theorem 1 is about the martingale property on finite horizons only. Applied to constant exposure, the criterion of Mijatović and Urusov `mijatovic2010martingale` (Theorem 2.3) gives a uniformly integrable martingale on \([0, \infty)\) only at \(\pi = 0\) (Proposition 4), and for geometric Brownian motion with a deterministic exposure schedule Ruf's criterion `ruf2015martingale` gives one exactly when \(\int_0^\infty\pi(t)^2\,dt < \infty\) (Proposition 5); both are machine-checked, conditional on the respective criteria, and the weak-tail conditions of Hulley and Ruf `hulley2019weak` are not applied.
- **Stochastic volatility.** The argument is one-dimensional: \(\sigma\) depends on the level of \(S\) alone. Whether the threshold \(1/2\) and the zero-endpoint effect survive when volatility has its own dynamics is open. Any such statement must start from Sin `sin1995complications`, where the price alone already changes its martingale property with the pricing measure.
- **Tests with power.** By Theorem 22, a test with power above size needs a model class that excludes the linear-tail completions \(\sigma_n\), for example a stated restriction on the tail of \(\sigma\) that they violate. Restricting the physical dynamics alone does not suffice, since the theorem holds with \(P = Q\). The declared power tail of Proposition 25, and its common-exponent version across names in Proposition 26, are such restrictions; the band rules need jointly covering confidence bands, constructed in Proposition 27 only for a correctly specified finite catalogue of likelihoods, which price data supply only under the strong assumptions of Proposition 28, and the threshold tests are only pointwise. Other restrictions, and a comparison with existing tests, are not treated. Any test with power above size must also exclude the hidden-measure nulls of Proposition 20, which lie outside local volatility.
- **A usable test.** Section 3.5 gives ingredients, not yet a usable test. Still to be done: a realistic model class (a grid of elasticity exponents, stochastic volatility, and jumps if feasible), since the misspecification bounds of Proposition 43 are nearly vacuous at high sampling frequency and a valid likelihood for rounded prices at high frequency needs heavy computation, and a contamination budget for jumps is uninformative over realistic windows, volatility that changes within the evaluation window has no informative budget, and nulls that allow one switch or Poisson jumps are valid but nearly powerless on daily data, the first because time variation and level dependence of volatility are nearly confounded there and the second because computable bounds on the jump family's supremum remain loose, with the false-alarm rate and power measured when the true model lies outside it; a better way to declare a volatility interval for a real asset in advance, since a known interval recovers much of the power that a free scale costs, but an honest declaration from an earlier window recovers little and assumes the volatility does not change between windows; power on realistic samples, where pooling across constituents was tried and did not beat per-name testing, even with a forecast that models the dependence across names, and where the simulations of Section 5 find useful power only with long daily records or with intraday data under a declared clock; and a procedure that, on real prices, both keeps false alarms down and flags the consensus episodes, which none of the procedures compared in Section 6 does.
- **What Section 3.4 does not give.** Bubble values conditional on nontrivial information; the supremum of the set in Proposition 20; a characterization of all feasible defect curves in Proposition 19; a confidence procedure; observations with option prices or an unbounded horizon.
- **Estimating the visited integral.** Proposition 23 is exact for a continuously observed path, and the sampled-price estimator of Proposition 24 covers only a fixed interval with a known floor on \(\sigma^2/x^2\), and is machine-checked only conditionally on Jacod's kernel estimation theorem. An estimator over the random visited range, its behaviour near the maximum of the path, where the local time is small, and confidence intervals are not treated.
- **Baskets.** Section 4.2 covers fixed positive holdings without dividends or rebalancing, and its recovery formula for single-name bubbles assumes a terminal density depending on the index alone, not shown to be testable, and idealized population inputs; Proposition 78 estimates the contributions and tests their necessary inequalities only in a synthetic experiment with repeated terminal panels and a supplied pricing law, where on a realistic basket its fixed-bin bias is as large as the contribution and its test has no power (Section 5.8), and estimating that law from option panels, or the physical law from one historical path of constituent prices, is not treated.
- **Certifying the absence of bubbles.** Section 4.3 bounds bubbles above, up to a tolerance, and an upper budget never singles out a bubbly name; a positive monetary lower bound for single names is not available; exact certification of no bubble from positive option prices is not available, and the upper bound on the American value and the tracking bound under \(Q\) are not supplied from data.
- **Which names are bubbly.** The discovery list of Proposition 40 controls the false-discovery rate only within an exact price-law catalogue under \(P = Q\); it gives no guarantee conditional on a screen, for realized lists, or across repeated testing dates.
- **Prior art.** The investment-with-bubbles literature cited in Section 7 has not been checked for special cases of Theorem 1.

## Appendix A. Machine verification

The formalization is in Lean 4 with Mathlib. Mathlib has no stochastic calculus for diffusions, so the cited stochastic results enter as hypothesis structures, and what is proved is the deterministic analysis built on them. The statement of Theorem 1 is \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D1TwoEndpoint.lean}\), readable without the proofs; the proofs are in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D1TwoEndpointProof.lean}\). There is no \(\texttt{sorry}\), and no axiom beyond Lean's standard ones.

- **Theorem 1, deterministic core (machine-checked, unconditional).** For every \(\sigma\) satisfying standing assumption 1, every \(\pi > 0\) and every \(c > 0\): the endpoint condition of the martingale criterion for \(b_\pi\), written out with the source's scale density, scale function, Feller test function and goodness conditions, holds if and only if the right-hand side of (2) fails. Both endpoints are proved bad, the auxiliary scale (3) is computed in closed form, and the finiteness of \(I_0\) and \(I_\infty\) is proved independent of \(c\). The formal proof differs from the proof in Section 3: it evaluates the endpoint limits of the auxiliary Feller test function directly, by dominated convergence in one direction and Fatou's lemma in the other, so it does not use Feller's test.
- **Theorem 1, wealth classification (machine-checked, conditional).** The standalone statement proves (2) and the table for \(v_0 Z^\pi\) from the conclusion of the martingale criterion at this instance, taken as a premise. The proof file also derives it from hypothesis structures for the martingale criterion and the JKP criterion (\(\texttt{wealth\_classification}\), \(\texttt{counterexample}\)), and checks that the criterion's condition as stated there is the one restated in the standalone file. These structures currently have only vacuous instances, because the pinned Mathlib cannot construct a Brownian motion. The hypotheses of these conditional theorems have therefore never been shown jointly satisfiable in Lean, and the theorems are exactly as conditional as the prose: on the martingale criterion, the JKP criterion and (W). They add no evidence that those inputs hold. Assumption (W) enters as the definition \(V^\pi = v_0 Z^\pi\). The usual conditions of standing assumption 2 are not needed.
- **Corollary 2.** Its last sentence is machine-checked with \(I_0 < \infty\) in place of "\(S\) can reach zero" and \(I_\infty < \infty\) in place of "\(S\) is strict": the conjecture so read holds for all \(\pi > 0\) when \(I_0 = \infty\), and fails for every \(0 < \pi < 1/2\) when \(I_0 < \infty\). The equivalence of \(I_0 < \infty\) with reaching zero, an application of Feller's test, is not formalized.
- **Example 3.** Part (a) is machine-checked: \(I_0 = 1/2\), \(I_\infty = \infty\), and, conditionally as above, \(S\) is a martingale and \(V^{1/4}\) is strict on every finite horizon. Part (b) is not formalized; its numerical values come from a registered experiment (\(\texttt{experiments/001-d1-threshold.md}\)) and are evidence about the cases computed, not a proof.
- **Proposition 4 (machine-checked, conditional).** The endpoint computation is proved unconditionally: for \(\pi \ge 0\), the criterion's four alternatives hold exactly when \(\pi = 0\), with the vanishing alternative read as almost everywhere on \((0, \infty)\). The uniform-integrability criterion is a hypothesis structure with a vacuous instance, and Theorem 1's lemmas (both endpoints bad) are reused; the conclusion is kept as the conjunction of the martingale property and uniform integrability. That \(V \equiv v_0\) at \(\pi = 0\) is not encoded (the zero row comes from the vanishing alternative), and the strictness in the table is Theorem 1's. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D33ConstantExposureUI.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D33ConstantExposureUIProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 5 (machine-checked, conditional).** Ruf's criterion is restated in Lean for this model, with the canonical space, the localization, the energies, the stopped \(\sigma\)-field and the auxiliary law's density identities defined as in the source, and the stochastic exponential and the shifted martingale problem carried as fields, since Mathlib has no stochastic integral; it has a degenerate instance and no bridge to an Upstream structure yet. Proved: the energy-density identity and pathwise bounds of (a); in (b) the exhaustion, the identity of the lifetimes, the nonexit under every auxiliary law, the criterion's integral and the three schedules, with \((1 + t)^{-\gamma}\) for every real \(\gamma\); and (c) from Theorem 1's classification. Of the geometric law, only that its lifetime is infinite is used. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D33TimeExposure.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D33TimeExposureProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 6 (machine-checked, conditional).** The supermartingale property is the restatement used for Proposition 8, and each factor's scalar inputs (the finite-horizon test of Theorem 1, the uniform-integrability condition of Proposition 4, and \(Z_i \equiv 1\) at zero exposure) are supplied as premises; the independence of the full systems and the joint local-martingale property are explicit premises, and no trading through default is constructed. The tail bound used in (b) is proved; its converse, from the factors to the product, is not needed and not formalized. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D33IndependentAbsorbed.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D33IndependentAbsorbedProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 7 (machine-checked, unconditional).** The identified sets (7) are proved as equalities of subsets of \([0, \infty]\), together with the strict lower bound \(F_c(g) > J_{cR}\), the admissibility of the completions (9), the values \(F_c(g_1) = \infty\) and (10), and their positivity and continuity when \(\sigma\) is positive and continuous. No hypothesis is added. The reading of \(\theta\) as the bubble label is proved from the hypothesis structure for the JKP criterion, which has only a vacuous instance, so that reading is exactly as conditional as the prose. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D8TailCompletions.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D8TailCompletionsProof.lean}\).
- **Propositions 8, 9 and 11 (machine-checked, conditional).** Every part is proved from bounded stopping and the supermartingale property stated as hypotheses; the proof also derives these from hypothesis structures whose instances are degenerate, a constant process on a one-point space, so they give no evidence that the inputs hold for a non-constant price. The daily cost accounting (13) and its specializations are proved by algebra. The examples of Propositions 9 and 11 are conditional corollaries, the second also on the martingale criterion and (W); they take the processes in a version with every path continuous, while the cited results give continuity only almost surely. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D9HoldingPeriod.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D9HoldingPeriodProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 10 (machine-checked, conditional).** Proved from bounded stopping, finite trading, the stopping-time property of exit times and the stopping property of stochastic integrals stated as hypotheses, whose structures have degenerate instances; the bounds are proved path by path. The positivity of \(Q(\tau_a < T)\) is proved by comparison with the signed contract, without the supermartingale property. That the stopped process is the self-financing wealth closed into cash is its definition here; the stopped-integral identity that interprets it is not formalized. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D9InverseLiquidation.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D9InverseLiquidationProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 12 (machine-checked, conditional).** Proved from the supermartingale property and the finite-trading fact stated as hypotheses; the hypothesis structure for finite trading has a degenerate instance, a constant price, so it gives no evidence for a non-constant one. The pull-out of the holdings is proved directly, using the supermartingale property for integrability. The diffusion classification and the \(\sigma(x) = x^2\) comparison are conditional corollaries through hypothesis structures for the JKP criterion and the martingale criterion, whose instances are vacuous. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D9FiniteGrid.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D9FiniteGridProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 13 (machine-checked, conditional).** Proved from Émery's stability theorem, the stochastic-integral calculus, the stopping-time property of exit times and the supermartingale property, all stated as hypotheses; the hypothesis structures for the first two have degenerate instances, so they give no evidence that the inputs hold for a non-constant price. The stochastic integral and its bracket are relations with the cited properties, and their existence is proved from those properties. Uniform integrability in the expectation statement is Mathlib's notion, assumed. The identification with Theorem 1's wealth is with the explicit exponent of the stochastic exponential; its link to the hypothesis structure for the martingale criterion is not formalized. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D9Refinement.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D9RefinementProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 14 (machine-checked, conditional).** Conditional on the inputs of Propositions 12 and 13; the convergence-in-probability forms of Fatou's lemma and dominated convergence are proved from the Lean library (via a subsequence argument) rather than taken as cited inputs, and uniform integrability is Mathlib's notion. The diffusion regimes take the martingale criterion at this instance, in the form of Theorem 1's statement, as a premise, so they are conditional on it and on (W). The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D9TrueLimit.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D9TrueLimitProof.lean}\).
- **Numerical evidence after Proposition 14.** The values for \(\pi = 3/4\) come from a registered experiment (\(\texttt{experiments/012-d9-ui.md}\)); they are evidence about the cases computed, not a proof.
- **Propositions 15 and 73 (machine-checked, conditional).** Proved from the inputs of Propositions 10 and 12 stated as hypotheses, with degenerate instances; the companion process \(R\) is shown to be a local martingale by a lemma on linear combinations of continuous local martingales, localized at joint exit times. The stopped process is the liquidated wealth by definition; the closing-trade interpretation is not formalized. The elimination in (b) is a separate unconditional statement. The statements are in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D9PositiveLiquidation.lean}\) and \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/OuterSetLev.lean}\). The proofs are in the corresponding files under \(\texttt{lean/Novel/}\), with \(\texttt{Proof}\) appended to the name.
- **Proposition 16 (machine-checked; (b) and (c) conditional).** Parts (a) and (d) are proved path by path with no probability; (d) uses explicit tent paths. Parts (b) and (c) are proved from the inputs of Propositions 10 and 15; dense monitoring means every time in \([0, T]\) is eventually within any \(\varepsilon\) of the monitoring set, and (c) uses eventual detection of an open excursion and dominated convergence. That \(d_F\) can be negative is not formalized. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D9FloorMonitoring.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D9FloorMonitoringProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 17 (machine-checked, unconditional).** Both tails range independently over \((0, \infty] \times (0, \infty]\), with the known middle integrals added; the lower tail is (19) and the upper tail reuses the completions of Proposition 7.
- **Theorem 18, Propositions 19–20, Corollary 21 (machine-checked, conditional).** Proved from bounded stopping, the supermartingale property and, for the exactness of (22), the stopping-time property of exit times, all stated as hypotheses; the hypothesis structures for these inputs have degenerate instances, so they give no evidence that the inputs hold for a non-constant price. Tests are measurable functions of \(k\) paths on \([0, T]\), which is equivalent to the Borel tests of Section 3.4. Convexity in Proposition 19 is proved by mixing two whole models on their disjoint union, which avoids hitting-time theory there. Proposition 20 is proved from a configuration derived from a hypothesis structure for Sin's model whose instance is vacuous, because the pinned Mathlib cannot construct a Brownian motion; it is exactly as conditional as the prose. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D8NoGo.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D8NoGoProof.lean}\).
- **Theorem 22 (machine-checked, conditional).** Proved from stopped uniqueness in law stated as a hypothesis, whose structure has a degenerate instance (every law the point mass at a constant path), so it gives no evidence for genuine diffusion laws. The pricing laws themselves are a premise: a law for each admissible coefficient, a probability on paths that start at \(s_0\), stay nonnegative and are absorbed at zero; weak existence is not formalized. The completions \(\sigma_n\) are those of Proposition 7. All four families (positive continuous coefficients or not; \(P = Q\) or any equivalent law) are covered, and the bound, its sharpness and the asymptotic statement are proved. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D3LocalVolNoGo.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D3LocalVolNoGoProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 23 (machine-checked, conditional).** Proved from the occupation formula, the bracket formula, local-time positivity and Feller's test stated as hypotheses; the first two structures have degenerate instances and the positivity structure a vacuous one, so they give no evidence that the inputs hold for a genuine diffusion. The solution is supplied, with its stochastic-integral representation, and the occupation formula is used in the form of a continuous local martingale whose bracket has a density in time. The deterministic step, dividing out a density where it is positive, is a separate unconditional statement, \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/DensityCancellation.lean}\). The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D3VisitedRange.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D3VisitedRangeProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 24 (machine-checked, conditional).** Proved from Jacod's kernel estimation theorem stated as a hypothesis structure with a vacuous instance, with the representation of \(\log S\), the equivalence with the driftless equation and the local time as premises. The change of variables, the bounds, the finite sum and the clipping bound (b) are proved without probability, in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/CompactClipping.lean}\). The rate in (c) is stated in the bounded-in-probability form taken as a premise by Proposition 25(c). The reciprocal-local-time estimator mentioned after it is that of a registered experiment (\(\texttt{experiments/013-d3-visited-integral.md}\)). The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D3CompactEstimator.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D3CompactEstimatorProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 25 (machine-checked; (c) and the test in (d) conditional).** Parts (a) and (b), and the coefficient statement of (d), are proved unconditionally, with the tail by closed-form power integrals and the attaining coefficients from Proposition 7's completions; the size in (b) assumes measurable bands with the stated joint coverage. Part (c) takes the rate of the estimator of Proposition 24 and the convergence of its occupation events as premises; Proposition 24 proves them conditionally on Jacod's theorem. The test statement of (d) is conditional on the pricing-law family and stopped uniqueness, as in Theorem 22. The bubble reading of the label rests on the JKP criterion, whose instance is vacuous. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D3PowerTail.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D3PowerTailProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 26 (machine-checked; (d) conditional).** Parts (a)–(c) are proved as finite algebra with Proposition 25's constructions, using no hypothesis structure; (c) assumes measurable rectangles with the stated simultaneous coverage, and the two-name example is an explicit finite space. Part (d) takes the premises of Proposition 25(c) for every name under one joint law. The laws of the prices on a grid and their conditional densities are supplied as premises, as finite-grid iterates of the cited kernels; one-step normalization is cited and the finite-product normalization is proved. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D13DriftRect.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D13DriftRectProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 33 (machine-checked; false-discovery bounds conditional).** The false-discovery bounds, including the bound conditional on the earlier record, are conditional on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Proved: the conditional moments on the coverage event, in set-integral form; the corrected size, threshold, safe-score moment and rejection condition; the measurability of the declared score; the earlier-window rectangle, its roots, its containment of the likelihood set, its coverage bound and the null fallback event; and the three obstructions in (d), the counterexample with no cited input. The remarks on which dynamics supply the conditional-law premise, and the pricing link, are not formalized. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D13Declaration.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D13DeclarationProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 34 (machine-checked; false-discovery bound conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29, and the clock laws enter only as components of the null mixture, so their normalization is not used. Proved: the closed-form supremum and its unique maximizer, by an exact gap identity; the singular set and its measure zero; measurability; the scale invariance and the reduction to Proposition 30; validity under the mixture; and the orderings. The declaration and coverage of clocks are premises. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D17ClockDrift.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D17ClockDriftProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 35 (machine-checked).** The product rule for conditional e-values is the theorem proved from Mathlib, and the statistics and profile lemmas of Proposition 34 are reused; no new hypothesis structure is added. The null is stated as a conditional law given \(\mathcal F_{t-1}\) for every event and Borel set, and the step from it to the conditional mean of each oracle factor is proved. Proved: the clipped profile, its attainment, finiteness and measurability; the conditional stopped-mean and crossing bounds given \(\mathcal F_0\), and the same for smaller numerators and larger denominators; and the tracked local-lognormal law as a null. That the local-lognormal recursion keeps prices positive is a hypothesis; the remarks that \(E^{IV}\) need not be a supermartingale and on the scope of the null are not formalized. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D28PredictableIV.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D28PredictableIVProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 36 (machine-checked; false-discovery bound conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29, and the clock profiles of Proposition 34 are reused. Proved: the clock set and the measurability of retention; the coverage bound with no union over the catalogue; the two-stage conditioning that gives the raw and safe bounds; the comparisons, ceiling and boundary cases; and a nonzero model satisfying the premises, on the calendar clock. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D20ClockConfidence.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D20ClockConfidenceProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 37 (machine-checked; false-discovery bound conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the profile of Proposition 34 is reused for each day. Proved: the separation of the supremum, the singular days and their null set, measurability and scale invariance; validity under the history-dependent mixture law; the comparisons with grouped days and with full-record clocks; and the calendar identity with its equality case, through the unique optimizer of Proposition 34. That the optional constant-elasticity forecast has no atom at zero is not formalized here. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D23DailyProfile.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D23DailyProfileProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 38 (machine-checked; false-discovery bound conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the daily statistics of Proposition 34, the daily profile of Proposition 37 and the partition machinery of Proposition 39 are reused. Proved: the run profile, its attainment and its singular criterion; the partition maximum, its infinity criterion, attainment, measurability and the recursion; validity under the path mixture, also for a finite catalogue of full-record shapes; the nesting and its reversal for scores; and invariance under a common shape multiplier. The operation counts and the remarks on what does not hold in general are not formalized. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D23ScaleSwitches.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D23ScaleSwitchesProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 39 (machine-checked; false-discovery bound conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the profiles of Propositions 34 and 37 are reused on concatenated runs. Proved: the pooled run residual and its singular criterion; the partition maximum, its exchange with products, infinity and attainment, and the recursion; measurability on positive prices; validity under the mixture, also for a finite catalogue of full-record shapes; the nesting including the free-daily-drift denominator; the identity with the common profile; and the calendar cost and its additivity. The operation count and the diagnostic code are not formalized. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D23JointSwitches.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D23JointSwitchesProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 40 (machine-checked, conditional).** Proved from the e-BH theorem stated as a hypothesis, whose structure has a degenerate instance, and from the split-likelihood moment bound, which is proved; the rule is encoded without sorting and shown to report exactly the first \(k^*\) names. The bubble interpretation is commentary. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D12CatalogueFDR.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D12CatalogueFDRProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 41 (machine-checked).** The Gaussian maximum, including the unbounded singular case, is proved from the spectral theorem and the determinant identities; so are the rank bound, the null set of singular arrays when \(n \ge m + 1\), validity for any normalized forecast, the forecast's normalization and the separation of its training maximization, and the correlation identity (c). The forecast's constant-elasticity kernels enter as hypothesis structures with vacuous instances, as for Proposition 29; the Gaussian formula enters only as a component of the null mixture, so its normalization is not used. The operation counts and the comparisons with per-name testing are not formalized. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D16GaussianPool.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D16GaussianPoolProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 42 (machine-checked).** The Gaussian copula construction enters as a hypothesis on each step's normalization and marginals, and the constant-elasticity kernels as for Proposition 29. Proved: finiteness of the normal scores, the training-only measurable correlation rule with its bounds, normalization of the whole-path copula and mixture forecasts by a chain of normalized steps, the validity conclusions through Proposition 41, the mixture floor, the exact gain and the reduction at \(R = I\). The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D16CopulaForecast.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D16CopulaForecastProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 43 (machine-checked; (b) conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound in (b) is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; disintegration of the actual law, the split-likelihood bound, Markov's inequality, the bounded-function comparison and the Gaussian affinity and its limits are proved. The band rule's containment in the score rule is proved for any truth. The Gaussian part is a hypothesis on the law of log returns; the decimal values after the proposition are numerical evaluations of the formalized formulas, not formalized themselves. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D14Misspecification.lean}\), and the proofs in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D14MisspecificationProof.lean}\) and \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D14GaussianProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 44 (machine-checked; (b) conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity and lognormal kernels enter as for Proposition 29. The support-restricted split-likelihood step, Markov's inequality, the comparison on the full observation space and the absorption bound are proved. The observation space is the reals with one error symbol, as a standard Borel space; the result is proved for any finite catalogue of normalized densities and then instantiated at the objects of Proposition 29, and the screen may take values in \([0, 1]\). The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D14SupportRobustness.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D14SupportRobustnessProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 45 (machine-checked, conditional).** The exchangeable quantile lemma and the training-conditional guarantee enter at the reference laws used, as hypothesis structures bridged from interface structures whose only instances are degenerate (a shared fair bit and a point-mass score law), so they are not discharged for arbitrary reference laws. Proved: measurability of the rank threshold and the flag event; that a common measurable map preserves exchangeability and that independent laws are exchangeable; the flag probability as the mean of the realized risk; (a), (b) and (c), with the full calibration record on a standard Borel space and the kernel premise simultaneous over events on one full-measure set; \(r = n - k\); and (d) exactly, with its total variations. (b) is proved through the flag event, not through a bound on the total variation of the whole joint law, and the budgets may be any nonnegative numbers. The scope remarks after the proof are not encoded. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D34ScreenCalibration.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D34ScreenCalibrationProof.lean}\), with \(\texttt{D34ScreenCalibrationFlag}\), \(\texttt{D34ScreenCalibrationExample}\) and \(\texttt{D34ScreenCalibrationBridge}\).
- **Proposition 46 (machine-checked; (b) conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Proved: the free-drift score's reference moment at every training record, including impossible ones; the size bounds, the adjusted threshold, the capped moment and the rejection condition, for any score with such a moment; that the no-jump law is the Gaussian law of Proposition 30, with the Bernoulli construction realizing every \(p \in (0, 1)\); and the total-variation bound for dependent jumps. The mixture weight and jump law need not depend measurably on the training record. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D14JumpRobustness.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D14JumpRobustnessProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 47 (machine-checked; (b) conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Proved: the affinity (a), on log returns and on prices through the log-normal change of variables, with its measurability; the averaged size, threshold, clipped-moment and zero-budget conclusions for any latent kernel; the reference moment for both scores at every training record; and the envelope (c). The optional constant-elasticity reference with power one and zero drift for the rectangle score is not encoded. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D14VolPaths.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D14VolPathsProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 48 (machine-checked; (b) conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Proved: the factorization over segments, the finiteness criterion, the collapse at a one-step segment, that the exceptional set has measure zero, the normalization of the switch laws, the moments under the latent mixture, the ordering and the absorption cases. In the example showing that the score is not identically zero, positivity of the fitted likelihood on positive arrays is taken as a hypothesis. The interpretation of the switch null is not formalized. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D15Switch.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D15SwitchProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 49 (machine-checked, conditional).** Part (a) is proved from the quadratic variation of a Brownian integral plus a continuous finite-variation term, in its realized form (sums of squared increments along partitions with mesh tending to zero, converging in probability), stated as a hypothesis structure with a vacuous instance; the finite-variation-only case is proved by uniform continuity, not assumed. The stochastic integral is a relation quantified over, and existence of the representation is a premise. Parts (b) and (c), including sharpness, the size and power bounds for measurable arrays and the sampled-path constraint, are proved without hypothesis structures. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D30BVIdentification.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D30BVIdentificationProof.lean}\), with the bridge in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D30BVIdentificationBridge.lean}\).
- **Proposition 50 (machine-checked).** The split-likelihood bound enters through its proved domination inequality, and the grid law of the time-changed price is derived from Mathlib's pre-Brownian motion; no hypothesis structure is added. The certificate values are extended reals and the scores lie in \([0, \infty]\). Proved: the interpretation in both directions, by the mean value of the clock rate on each step; measurability, positivity, finiteness, the unique maximizer, the zero-return case, monotonicity and the closed form at \(V = 0\); the scalar suprema in all cases, the certificate bounds with the nonnegative gap decomposition, existence of a finite certificate and of a zero-gap one at \(V = 0\); and validity for the score, the certified score and every score below it, also for mixtures of null laws. The example forecast from constant-elasticity transitions, and the remark that jumps of the clock rate do not make the price jump, are not formalized. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D30VariationProfile.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D30VariationProfileProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 51 (machine-checked, conditional).** The reciprocal-Bessel kernel's normalization is a hypothesis structure bridged from the cited Bessel kernel, the convergence theorem enters through the lab's mean-one \(L^1\) lemma, and the geometric Brownian law in coordinates is proved by induction with one-dimensional change of variables and Tonelli; the price laws are the normalized chains of Proposition 28 with \(P = Q\). The consequences for larger classes and for the time-and-level models are remarks, not encoded. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D30SmallScale.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D30SmallScaleProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 52 (machine-checked; false-discovery bound conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29. Proved: the domination of every jump law by the envelope, for the series formula as written, without using its normalization or its interpretation as Merton's law; the envelope's finiteness, measurability, monotonicity and limits; the truncation bound; that the infinite-denominator set has measure zero; the moments under the latent mixture; and the orderings. In the example of a positive score, positivity of the fitted likelihood is taken as a hypothesis. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D15JumpEnvelope.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D15JumpEnvelopeProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 53 (machine-checked; false-discovery bound conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the constant-elasticity kernels enter as for Proposition 29, and the conclusions of Proposition 52(b) are proved for any valid envelope. Proved: the domination and the comparison with Proposition 52's envelope; measurability, through a supremum over rational parameters; the enclosing rectangle and attainment of the maximum; the box bounds, their monotonicity under subdivision and their convergence; and validity for every measurable cover rule. No outward-rounding certificate is given for floating-point evaluation. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D15SharedJump.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D15SharedJumpProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 54 (machine-checked).** From Mathlib's measure maps, restrictions, Dirac masses and the Gaussian density and integral; the split-likelihood step is proved with the supplied densities, and no hypothesis structure is used. Means are lower integrals of the nonnegative scores, and the profiled denominator in (b) is written directly as the supremum over the drift. The positivity of the prices \(1, e^R\) and \(1, e^Y\) is not encoded. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D31FilterScope.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D31FilterScopeProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 55 (machine-checked, conditional).** The price kernels are hypothesis structures with vacuous instances and the grid laws are supplied as premises, as for Proposition 28; the false-discovery bound is conditional on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance. Proved outright: (a), for every law on the reals and, for vectors, every absolutely continuous reference; the cell probability and the divergence of the plug-in mean in (b), and the bounds at \(h = 1/2\), \(\kappa = 1/100\); the conditional-density facts in (c), for an arbitrary selector; and (d), for every \(h > 0\), by a bound on the log density ratio. The decimal \(0.175\) and the remark that the weights use the whole training prefix are not formalized. Measurability of the observed prices is a disclosed premise. The deterministic parts are stated in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/RoundedCells.lean}\) and proved in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/RoundedCellsProof.lean}\). The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D14RoundedObservations.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D14RoundedObservationsProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 56 (machine-checked; (c) conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance. Proved: the restarted laws as images of the whole block, their positivity and normalization; the first-return shift and its affinity; the budget at almost every training record, for any conditional law of the exact price in its cell; the size, threshold and clipped-moment consequences; and the tolerance and limits in (d). The remarks on which dynamics supply the premise are not formalized. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D14RoundedStart.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D14RoundedStartProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 57 (machine-checked; false-discovery bound conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance. Proved: the normal distribution function's properties, the clipped-midpoint supremum and its range; the domination along the path, by induction, for any law of the exact price in its cell; the moments and size; the row sums and total mass in (c); and the counterexample. The operation count is not a mathematical statement, and no floating-point evaluation is certified. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D14Cell.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D14CellProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 58 (machine-checked; false-discovery bound conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance. The operation and storage counts are not mathematical statements, and no floating-point evaluation is certified. The statement is in \(\texttt{lean/Standalone/D14CellSubdivision.lean}\), and the proof in \(\texttt{lean/Novel/D14CellSubdivisionProof.lean}\).
- **Proposition 59 (machine-checked; false-discovery bound conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; the Gaussian cell lemmas of Proposition 57 are reused. - **Proposition 60 (machine-checked; false-discovery bound conditional).** Only the false-discovery bound is conditional, on the e-BH theorem with a degenerate instance; no constant-elasticity kernel is used.
